Monotonicity of the Relative Entropy and the Two-sided Bogoliubov Inequality in von Neumann Algebras


Abstract
This text studies, on the one hand, certain monotonicity properties of the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy and, on the other hand, unbounded perturbation theory of KMS-states which facilitates a proof of the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality in general von Neumann algebras. After introducing the necessary background from the theory of operator algebras and Tomita-Takesaki modular theory, the relative entropy functional is defined and its basic properties are studied. In particular, a full and detailed proof of Uhlmann’s important monotonicity theorem for the relative entropy is provided. This theorem will then be used to derive a number of monotonicity inequalities for the relative entropy of normal functionals induced by vectors of the form V ​ Ω , V ​ Φ ∈ ℋ V\varOmega,V\varPhi\in\mathcal{H} , where V ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) V\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}) is a suitable transformation. After that, an introduction to perturbation theory in von Neumann algebras is given, with an emphasis on unbounded perturbations of KMS-states following the framework of Dereziński-Jakšić-Pillet. This mathematical apparatus will then be used to extend the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality for the relative free energy, which was very recently proved for quantum-mechanical systems, to arbitrary von Neumann algebras.

Chapter I
Introduction

In recent years, a substantial interest in information-theoretic aspects of quantum field theory has developed. An indispensible tool in corresponding studies is the
relative entropy functional
which was already defined in 1977 in its most general form for arbitrary von Neumann algebras. Lately, it has experienced a renaissance in mathematical physics, especially in algebraic quantum field theory, with many new applications being discovered and investigated. To motivate the specific problems studied in the present text, the use of the relative entropy in mathematical physics is reviewed briefly in
Section

I.1
. Following this,
Section

I.2
provides a concise outline of the text.

I.1
The Relative Entropy in Mathematical Physics

I.1.1
The Kullback-Leibler divergence.

The notion of relative entropy was first introduced in mathematical statistics by
S. Kullback
and
R. A. Leibler
in 1951
[
99
]
. In modern terminology, it can be defined as follows: let
(\varOmega,\varSigma)
be a measurable space and
P,Q
be two probability measures on
\varSigma
such that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
. In this case, the Radon-Nikodým derivative
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}P}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{Q}}
exists
[
40
, Thm. 4.2.2]
, and the
classical relative entropy
or
Kullback-Leibler divergence
between
P
and
Q
is defined to be
[
91
, p. 184]

D(P,Q)\coloneqq\int_{\varOmega}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}P}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{Q}}}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}P=\int_{\varOmega}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}P}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{Q}}\,\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}P}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{Q}}}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Q\ .

(I.1)

If
P
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
, one can extend the above definition by setting
D(P,Q)\coloneqq+\infty
. (
I.1
) has a number of interesting properties, for example
[
91
, Prop. 4.1, 4.4 & 4.8]
:

(a)

D(P,Q)\geq 0
and
D(P,Q)=0
if and only if
P=Q
;

(b)

D\bigl(\alpha(P),\alpha(Q)\bigr)\leq D(P,Q)
for every stochastic mapping
\alpha
;

(c)

(P,Q)\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow D(P,Q)
is jointly convex.

Based on
(a)
, the relative entropy
D(P,Q)
can be interpreted as a measure for the
distinguishability
of the probability distributions
P
and
Q

[
118
, pp. 8 & 84]
. In fact, it can be shown that if one draws
N
samples according to
Q
, then the probability that they will look as if they were drawn from
P
is proportional to
\exp(-ND(P,Q))
for large
N

[
165
, p. 200]
. In light of this interpretation,
(b)
implies that the probability distributions
P
and
Q
can only become less distinguishable under stochastic evolution
[
165
, p. 198]
, and property
(c)
asserts that mixing probability distributions decreases distinguishability
[
165
, p. 203]
.

The notion of distinguishability is of fundamental importance for information theory because the degree to which one can distinguish between different physical states of a system determines the amount of information that can be encoded and manipulated
[
165
, p. 197]
. This is the main reason why generalizations of the relative entropy (
I.1
) to non-commutative probability spaces have become so important in quantum information theory and quantum field theory, as will be outlined in the following.

I.1.2
The Umegaki relative entropy.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. The Kullback-Leibler divergence (
I.1
) was extended to the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
by
H. Umegaki
in 1962
[
164
]
: if
\omega
and
\varphi
are two normal states on
\mathfrak{M}
represented by density matrices
\rho_{\omega},\rho_{\varphi}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\ker(\rho_{\omega})^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(\rho_{\varphi})^{\perp}
, then the
quantum
or
Umegaki relative entropy
of
\omega
with respect to
\varphi
is defined to be
[
118
, p. 16]

S(\rho_{\omega},\rho_{\varphi})\coloneqq\tr\bigl(\rho_{\omega}(\log\rho_{\omega}-\log\rho_{\varphi})\bigr)\ .

(I.2)

One can show that if
\rho_{\omega}
and
\rho_{\varphi}
commute with each other, then (
I.2
) reduces to (
I.1
)
[
118
, p. 16]
. Furthermore, analoga of the properties
(a)
–
(c)
from
I.1.1
can be proved for the Umegaki entropy as well
[
118
, Prop. 1.1 & Thm 1.4, 1.5]
. Therefore, one can interpret the quantity
S(\omega,\varphi)
as a measure for the distinguishability of
\omega
and
\varphi

[
165
, p. 207]
.

The relative entropy (
I.2
) was introduced to mathematical physics by
G. Lindblad
in 1973 – 1975
[
101
,
102
,
103
]
. He used this quantity to analyze the quantum-mechanical measurement process, and he showed that
S
is non-increasing under completely positive maps; see
[
114
,
129
]
for recent discussions and refinements of this monotonicity property. Since then, the quantum relative entropy has become an indispensible tool in two areas of mathematical physics:
statistical mechanics

[
169
]
and
quantum information theory

[
146
,
165
]
. In the former, Lindblad’s monotonicity result can be seen as a
“generalized
H
-theorem”
for open quantum systems
[
72
, p. 272]
, and the analogue of
I.1.1

(a)
can be used to prove that the canonical Gibbs state is the unique minimizer of the free energy
[
118
, Prop. 1.10]
. Recently, there have also been studies which suggest reformulations of the second and third law of thermodynamics using the relative entropy
[
57
,
64
,
139
]
. In quantum information theory, the Umegaki relative entropy plays a role analogous to that of the Kullback-Leibler divergence in classical statistics
[
165
, p. 208]
. Among other reasons, this is due to the fact that the relative entropy can be used to quantify the amount of
entanglement
present in a single quantum state
[
146
, p. 2]
,
[
165
, p. 219]
.

Lately, also an interest in entanglement measures for relativistic quantum field theory has developed, see the overviews
[
90
,
173
]
and other references given below in
I.1.3
. Since the Umegaki relative entropy is not available for general quantum field theories
[
108
, p. 114]
, a more general definition of a relative entropy functional is needed.

I.1.3
The Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy.

A generalization of the Umegaki relative entropy to arbitrary von Neumann algebras was discovered by
H. Araki
in 1977, who had previously already made substantial contributions to the mathematical theory of von Neumann algebras (
e.g.
,
[
8
,
15
]
) and algebraic quantum field theory (
e.g.
,
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
). He developed an extension of
Tomita-Takesaki modular theory
and used it to define a notion of relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\omega,\varphi)
between normal functionals
\omega,\varphi
on an arbitrary von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}

[
10
,
11
]
. To some extend, he was motivated by questions of statistical mechanics regarding certain properties of
KMS-states
, where he had already applied methods from modular theory and perturbation theory in operator algebras
[
7
,
9
]
.

Simultaneously and independently,
A. Uhlmann
also introduced a notion of relative entropy between positive linear functionals on
\ast
-algebras using an abstract quadratic interpolation theory
[
163
]
. Later, it was shown by
D. Petz
that
Uhlmann
’s and
Araki
’s definition actually coincide on von Neumann algebras
[
123
, Lem. 2]
,
[
118
, pp. 79 f.]
, whence this functional, which is the protagonist of this text, is termed the
Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy
; it will be formally introduced in
Definitions

IV.1.1
and
IV.2.1
. In
[
163
, Prop. 18]
,
Uhlmann
also proved a very general
monotonicity theorem
for the relative entropy which generalizes Lindblad’s result mentioned in
I.1.2
. Namely, he showed that for a unital normal Schwarz mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, the following inequality holds true
[
118
, Cor. 5.12 (iii)]

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(\omega\circ\alpha,\varphi\circ\alpha)\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\omega,\varphi)\ .

(I.3)

This property of the relative entropy is highly important for many reasons,
e.g.
, the fact that analoga of
(a)
and
(c)
from
I.1.1
can be proved with the help of (
I.3
);
Section

IV.3
of this text is devoted to this topic. Further properties of the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy were studied in the 80s and 90s by
M. J. Donald

[
52
,
54
]
,
H. Kosaki

[
96
]
, and
D. Petz

[
123
,
124
,
125
,
126
,
127
,
128
]
, to name just a selected few.

After the introduction of the relative entropy on general von Neumann algebras, it was primarily applied in statistical mechanics to study KMS-states
[
14
,
30
]
. The interest of quantum field theorists in the relative entropy started only a couple of years ago. To the best knowledge of the author, this can be traced back to a paper of
H. Casini
from 2008
[
34
]
who used the Umegaki relative entropy (
I.2
) to give a proof of the so-called
Bekenstein bound
which provides an upper limit for the entropy of a system in terms of its energy and size. Ten years later,
R. Longo
and
F. Xu

[
108
]
pointed out that
Casini
’s argument breaks down for general quantum field theories, and they gave a more rigorous proof of the Bekenstein bound using the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy. Since then, this quantity has been applied to various problems in (algebraic) quantum field theory (in curved spacetimes). To begin with,
Casini

et al.

[
35
]
and
Longo

[
106
]
independently computed, in the setting of a free scalar field, the relative entropy between the vacuum state and a coherent excitation thereof; generalizations of these computations were considered afterwards by various authors
[
86
,
27
,
28
,
68
]
. Furthermore, a connection between the relative entropy and
quantum energy inequalities
was discovered by
F. Ceyhan
and
T. Faulkner

[
36
]
(see also
[
38
,
113
,
168
]
), and more general investigations of
relativistic quantum information theory
were conducted by
F. Hiai
,
S. Hollands
,
R. Longo
, and others
[
39
,
60
,
61
,
67
,
81
,
82
,
84
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
,
107
]
.

I.2
Outline of the Text

As illustrated above, the study of the relative entropy in von Neumann algebras is a highly active field of research. It is the goal of this text to contribute to this field by providing a detailed introduction to its mathematical foundations, and by discussing certain properties of the relative entropy related to monotonicity and perturbation theory of KMS-states. To this end,
Chapter

II
gives an overview of the theory of
C^{\ast}
-algebras and von Neumann algebras, and
Chapter

III
contains a fairly detailed discussion of Tomita-Takesaki modular theory.

The Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy is introduced in
Chapter

IV
, first for von Neumann algebras in standard form, and second for arbitrary representations. The main part of this chapter is devoted to the monotonicity theorem of
A. Uhlmann
. Fully detailed proofs of this result and of all the auxiliary lemmata required to establish it are presented; in this form, this is not readily available in the literature.
Section

IV.4
contains some original results regarding the following question: given von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1},\mathfrak{M}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and vectors
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
, for which Hilbert-space transformations
V\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
can one prove monotonicity inequalities of the form
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,V)
? Results along these lines will be obtained by applying Uhlmann’s general theorem to specific situations (see
Propositions

IV.4.2
,
IV.4.6
,
IV.4.7
,
IV.4.8
and
IV.4.11
).

In
Chapter

V
, perturbation theory in operator algebras is discussed. Primarily, the chapter deals with unbounded perturbations of KMS-states by reviewing relatively recent results of
J. Dereziński
,
V. Jakšić
, and
C.-A. Pillet

[
50
]
, and extending them slightly in
Section

V.3.d
(see
Lemma

V.3.10

(c)
,
Theorem

V.3.11

(g)
, and
Proposition

V.3.13
). The final
Section

V.4
is concerned with the so-called
two-sided Bogoliubov inequality
. This inequality was first discussed in the setting of classical statistical mechanics as a tool to estimate finite-size effects in molecular simulations
[
48
]
. It was extended to quantum statistical mechanics by the author of this text in his bachelor’s thesis; the findings were published in
[
136
]
(they will be reviewed very briefly in
Section

V.4.a
), and concrete applications of this inequality to different problems were subsequently presented in
[
137
,
49
]
. In this work, using the extended framework of unbounded perturbation theory of KMS-states, the results of
[
136
]
are generalized to the setting of arbitrary von Neumann algebras. First, a version of the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality is obtained (
Proposition

V.4.7
) which reduces to the known inequality of
[
136
, Thm. 4.1]
on the von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Second, the variational bounds of
[
136
, Sect. 4.1]
are extended to certain classes of unbounded perturbations in
Section

V.4.c
(see
Propositions

V.4.12
and
V.4.15
as well as
Corollary

V.4.16
).

The main part of text will be concluded with
Chapter

VI
which summarizes the results and provides a brief outlook. Finally,
Appendices

A
,
B
and
C
contain some selected definitions and results from the fields of topological vector spaces, (un)bounded linear operators, and quadratic forms on Hilbert spaces which are needed throughout the text.

Chapter II
Aspects of the Theory of Operator Algebras

To commence the study of the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy, this chapter presents some basic definitions and results from the mathematically rich theory of operator algebras. First,
Section

II.1
introduces
C^{\ast}
-algebras
and their
positive elements
. Following that,
Section

II.2
studies properties of
positive mappings
between
C^{\ast}
-algebras. In
Section

II.3
, the important subclass of
von Neumann algebras
and some of their basic properties are discussed. Finally,
Section

II.4
presents the important
GNS-construction
. Due to the vastness of the theory, the limited amount of space available, and the wide range of literature on the topic, only a few selected proofs will be given.

References
.

The main source for this chapter is
[
29
, Ch. 2]
. In addition, the following references were consulted:
[
17
]
,
[
23
, Ch. 26 & 30]
,
[
25
, Ch. I]
,
[
45
, Ch. VII & VIII]
,
[
141
, Ch. 1]
,
[
145
, Ch. 2]
,
[
156
, Ch. 1, 2 & 3]
,
[
159
, Ch. I & II]
, and
[
171
, Ch. IX]
.

II.1
C^{\ast}
-Algebras

II.1.a  Definitions and Examples

The properties characterizing
C^{\ast}
-algebras are, on the one hand, an abstraction of properties displayed by the space
(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}})
of bounded linear operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. On the other hand, the class of
C^{\ast}
-algebras forms a subclass of the more general Banach algebras, characterized by additional requirements on the algebra norm. Here, the definition of a
C^{\ast}
-algebra shall be presented directly without a deductive tour through the theory of Banach algebras; for the latter, see
[
25
,
93
,
159
]
. First, however, some purely algebraic concepts are recalled.

II.1.1
\ast
-algebras.

(
[
25
, Ch. I]
,
[
145
, Sect. 2.1]
)
A
\ast
-algebra
is a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra together with an
algebra involution

\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{\ast}
, that is, a mapping satisfying for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
and
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{K}
:

(A^{\ast})^{\ast}=A{\ ,\quad}(\lambda\cdot A+\mu\cdot B)^{\ast}=\overline{\lambda}\cdot A^{\ast}+\overline{\mu}\cdot B^{\ast}\ ,\quad\text{and}\quad(AB)^{\ast}=B^{\ast}A^{\ast}\ .

A subset
\mathfrak{J}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
is called
self-adjoint
iff
A^{\ast}\in\mathfrak{J}
for every
A\in\mathfrak{J}
, and a self-adjoint subalgebra (ideal) of
\mathfrak{A}
is called a
\ast
-subalgebra
(
\ast
-ideal
). An element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
is called
self-adjoint
iff
A^{\ast}=A
, and the set of all self-adjoint elements is denoted by
\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. This set is a real linear subspace of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}\oplus_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{i}\,\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}

[
25
, Lem. I.12.3]
. A linear mapping
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
between two
\ast
-algebras is called a
\ast
-homomorphism
iff
f(AB)=f(A)f(B)
and
f(A^{\ast})=f(A)^{\ast}
for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
. Finally, an element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
of a unital
\ast
-algebra
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbbm{1})
is called
invertible
iff there exists an element
A^{-1}\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
AA^{-1}=A^{-1}A=\mathbbm{1}
. The set of all invertible elements of
\mathfrak{A}
is denoted by
\mathrm{GL}(\mathfrak{A})
.

Definition II.1.2
(
C^{\ast}
-algebra).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\ast
-algebra. If there exists a norm
\|\cdot\|:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty)
such that (1)
\mathfrak{A}
is
\|\cdot\|
-complete, (2)
\|\cdot\|
is
sub-multiplicative
, that is,

\forall A,B\in\mathfrak{A}\ :\ \|AB\|\leq\|A\|\,\|B\|\ ,

(II.1)

and (3)
\|\cdot\|
satisfies the the so-called
C^{\ast}
-property

\forall A\in\mathfrak{A}\ :\ \|A^{\ast}A\|=\|A\|^{2}\ ,

(II.2)

then
(\mathfrak{A},\|\cdot\|)
is called an
abstract

C^{\ast}
-algebra
,
1
1

1

The terminology
“abstract”
will be apparent after
concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebras
are introduced in
Examples

II.1.3

(h)
. The name
“
C^{\ast}
-algebra”
was coined by
I. E. Segal
in 1947
[
147
]
.

and
\|\cdot\|
is called a
C^{\ast}
-algebra norm
. If there is no risk of confusion, such an algebra will be called
C^{\ast}
-algebra and denoted by
\mathfrak{A}
for short. The algebra norm
\|\cdot\|
defines a metric topology on
\mathfrak{A}
which is referred to as the
uniform
or
norm topology

\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{norm}}
. Finally, a
C^{\ast}
-subalgebra
of
\mathfrak{A}
is defined to be a uniformly closed
\ast
-subalgebra
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
.

Examples II.1.3
.

(a)

The complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
form a commutative
C^{\ast}
-algebra with involution given by complex conjugation
z=a+\mathrm{i}b\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\overline{z}\coloneqq a-\mathrm{i}b
and norm given by the absolute value.

(b)

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space and
C^{0}(X)\coloneqq C^{0}(X;\mathbb{C})
be the space of continuous functions
f:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. With multiplication defined pointwise,
(fg)(x)\coloneqq f(x)g(x)
for
f,g\in C^{0}(X)
and
x\in X
, involution given by
f^{\ast}(x)\coloneqq\overline{f(x)}
, and norm defined to be

\|f\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in X}|f(x)|\ ,

this space becomes a commutative
C^{\ast}
-algebra
[
29
, Exa. 2.1.4]
. It is unital with unit given by the constant function
\mathbf{1}_{X}:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{K}
,
x\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow 1
.

(c)

Similarly to the previous example, for a measure space
(\varOmega,\varSigma,\mu)
it holds that the essentially bounded functions
L^{\infty}(\varOmega,\mu)
with pointwise multiplication, the involution from
(b)
, and the essential supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
form a unital commutative
C^{\ast}
-algebra.

(d)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The space
\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{K})
consisting of
(n\times n)
-matrices with entries in
\mathbb{K}
is a unital
\mathbb{K}
-algebra with multiplication given by the matrix product. If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, one can define an involution in terms of the transposition
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{\mathrm{T}}
, and if
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
, an involution is given by
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow(\overline{A})^{\mathrm{T}}
. There exist various sub-multiplicative, complete norms on
\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{K})
,
e.g.
, the Frobenius norm
[
172
, p. 211]
, but only the operator norm satisfies the
C^{\ast}
-property
[
29
, Cor. 2.2.6]
.

(e)

Let
(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
be a Hilbert space. The space
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
of bounded linear operators
T:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a unital
\ast
-algebra with multiplication
(T,S)\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow T\circ S\equiv TS
and involution given by the operation
T\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow T^{\ast}
of taking adjoints. Furthermore, the
operator norm

[
171
, p. 50]

\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\coloneqq\|T\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})}\coloneqq\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|T\xi\|}{\|\xi\|}=\sup_{\|\xi\|\leq 1}\|T\xi\|=\sup_{\|\xi\|=1}\|T\xi\|

is sub-multiplicative because for
T,S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, the inequality

\|T(S\xi)\|\leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|S\xi\|\leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|S\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\xi\|\ ,

which follows from boundedness of
T
and
S
, implies
\|TS\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|S\|_{\mathrm{op}}

[
171
, Lem. II.1.6]
. It holds that
(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}})
is complete
[
171
, Thm. II.1.4]
, and the involution satisfies
\|T^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}

[
171
, Thm. V.5.2]
. Finally, for all
T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies

\displaystyle\|T\xi\|^{2}=\langle{T\xi,T\xi}\rangle=\langle{\xi,T^{\ast}T\xi}\rangle\leq\|\xi\|\,\|T^{\ast}T\xi\|\ .

With this inequality and (
II.1
), it follows that

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\xi\|\leq 1}\|T\xi\|^{2}\leq\sup_{\|\xi\|\leq 1}\|\xi\|\,\|T^{\ast}T\xi\|\leq\|T^{\ast}T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|T^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\ .

This chain of inequalities shows that there is actually equality,
i.e.
,
\|T^{\ast}T\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}
for all
T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, hence
(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}})
is a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra
[
171
, Thm. V.5.2 (f)]
.

(f)

Let
\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be the space of compact operators on
\mathcal{H}
. It holds that
\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})
is a
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
-closed subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})

[
171
, Thm. II.3.2]
which is not unital if
\dim(\mathcal{H})=+\infty

[
171
, p. 506]
. Furthermore,
T\in\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})
if and only if
T^{\ast}\in\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})

[
171
, Thm. III.4.4]
, hence
\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})
is a normed
\ast
-ideal of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, and thus, in particular, a
C^{\ast}
-algebra as well.

(g)

The space
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
of trace-class operators and the space
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
of Hilbert-Schmidt operators (
Definition

B.1.2
) are
\ast
-ideals in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, and with respect to the trace norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{tr}}
, respectively, the Hilbert-Schmidt norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
, they are complete (
Proposition

B.1.4
). However, they are not
C^{\ast}
-algebras because
\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\|A^{\ast}A\|_{\mathrm{tr}}
for all
A\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\subseteq\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.

(h)

More generally, it holds that any uniformly closed
\ast
-subalgebra
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a
C^{\ast}
-algebra, referred to as a
concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra
. To see this, let
A\in\mathfrak{A}
be arbitrary. Then there exists a sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
such that
\|A_{n}-A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
. It follows from continuity of the multiplication and involution, and the
C^{\ast}
-property (
II.2
) that

\|A^{\ast}A\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|A_{n}^{\ast}A_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|A_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}=\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\ .

II.1.b  Positive Elements

In the following, the class of positive elements in a
C^{\ast}
-algebra will be introduced. It shall be assumed that all appearing algebras are considered over the field
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
of complex numbers. Recall that the
spectrum
of an element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
of a unital
\mathbb{C}
-algebra is given by

\sigma(A)\coloneqq\{{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,A-\lambda\mathbbm{1}\notin\mathrm{GL}(\mathfrak{A})}\}\ .

Definition II.1.4
(Positive element).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a unital
\ast
-algebra. An element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
is called
positive
, denoted by
A\geq 0
, iff
A
is self-adjoint and satisfies
\sigma(A)\subseteq[0,+\infty)
. The set of all positive elements will be denoted by the symbol
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
.

The following theorem characterizes the positive elements in a
C^{\ast}
-algebra. The proof can be found in
[
29
, Thm. 2.2.10 & 2.2.12]
or
[
45
, Thm. VIII.3.6]
.

Theorem II.1.5
(Characterization of positive elements).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra. For an element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

A\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
;

(b)

there exists a unique
B\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
such that
A=B^{2}
;

(c)

there exists
B\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
A=B^{\ast}B
.

II.1.6
Self-dual convex cones.

Recall the following algebraic definitions
[
143
, p. 38]
: a
cone
in an
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V
is a subset
C\subseteq V
which is closed under multiplication by non-negative scalars,
i.e.
,
x\in C,\,\lambda\geq 0\,\Rightarrow\,\lambda x\in C
. The cone is called
convex
iff
\lambda,\mu\geq 0,\,x,y\in C\,\Rightarrow\,\lambda x+\mu y\in C
. For later use, introduce also the following notion
[
143
, p. 218]
: a cone
C\subseteq\mathcal{H}
in a Hilbert space
(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
is called
self-dual
iff
C=\{{y\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,\text{$\langle{y,x}\rangle\geq 0$ for all $x\in C$}}\}
.

Proposition II.1.7
(
[
29
, Prop. 2.2.11]
).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra. The set
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
is a uniformly closed convex cone satisfying
\mathfrak{A}^{+}\cap(-\mathfrak{A}^{+})=\{0\}
. Moreover, if
A\in\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, then the elements
A_{\pm}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(|A|\pm A)
are positive, and they satisfy
A_{+}A_{-}=0
and
A=A_{+}-A_{-}
.

According to this result, the sum of two positive elements is again positive. Regarding the product of two such elements, the following property is found which will be needed later in
Chapter

IV
.

Lemma II.1.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
. Then
AB\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
if and only if
AB=BA
.

Proof
.

1.
Assume first that
A
and
B
commute. Using
Theorem

II.1.5
, one can define the
square root
of a positive element
C\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
: it is the unique element
\sqrt{C}\coloneqq C^{1/2}\coloneqq D\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
such that
D^{2}=C

[
29
, p. 34]
. With this, the following decomposition holds true:

AB=\sqrt{A}\sqrt{A}\sqrt{B}\sqrt{B}=\sqrt{B}\sqrt{A}\sqrt{A}\sqrt{B}=\bigl(\sqrt{B}\bigr)^{\ast}\bigl(\sqrt{A}\bigr)^{\ast}\sqrt{A}\sqrt{B}=\bigl(\sqrt{A}\sqrt{B}\bigr)^{\ast}\sqrt{A}\sqrt{B}\ .

Note that
\sqrt{A}
and
\sqrt{B}
commute according to the assumption and the continuous functional calculus in
C^{\ast}
-algebras
[
156
, p. 22]
. From
Theorem

II.1.5
, it now follows that
AB
is a positive element.

2.
Conversely, if
AB\geq 0
, then
\sigma(AB)\subseteq[0,+\infty)
and
(AB)^{\ast}=AB
by definition. But
(AB)^{\ast}=B^{\ast}A^{\ast}=BA
since
A
and
B
are self-adjoint, hence
AB=BA
.

II.1.9
Order relation for self-adjoint elements.

(
[
29
, p. 36]
)
Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra. The closed convex cone
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
induces an order structure on the real linear subspace
\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
of self-adjoint elements: for
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
define

A\leq B\quad\text{(or $B\geq A$)}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad B-A\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}\ .

This relation defines a partial order on
\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
: (1) it is clear that
A\leq A
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
because
0\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
; (2) if
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
such that
A\leq B
and
B\leq A
, it follows from
Proposition

II.1.7
that
B-A\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}\cap(-\mathfrak{A}^{+})=\{0\}
, hence
B=A
; (3) finally, if there is a third element
C\in\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
such that
A\leq B
and
B\leq C
, then
C-A=C-B+B-A
is the sum of two positive elements, hence
C-A\geq 0
as
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
is a convex cone.

Lemma II.1.10
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbbm{1})
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
A\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}
be a positive element. Then
A
is invertible if and only if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
A\geq\varepsilon\,\mathbbm{1}
.

Proof
.

1.
Assume that
A
is invertible. From positivity of
A
, it follows that
\sigma(A)\subseteq[0,+\infty)
, and from invertibility of
A
, one can conclude that
0\notin\sigma(A)
. Therefore, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\sigma(A)\subseteq[\varepsilon,+\infty)
. Since
\sigma(A-\varepsilon\,\mathbbm{1})=\{{\lambda-\varepsilon:\lambda\in\sigma(A)}\}

[
29
, Prop. 2.2.3]
and
\lambda\geq\varepsilon
for all
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
, it follows that
A-\varepsilon\,\mathbbm{1}\geq 0
.

2.
Conversely, assume that
A\geq\varepsilon\,\mathbbm{1}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Employing an analogous argument as before, one obtains
\lambda\geq\varepsilon
for all
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
. Hence,
\sigma(A)\subseteq[\varepsilon,+\infty)
, that is,
0\notin\sigma(A)
, so
A
must be invertible.

Example II.1.11
(Positive elements in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
).

Let
(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
be a Hilbert space and
\mathfrak{A}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be the
C^{\ast}
-algebra of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
from
Examples

II.1.3

(e)
. Then for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, the following characterization holds true
[
45
, Thm. VIII.3.8]
:

A\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\forall\xi\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,\langle{\xi,A\xi}\rangle\geq 0\ .

II.2
Positive Linear Maps

Having introduced the closed convex cone
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
of positive elements, now, a class of linear mappings between
C^{\ast}
-algebras shall be defined which preserve these cones.

II.2.a  Basic Properties

Definition II.2.1
(Positive mapping).

A linear mapping
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
between two unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
is called
positive
iff for every
A\in\mathfrak{A}
,
A\geq 0
implies
f(A)\geq 0
, that is, iff
f
maps the cone
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
into the cone
\mathfrak{B}^{+}
.

Remark II.2.2
.

It follows immediately from this definition that positive linear mappings
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
are
order-preserving
with respect to the order relation introduced in
II.1.9
: if
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}^{\mathrm{sa}}
satisfy
A\leq B
, then
B-A\geq 0
and hence
f(B)-f(A)\geq 0
in
\mathfrak{B}
,
i.e.
,
A\leq B\Rightarrow f(A)\leq f(B)
.

Next, it will be shown that positive mappings automatically satisfy an important algebraic property related to the algebra involution
[
154
, p. 2]
.

Proposition II.2.3
.

Let
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a positive linear mapping between unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
. Then
f
is
\ast
-preserving, i.e.,
f(A^{\ast})=f(A)^{\ast}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof
.

Any element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
can be written as
A=A_{1}-A_{2}+\mathrm{i}(A_{3}-A_{4})
for
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{+}
(
cf.

II.1.1
and
Proposition

II.1.7
). It holds that
A^{\ast}=A_{1}-A_{2}-\mathrm{i}(A_{3}-A_{4})
since positive elements are, by definition, self-adjoint. From positivity of
f
, it follows that
f(A_{i})\in\mathfrak{B}^{+}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{sa}}
for all
i\in\{1,\dotsc,4\}
, hence linearity of
f
implies
f(A^{\ast})=f(A)^{\ast}
.

Positive mappings are also well-behaved in regards to the topological structure of the algebras: they are automatically continuous. A simple proof can be found in
[
120
, Prop. 2.1]
or
[
154
, p. 2]
.

Proposition II.2.4
.

Let
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a positive linear mapping between unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras. Then
f
is bounded with
\|f\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2\|f(\mathbbm{1})\|_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

II.2.b  Completely Positive Maps

For applications, one often requires mappings which satisfy a stronger positivity-preserving property than the one introduced in
Definition

II.2.1
. (See, for example,
Section

IV.3
.) These maps will be introduced in this subsection.

II.2.5
Matrices over a
C^{\ast}
-algebra.

(
[
23
, Sect. 30.4]
,
[
120
, pp. 2 f.]
)
Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. Denote by
\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})
the space of all
(n\times n)
-matrices
A=[A_{ij}]
with entries
A_{ij}
,
i,j\in\{{1,\dotsc,n}\}
, in the algebra
\mathfrak{A}
; one also writes
A
as

A=\begin{bmatrix}A_{11}&\dots&A_{1n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
A_{n1}&\dots&A_{nn}\end{bmatrix}\ .

It is clear that the space
\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})
is a
\ast
-algebra with respect to the usual matrix multiplication and involution, both being defined in terms of the respective algebraic operations on
\mathfrak{A}
:

[A_{ij}][B_{ij}]\coloneqq\biggl[\,\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ik}B_{kj}\biggr]\quad\text{and}\quad[A_{ij}]^{\ast}\coloneqq[A_{ji}^{\ast}]\ .

Furthermore, one can show that there exists a canonical algebra norm on
\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})
with respect to which this space becomes a
C^{\ast}
-algebra
[
29
, p. 143]
,
[
159
, p. 192]
. Finally, one can show that
[
46
, p. 192]

\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})=\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{C})\ .

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be another
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a linear map. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define linear mappings
f_{(n)}:\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{B})
by setting for all
A=[A_{ij}]\in\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})
:

f_{(n)}\bigl([A_{ij}]\bigr)\coloneqq\bigl[f(A_{ij})\bigr]\ .

(II.3)

Definition II.2.6
(Completely positive mapping).

Let
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a linear map between unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
. For some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f
is called
n
-positive
iff
f_{(n)}:\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{B})
is a positive mapping in the sense of
Definition

II.2.1
. Furthermore,
f
is called
completely positive
iff for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f_{(n)}
is positive.

Remark II.2.7
(Physical interpretation).

(
[
103
, p. 148]
)
Consider a physical system
S_{1}
described by a concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
over a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, and let
S_{2}
be a second system, independent of the system
S_{1}
, which is described by the algebra
\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{C})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. According to the postulates of quantum mechanics
[
130
, Sect. 2.1]
, the Hilbert space for the combined system
S_{1}+S_{2}
is given by the Hilbert-space tensor product
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
, where
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{2})=n
.

Let
T:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}
be a linear mapping. By the duality between the bounded operators
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and the trace-class operators
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
,
cf.

Proposition

II.3.4
below,
T
induces a linear mapping
T^{\ast}
on the space
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1})

[
23
, Lem. 31.3]
. For
T^{\ast}
to send quantum states of
S_{1}
(that is, density matrices on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
) again to quantum states, it is necessary that the mapping
T^{\ast}
is positive.
2
2

2

Although
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
is not a
C^{\ast}
-algebra itself (
Examples

II.1.3

(e)
), it is a
\ast
-ideal in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
(
Proposition

B.1.4
). Therefore, one can define notions such as positivity of elements and complete positivity of mappings similarly as before
[
23
, p. 486]
.

Furthermore, under the assumption that
T
does not influence the system
S_{2}
directly, the mapping
T_{(n)}=T\otimes\mathrm{Id}:\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{C})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{C})
, defined as in
Eq.

II.3
, describes the resulting transformation on the combined system
S_{1}+S_{2}
:
T_{(n)}(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})=T(A_{1})\otimes A_{2}
. Therefore, in order for
(T_{(n)})^{\ast}
to map quantum states of the combined system
S_{1}+S_{2}
to quantum states,
(T_{(n)})^{\ast}
has to be positive,
i.e.
,
T^{\ast}
has to be completely positive which is the case if and only if
T
is completely positive
[
23
, Lem. 31.4]
.

Examples II.2.8
.

(a)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras, let
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a positive linear functional, let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a
\ast
-homomorphism, and let
\alpha:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be defined by
\alpha(A)\coloneqq V^{\ast}\pi(A)V
for some
V\in\mathfrak{B}
. All of these three mappings are completely positive
[
23
, pp. 474 f.]
. To show this, it suffices to check, according to
[
23
, Cor. 30.1]
, that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
, and
\{B_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
, there holds
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}B_{i}^{\ast}\alpha(A_{i}^{\ast}A_{j})B_{j}\geq 0
in the algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. Indeed, for the mapping
\alpha
one finds that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}B_{i}^{\ast}\alpha(A_{i}^{\ast}A_{j})B_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}B_{i}^{\ast}V^{\ast}\pi(A_{i})^{\ast}\pi(A_{j})VB_{j}=\bigg(\,{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(A_{i})VB_{i}}\bigg)^{\ast}\bigg(\,{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\pi(A_{j})VB_{j}}\bigg)

is a positive element in
\mathfrak{B}
by
Theorem

II.1.5
, hence
\alpha
is completely positive.

(b)

The notion of complete positivity can be used to distinguish between classical and quantum situations
[
72
, p. 260]
,
[
95
, p. 449]
: a positive mapping
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
between
C^{\ast}
-algebras is automatically completely positive if either
\mathfrak{A}
or
\mathfrak{B}
is of the form
C^{0}(X;\mathbb{K})
for a compact Hausdorff space
X

[
120
, Thm. 3.9 & 3.11]
,
[
154
, Thm. 1.2.4 & 1.2.5]
.

(c)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
\tr:\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}
be defined by
\tr([A_{ij}])\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{ii}
. Then
\tr
is a positive linear functional because for all
A=[A_{ij}]\in\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathfrak{A})
, there holds

\displaystyle\tr(A^{\ast}A)=\tr\Biggl(\biggl[\,\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ki}^{\ast}A_{kj}\biggr]\Biggr)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ki}^{\ast}A_{ki}\geq 0

since
A_{ki}^{\ast}A_{ki}\geq 0
in
\mathfrak{A}
for all
i,k\in\{{1,\dotsc,n}\}
(
Theorem

II.1.5
) and
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
is a convex cone (
Proposition

II.1.7
). One can show that
\tr
is actually completely positive
[
120
, p. 40]
.

(d)

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be two Hilbert spaces and
\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Define a mapping
\alpha:\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
, which is completely positive
[
130
, Exa. 9.1]
. Its dual map
\alpha^{\ast}:\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
, in the sense discussed in
Remark

II.2.7
, is the so-called
partial trace

\alpha^{\ast}(A\otimes B)=A\tr(B)
which is physically very relevant
[
130
, pp. 11 f.]
. By
[
23
, Lem. 31.4]
,
\alpha^{\ast}
is completely positive.

(e)

Consider the
(2\times 2)
-matrices
\mathfrak{A}=\mathrm{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})
and the transposition
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{T}
. This is a positive mapping: for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, it holds that

f(A^{\ast}A)=(A^{\ast}A)^{T}=A^{T}(A^{\ast})^{T}=A^{T}(A^{T})^{\ast}\geq 0\ .

However,
f
fails to be 2-positive
[
85
, Exa. 2.48]
. To see this, consider the following element of the algebra
\mathrm{Mat}(2;\mathfrak{A})=\mathrm{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathrm{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{Mat}(4;\mathbb{C})
:

A\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
2&0\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}2&0&0&2\\
0&1&1&0\\
0&1&1&0\\
2&0&0&2\end{bmatrix}\ .

Its eigenvalues are
\sigma(A)=\{{4,2,0}\}
(the last eigenvalue appears twice), hence it is positive. Yet,

f_{(2)}(A)=\begin{bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
2&0\end{pmatrix}\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}2&0&0&1\\
0&1&2&0\\
0&2&1&0\\
1&0&0&2\end{bmatrix}

has eigenvalues
\sigma(f_{2}(A))=\{{3,1,-1}\}
(the first eigenvalue appears twice),
i.e.
, it is not a positive element in
\mathrm{Mat}(2;\mathfrak{A})
. One can even show that for all
n>1
,
f_{(n)}
is not positive
[
46
, p. 192]
.

The following result of
M.-D. Choi

[
37
, Prop. 4.1]
characterizes 2-positive mappings.

Proposition II.2.9
.

Let
f:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a positive linear mapping between two unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras, and suppose that
f(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{A}})\in\mathfrak{B}^{+}\cap\mathrm{GL}(\mathfrak{B})
is positive and invertible. Furthermore, let
R,S,T\in\mathfrak{A}
be arbitrary with
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{+}\cap\mathrm{GL}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then the following properties are equivalent:

(a)

f
is 2-positive;

(b)

If
R\geq ST^{-1}S^{\ast}
in
\mathfrak{A}
, then
f(R)\geq f(S)f(T)^{-1}f(S)^{\ast}
in
\mathfrak{B}
;

(c)

f(ST^{-1}S^{\ast})\geq f(S)f(T)^{-1}f(S)^{\ast}
in
\mathfrak{B}
.

II.2.c  Schwarz Mappings

To conclude this section, another class of positive mappings between
C^{\ast}
-algebras is introduced which will be of particular relevance for the study of monotonicity of the relative entropy in
Section

IV.3
. The following definition subscribes to the convention of
[
118
, p. 81]
.

Definition II.2.10
(Schwarz map).

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{A}})
and
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{B}})
be two unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras. A linear mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
is called a
Schwarz mapping
iff
\alpha
is unital, that is,
\alpha(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{A}})=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, and satisfies for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
the following inequality, referred to as
Schwarz inequality
:

\alpha(A)^{\ast}\alpha(A)\leq\alpha(A^{\ast}A)\ .

(II.4)

Lemma II.2.11
.

Every Schwarz mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
is positive and satisfies the inequality
\alpha(A)\alpha(A)^{\ast}\leq\alpha(AA^{\ast})
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof
.

1.
Let
A=B^{\ast}B
,
B\in\mathfrak{A}
, be an arbitrary positive element in the
C^{\ast}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
(
cf.

Theorem

II.1.5
). Then
\alpha(A)=\alpha(B^{\ast}B)\geq\alpha(B)^{\ast}\alpha(B)\geq 0
in
\mathfrak{B}
by (
II.4
).

2.
Since
\mathfrak{A}
is closed under the algebra involution, one can choose
A^{\ast}
in (
II.4
) to obtain
\alpha(A^{\ast})^{\ast}\alpha(A^{\ast})\leq\alpha(AA^{\ast})
. As was just shown,
\alpha
is a positive mapping, hence it is especially
\ast
-preserving by
Proposition

II.2.3
. This proves the asserted inequality.

According to
Lemma

II.2.11
, Schwarz mappings are stronger than positive mappings. Similarly, the next proposition shows that 2-positive mappings are stronger than Schwarz mappings; the proof can be found in
[
120
, Prop. 3.3]
.

Proposition II.2.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be two unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras, and let
\alpha:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{B}
be a unital 2-positive mapping. Then
\alpha
satisfies the Schwarz inequality (
II.4
).

II.3
von Neumann Algebras

In
Examples

II.1.3

(h)
, the notion of a concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra was defined to be a uniformly closed
\ast
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Considering further classes of
\ast
-subalgebras of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
which satisfy stronger closure conditions leads to the concept of
von Neumann algebras
.

II.3.a  Operator Topologies

Let
(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
be a Hilbert space and
\|\cdot\|
be the norm on
\mathcal{H}
induced by
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
. In the following, three important locally convex topologies on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
shall be defined by specifying a family of semi-norms (
cf.

Proposition

A.2.3
). The definitions can be found in
[
23
, Sect. 26.3]
,
[
29
, Sect. 2.4.1]
, or
[
156
, Ch. 1]
.

II.3.1
Strong topology.

For
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, the mapping
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\|A\xi\|
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is clearly a semi-norm. The topology
\mathfrak{T}_{so}\coloneqq\mathfrak{T}_{P_{so}}
induced by the system
P_{so}\coloneqq\{{p_{\xi}:\xi\in\mathcal{H}}\}
of semi-norms

p_{\xi}(A)\coloneqq\|A\xi\|{\ ,\quad}A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})\ ,

is called the
strong operator topology
(also:
so
-topology
) on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Since by
Proposition

A.2.3
a net
(A_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
converges to an operator
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
with respect to
\mathfrak{T}_{so}
if and only if
\lim_{i\in I}p_{x}(A_{i}-A)=\lim_{i\in I}\|(A_{i}-A)\xi\|\rightarrow 0
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, it follows that the strong operator topology is the topology of pointwise convergence on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

II.3.2
Weak topology.

For
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, the mapping
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow|\langle{\xi,A\eta}\rangle|
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a semi-norm. The topology
\mathfrak{T}_{wo}\coloneqq\mathfrak{T}_{P_{wo}}
induced by the family
P_{wo}\coloneqq\{{p_{\xi,\eta}:\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}}\}
, where

p_{\xi,\eta}(A)\coloneqq|\langle{\xi,A\eta}\rangle|{\ ,\quad}A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})\ ,

is called the
weak operator topology
(also:
wo
-topology
) on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. As for the strong operator topology, it follows from
Proposition

A.2.3
that the weak operator topology is the topology of pointwise weak convergence on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

II.3.3
\sigma
-weak topology.

Introduce the notation

\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N};\mathcal{H})\coloneqq\Bigg\{{(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ :\ \xi_{n}\in\mathcal{H},\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|\xi_{n}\|^{2}<+\infty}\Bigg\}

for the space of all sequences
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
which are square-summable,
i.e.
,
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|\xi_{n}\|^{2}<+\infty
.

Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\eta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N};\mathcal{H})
be arbitrary. For every
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, it follows by using first the Cauchy-Schwarz and then Hölder’s inequality that
[
29
, p. 67]

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|\langle{\xi_{n},A\eta_{n}}\rangle|\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\xi_{n}\|\|\eta_{n}\|\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\Bigg(\,{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|\xi_{n}\|^{2}}\Bigg)^{1/2}\Bigg(\,{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|^{2}}\Bigg)^{1/2}<+\infty\ .

This shows that
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|\langle{\xi_{n},A\eta_{n}}\rangle|
is a semi-norm on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. The topology
\mathfrak{T}_{uw}\coloneqq\mathfrak{T}_{P_{uw}}
induced by the system
P_{uw}\coloneqq\big\{{q_{(\xi_{n}),(\eta_{n})}:(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\eta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N};\mathcal{H})}\big\}
of semi-norms

q_{(\xi_{n}),(\eta_{n})}(A)\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|\langle{\xi_{n},A\eta_{n}}\rangle|{\ ,\quad}A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})\ ,

is called the
\sigma
-weak operator topology
(or:
ultraweak operator topology
;
uw
-topology
). It holds that the mappings
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow AB
and
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow BA
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
for fixed
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, as well as the mapping
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{\ast}
, are continuous with respect to both
\mathfrak{T}_{uw}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{wo}

[
29
, Prop. 2.4.2]
.

The next proposition characterizes the
\sigma
-weak topology in terms of trace-class operators. For proofs of the assertions, see
[
23
, Thm. 26.5 and pp. 378 f.]
and
[
29
, Prop. 2.4.3]
.

Proposition II.3.4
(
\sigma
-weak topology and
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
).

It holds that
\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigr)^{\ast}\cong\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)
with respect to the duality mapping

\varPhi:\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} \mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})&\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{\ast}\ ,\\
A&\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\bigl(\varPhi_{A}:\rho\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tr(A\rho)\bigr)\ .\end{aligned}\end{cases}

The weak-
\ast
topology
\sigma\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\bigr)=\sigma\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{\ast},\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\bigr)
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
arising from the above duality, i.e., the topology generated by the semi-norms
p_{\rho}(A)\coloneqq|\tr(A\rho)|
,
\rho\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
(cf.
A.2.7
) coincides with the
\sigma
-weak operator topology:

\mathfrak{T}_{uw}=\sigma\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\bigr)\ .

In particular, for every
\omega\in\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\mathfrak{T}_{uw}\bigr)^{\ast}
, there exist sequences
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\eta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N};\mathcal{H})
, equivalently, a trace-class operator
\rho\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, such that

\omega=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\langle{\xi_{n},\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \eta_{n}}\rangle{\ ,\quad}\text{equivalently}{\ ,\quad}\omega=\tr(\rho\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)\ .

The different topologies introduced in
II.3.1
,
II.3.2
and
II.3.3
are in the following relation (
cf.

A.1.2
) to one another
[
23
, p. 379]
,
[
29
, p. 70]
.

Proposition II.3.5
.

The following relations hold true:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}_{wo}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}_{so}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}_{uw}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{norm}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

II.3.b  Definition, Bicommutant Theorem, Examples

Definition II.3.6
(von Neumann algebra).

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. A
\ast
-subalgebra of operators
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
which contains the identity
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
and which is closed in the strong operator topology is called a
von Neumann algebra
3
3

3

This terminology was introduced by
J. Dixmier
in 1957
[
51
]
. In the works of
J. von Neumann

[
116
]
(and
von Neumann
&
F. J. Murray

[
115
]
), they were called
“rings of operators”
.
(or:
concrete
W^{\ast}
-algebra
).

Remark II.3.7
.

From Mazur’s theorem
[
166
, Cor. 2.11]
, it follows that a von Neumann algebra is also closed with respect to the weak operator topology. Furthermore, since
\mathfrak{T}_{so}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{norm}}
by
Proposition

II.3.5
, it holds that
\mathfrak{M}
is uniformly closed as well,
cf.

A.1.2
, and hence every von Neumann algebra is a concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra according to
Examples

II.1.3

(h)
.

Definition II.3.8
(Commutant).

For a subset
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, the
commutant
is defined to be the set

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{{A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})\ :\ \text{$AB=BA$ for all $B\in\mathfrak{A}$}}\}

of all bounded linear operators which commute with every element from the set
\mathfrak{A}
. Similarly, the
bicommutant
(or:
double commutant
) of
\mathfrak{A}
is defined as
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime})^{\prime}
.

Lemma II.3.9
(
[
112
, Prop. 6.3]
).

Let
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a subset. Then
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is a strongly closed, unital subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. If
\mathfrak{A}
is self-adjoint, then
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is even a von Neumann algebra.

The next theorem is one of the most important and deep results in the theory of von Neumann algebras. It was discovered by
J. von Neumann
in 1930
[
116
, Thm. 8]
, and a modern proof can be found in
[
29
, Thm. 2.4.11]
,
[
112
, Thm. 6.4]
, or
[
156
, Thm. 3.2 & Cor. 3.11]
.

Theorem II.3.10
(von Neumann’s bicommutant theorem).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a
\ast
-subalgebra which contains the identity
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{M}
is a von Neumann algebra;

(b)

\mathfrak{M}
is weakly closed;

(c)

\mathfrak{M}
is
\sigma
-weakly closed;

(d)

\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{M}^{\prime\prime}
.

Examples II.3.11
.

(a)

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space. Consider the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(X,\mu)
and the
C^{\ast}
-algebra
\mathfrak{M}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
from
Examples

II.1.3

(c)
. One can view
\mathfrak{M}
as a
\ast
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
by virtue of the identification
\mathfrak{M}\owns f\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow M_{f}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, where
M_{f}g\coloneqq fg
,
g\in\mathcal{H}
, is the multiplication operator with the function
f
; that is, one identities

\mathfrak{M}\cong\big\{{M_{f}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})\ :\ f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)}\big\}\ .

In this identification,
\mathfrak{M}
becomes a von Neumann algebra acting on
\mathcal{H}
. This can be proved by showing that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, see
[
45
, Thm. IX.6.6]
,
[
46
, Prop. 12.4]
, or
[
156
, Sect. 6.10]
for detailed arguments.

The
C^{\ast}
-algebra of continuous functions
\mathfrak{A}=C^{0}(X;\mathbb{K})
from
Examples

II.1.3

(b)
, which, for
X
a compact topological space, can also be viewed as
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
in terms of the above identification, does not form a von Neumann algebra because
\mathfrak{A}
is only uniformly, but neither strongly nor weakly closed in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})

[
93
, Exa. 5.1.6]
. (
L^{\infty}
-functions are pointwise almost-everywhere limits of continuous functions.)

(b)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Then the
C^{\ast}
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
from
Examples

II.1.3

(e)
is a von Neumann algebra since
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\{\mathbb{C}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\}^{\prime}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, hence
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})^{\prime\prime}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. To see this, note that if
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})^{\prime}
, then
A
commutes, in particular, with every one-dimensional projection
P_{\xi}=\langle{\xi,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ }\rangle\,\xi
,
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
. This implies that there exists a constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
such that
A\xi=\alpha\xi
. Since
A
is linear, it follows for all
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
that
A(\xi+\eta)=\alpha\xi+\beta\eta
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
, but one also has
A(\xi+\eta)=\gamma(\xi+\eta)
for
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
, hence one may conclude
\alpha=\beta=\gamma
, that is,
A=\alpha\,\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

The
C^{\ast}
-algebra of compact operators
\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})
from
Examples

II.1.3

(f)
is not a von Neumann algebra because
\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
as before, and hence
\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{H})^{\prime\prime}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. This follows since finite-rank operators are compact, and they are dense in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

Many applications in mathematical physics require working with
unbounded operators
(see, for example,
Chapter

V
). Due to the flexibility of von Neumann algebras, it turns out that one can
affiliate
unbounded operators with this class of
C^{\ast}
-algebras.

Definition II.3.12
(Affiliated operator).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra. A closed linear operator
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
on
\mathcal{H}
(
Definition

B.2.1
) is said to be
affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
, and the set of these operators is denoted by
\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
, iff for all
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
it holds that

A^{\prime}\bigl(\dom(T)\bigr)\subseteq\dom(T)\quad\text{and}\quad A^{\prime}T\subseteq TA^{\prime}\ .

Essentially, a closed operator
T
is affiliated with a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
T
strongly commutes
[
112
, Prop. 3.70]
with the elements of the commutant
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. The next important result shows that the above definition is somewhat natural
[
50
, Thm. 2.1]
.

Proposition II.3.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be self-adjoint. It holds that
T\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
if and only if all bounded Borel functions of
T
belong to the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
.

II.3.c  Cyclic and Separating Vectors

The concept of a cyclic and separating vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
for a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is of great importance for the theory and will be used extensively in the following three chapters.

II.3.14
Closed subspaces and projections.

For arbitrary subsets
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, introduce the following notation:

\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\lin_{\mathbb{K}}\{{A\xi\,:\,A\in\mathfrak{A},\,\xi\in\mathcal{K}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad[\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K}]\coloneqq\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K})\ .

By the correspondence between orthogonal projections and closed subspaces (
Remark

B.1.9
), there exists a unique orthogonal projection
P\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
onto
[\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K}]
. Often, the projection
P
will also be denoted by the symbol
[\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K}]
. In case that
\mathcal{K}=\{\xi\}
for some
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, one writes
\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K}\equiv\mathfrak{A}\xi
and
[\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{K}]\equiv[\mathfrak{A}\xi]
.

Definition II.3.15
(Cyclic and separating vectors).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra. A set of vectors
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is said to be
cyclic
for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
iff
[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]=\mathcal{H}
, that is, if the subspace generated by
\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}
with respect to the norm topology. The set
\mathcal{S}
is called
separating
for
\mathfrak{M}
iff for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
,
A\xi=0
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{S}
implies that
A=0
.

Proposition II.3.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a subset.

(a)

\mathcal{S}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
\mathcal{S}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.

(b)

\mathcal{S}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
\mathcal{S}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.

Proof
.

Ad

(a)
.
[
29
, Prop. 2.5.3]
Assume first that
\mathcal{S}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
. Let
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
be arbitrary such that for every
\xi\in\mathcal{S}
,
A^{\prime}\xi=0
. For all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, it follows that
A^{\prime}A\xi=AA^{\prime}\xi=0
, and hence
A^{\prime}[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]=0
by linearity and continuity of
A^{\prime}
. Since
[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]=\mathcal{H}
by assumption, one can conclude
A^{\prime}=0
which shows that
\mathcal{S}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.

Conversely, assume that for every
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
which satisfies
A^{\prime}\xi=0
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{S}
, it follows that
A^{\prime}=0
. Let
P\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
, then the claim
[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]=\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to the operator identity
P=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, see
Corollary

B.1.10

(b)
. On the set
\mathcal{S}
, it clearly holds that
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-P=0
since
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathfrak{M}
and hence
\mathcal{S}\subseteq[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]
. Moreover,
P\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}

[
29
, p. 73]
: for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, one has
AP\eta\in[\mathfrak{M}\mathcal{S}]
by definition of this space, hence
AP=PAP
(
Lemma

B.1.7
). This gives
PA=(A^{\ast}P)^{\ast}=(PA^{\ast}P)^{\ast}=PAP=AP
. Therefore, it follows that
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-P\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
by
Lemma

II.3.9
which, together with the previous observation and the assumption, implies that
P=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Ad

(b)
. If
\mathcal{S}
is separating for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
, it is for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime\prime}
as well by
Theorem

II.3.10
. Using assertion
(a)
together with the fact that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is a von Neumann algebra (
Lemma

II.3.9
), it follows that
\mathcal{S}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. Conversely, if
\mathcal{S}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
, it is separating for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime\prime}
by
(a)
, and hence also for
\mathfrak{M}
.

II.3.17
Cyclic projections.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary. Projections of the form
P_{\xi}\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]
and
P^{\prime}_{\xi}\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}\xi]
are called
cyclic projections
. It holds that
P_{\xi}\in\mathfrak{M}
and
P^{\prime}_{\xi}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. Indeed, in the proof of
Proposition

II.3.16
, it was already shown that
P^{\prime}_{\xi}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. Therefore, it also follows that
P_{\xi}=[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime\prime}=\mathfrak{M}
.

A vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
is cyclic for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
P^{\prime}_{\xi}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and it is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
P_{\xi}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
. This can be seen as follows:
\xi
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}

\Leftrightarrow

[\mathfrak{M}\xi]=\mathcal{H}

\Leftrightarrow

P^{\prime}_{\xi}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
according to
Corollary

B.1.10

(b)
. Similarly,
\xi
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}

\Leftrightarrow

\xi
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime\prime}

\Leftrightarrow

\xi
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}

\Leftrightarrow

[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]=\mathcal{H}

\Leftrightarrow

P_{\xi}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
by
Proposition

II.3.16
.

II.4
States and Representations

The goal of this section is to argue that every abstract
C^{\ast}
-algebra can be realized as a subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
for a suitable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. To this end, the important notions of a
state
on a
C^{\ast}
-algebra and a
normal state
on a von Neumann algebra will be introduced and studied in some detail. The former will be employed in the so-called
GNS-representation
of a
C^{\ast}
-algebra.

II.4.a  General States on a
C^{\ast}
-Algebra

Definition II.4.1
(State).

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbbm{1})
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra. A linear functional
\omega:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is called a
state
iff it is positive and
\omega(\mathbbm{1})=1
. The set of all states on the algebra
\mathfrak{A}
will be denoted by the symbol
\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
. If, for every
A\in\mathfrak{A}
,
\omega(A^{\ast}A)=0
implies
A=0
, then the state
\omega
is called
faithful
.

The following proposition contains fundamental properties of positive linear functionals on
C^{\ast}
-algebras. Its proof can be found, for example, in
[
17
, Prop. 5]
or
[
29
, Lem. 2.3.10]
.

Proposition II.4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
\omega:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a positive linear functional. Then the mapping
\mathfrak{q}_{\omega}:\mathfrak{A}\times\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{\omega}(A,B)\coloneqq\omega(A^{\ast}B)
, is a positive semi-definite Hermitian sesquilinear form on
\mathfrak{A}
. In particular, it satisfies the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
:

\forall A,B\in\mathfrak{A}\ :\ |\omega(A^{\ast}B)|^{2}\leq\omega(A^{\ast}A)\,\omega(B^{\ast}B)\ .

(II.5)

Examples II.4.3
.

(a)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathfrak{A}=\mathrm{Mat}(n;\mathbb{C})
from
Examples

II.1.3

(d)
. Consider the trace functional
\tr:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tr(A)
, which was shown to be a positive mapping in
Examples

II.2.8

(c)
. A corresponding state
\tau:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
can be obtained by setting
\tau(A)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\tr(A)
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
.

(b)

Let
(X,\mathfrak{T})
be a compact Hausdorff space, let
\mathfrak{A}=C^{0}(X;\mathbb{C})
be the
C^{\ast}
-algebra of continuous functions on
X
(
Examples

II.1.3

(b)
), and let
\mu
be a measure on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(X)
of
X
(that is, the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\mathfrak{T}
). Define a functional
\omega_{\mu}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\omega_{\mu}(f)\coloneqq\int\nolimits_{X}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\quad(f\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

From the properties of the
\mu
-integral, it follows that
\omega_{\mu}
is positive (
f\geq 0\,\Rightarrow\,\int_{X}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\geq 0
). Furthermore, if
\mu
is a probability measure,
i.e.
,
\mu(X)=1
, then
\omega_{\mu}
is a state on
\mathfrak{A}
.

(c)

Let
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a unital concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra (
Examples

II.1.3

(h)
) and
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
be a vector. Define a functional
\omega_{\varOmega}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\omega_{\varOmega}(A)\coloneqq\langle{\varOmega,A\varOmega}\rangle\quad(A\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

This functional is positive since for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, it holds that
\omega_{\varOmega}(A^{\ast}A)=\langle{A\varOmega,A\varOmega}\rangle=\|A\varOmega\|^{2}\geq 0
. One refers to
\omega_{\varOmega}
as the
vector functional
induced by the vector
\varOmega
. If
\|\varOmega\|=1
, then it follows that
\omega_{\varOmega}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=1
, hence
\omega_{\varOmega}
defines a state on
\mathfrak{A}
.

(d)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, let
\mathfrak{A}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
(
Examples

II.1.3

(e)
), and let
\rho\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{+}
be a positive trace-class operator. Defining
\omega_{\rho}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\omega_{\rho}(A)\coloneqq\tr(\rho A)\quad(A\in\mathfrak{A})

yields a positive linear functional:
\omega_{\rho}(A^{\ast}A)=\tr(\rho A^{\ast}A)=\tr(\rho^{1/2}A^{\ast}A\rho^{1/2})\geq 0
due to cyclicity and positivity of the trace. Furthermore, if
\rho
is a density matrix (
Definition

B.1.5
),
i.e.
,

\tr(\rho)=1
, then
\omega_{\rho}
is a state on
\mathfrak{A}
. States of this form are called
normal states
. One can show that
\omega_{\rho}
is faithful if and only if
\rho
is invertible
[
29
, Exa. 2.5.5]
.

If
\dim(\mathcal{H})<+\infty
, then every state on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is induced by a density matrix,
i.e.
,
\varSigma(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}))=\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})

[
100
, Thm. 2.7 & 2.8]
. This is no longer true if
\dim(\mathcal{H})=+\infty
, see
[
100
, p. 109]
.

II.4.b  Normal States on a von Neumann Algebra

In this subsection, normal states defined on an arbitrary von Neumann algebra will be investigated more closely.

Definition II.4.4
(Normal functional).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{T}_{uw})^{\ast}
be the set of all
\sigma
-weakly continuous linear functionals. A positive element of
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}
is called a
normal functional
, and the set of all these is denoted by the symbol
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
. A normal functional
\omega
satisfying
\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=1
is called a
normal state
, and the set of these is denoted
\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
.

Proposition II.4.5
(
[
29
, Prop. 2.4.18]
).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra. The space
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}
is a Banach space with respect to the operator norm, and
\mathfrak{M}
is the dual of
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}
with respect to the duality
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}\owns(A,\omega)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\omega(A)\in\mathbb{C}
, that is,
\mathfrak{M}=(\mathfrak{M}_{\ast})^{\ast}
.

Remarks II.4.6
.

(a)

The previous assertion establishes that every von Neumann algebra possesses a
predual space
. Generally, an abstract
C^{\ast}
-algebra which possesses a predual space is called a
W^{\ast}
-algebra
.
4
4

4

The terminology
“
W^{\ast}
-algebra”
was also introduced by
I. E. Segal
in 1951
[
148
, Def. 2.1]
.

It was shown by
S. Sakai
in 1956 that every
W^{\ast}
-algebra is a von Neumann algebra
[
142
]
,
[
141
, Thm. 1.16.7]
, hence these two notions are actually equivalent.

(b)

Let
\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}
be
\sigma
-weakly continuous. From
Proposition

II.3.5
and
A.1.2
, it follows that
\omega\in\mathfrak{M}^{\ast}
. Furthermore, characterization of continuity in a locally convex space
[
171
, Cor. VIII.2.4]
and the Hahn-Banach theorem
[
171
, Thm. III.1.4]
imply that
\omega
can be extended to a
\sigma
-weakly continuous linear functional on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
so that
Proposition

II.3.4
applies
[
29
, p. 75]
.

The next important result gives a characterization of normal states. Proofs of the assertions can be found in
[
29
, Thm. 2.4.21]
,
[
156
, Thm. 5.11]
, and
[
159
, Prop. II.3.20]
.

Theorem II.4.7
(Characterization of normal states).

Let
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{M})
. The following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

\omega
is normal;

(b)

there exist a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N};\mathcal{H})
such that
\omega=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\langle{\xi_{n},\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \xi_{n}}\rangle
;

(c)

there exists a density matrix
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\omega=\tr(\rho\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
;

(d)

for every increasing net
(A_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{+}
with an upper bound (with respect to the order relation from
II.1.9
), there holds
\omega\bigl(\sup_{i\in I}A_{i}\bigr)=\sup_{i\in I}\omega(A_{i})
.

Examples II.4.8
.

(a)

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a commutative von Neumann algebra on a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. According to a theorem of
I. E. Segal

[
148
,
149
]
, there is a compact Hausdorff space
X
and a measure
\mu
on
\bigl(X,\mathcal{B}(X)\bigr)
such that
\mathcal{H}\cong L^{2}(X,\mu)
and
\mathfrak{M}\cong L^{\infty}(X,\mu)

[
45
, Thm. IX.7.8]
,
[
51
, Sect. I.7.3, Thm. 1]
,
[
141
, Prop. 1.18.1]
. Since
L^{1}(X,\mu)^{\ast}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)

[
171
, Thm. II.2.4]
, it follows that the predual space of
\mathfrak{M}
is given by
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}=L^{1}(X,\mu)
. Explicitly, a function
h\in L^{1}(X,\mu)
gives rise to the measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\coloneqq h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
which induces the functional
\omega_{\nu}(f)=\int_{X}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu
,
f\in\mathfrak{M}
(
Examples

II.4.3

(b)
). If, additionally,
h
is assumed to be non-negative, then one can define
g\coloneqq\sqrt{h}\in\mathcal{H}
to obtain

\omega_{\nu}(f)=\int\nolimits_{X}fh\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int\nolimits_{X}\overline{g}fg\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\langle{g,fg}\rangle=\omega_{g}(f)\ ,

that is,
\omega_{\nu}
is equal to the vector functional
\omega_{g}
,
g\in\mathcal{H}
(
Examples

II.4.3

(c)
). This functional is normal by
Theorem

II.4.7
, and, in fact, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
and
h_{\omega}\in L^{1}(X,\mu)_{+}

[
29
, p. 83]
. Since
g_{\omega}\coloneqq\sqrt{h_{\omega}}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
is well-defined, one may also interpret this as a correspondence
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}\cong L^{2}(X,\mu)_{+}
, where
\omega=\omega_{g_{\omega}}=\langle{g_{\omega},\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ g_{\omega}}\rangle
is a vector functional.

(b)

For
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
,
Proposition

II.3.4
showed that
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{\ast}\cong\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})_{\ast}\cong\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
(considering
Proposition

A.2.5
), hence
(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})_{\ast})^{\ast}\cong\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Thus, in this case the first equivalences of
Theorem

II.4.7
are clear. More generally, let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be an arbitrary von Neumann algebra.
Theorem

II.4.7
implies that the functionals
\omega_{\xi}=\langle{\xi,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \xi}\rangle
,
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, and
\omega_{\rho}=\tr(\rho\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
,
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
, from
Examples

II.4.3

(c)
and
(d)
are normal states on
\mathfrak{M}
in the sense of
Definition

II.4.4
.

II.4.c  Support of a Normal Functional

II.4.9
Support projection.

(
[
156
, Sect. 5.15]
)
Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
be a normal functional on
\mathfrak{M}
. Using spectral theory, one can show the following result
[
156
, p. 123]
.

Statement
.

If
A\in\mathfrak{M}^{+}
is a positive element in
\mathfrak{M}
such that
\varphi(A)=0
, then it follows that
\varphi\bigl(\mathbf{s}(A)\bigr)=0
, where
\mathbf{s}(A)
denotes the support of
A
(introduced in
B.1.13
). Consequently, the family
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\{{P\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})\,:\,\varphi(P)=0}\}
is increasingly directed.

Denote the supremum of the family
\mathcal{F}
by
P_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})
, and let
(P_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a net such that
P_{i}\uparrow P_{0}
. Using that
\varphi
is normal (
Theorem

II.4.7
), it follows that

\varphi(P_{0})=\varphi\bigg(\,{\sup_{i\in I}P_{i}}\bigg)=\sup_{i\in I}\varphi(P_{i})=0\ .

This shows that
P_{0}
is the largest projection in
\mathfrak{M}
on which
\varphi
vanishes. Equivalently, the projection
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-P_{0}
, which is called the
support
(
projection
) of
\varphi
, is the
smallest projection in
\mathfrak{M}
satisfying the relation

\varphi\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)=\varphi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\ .

(II.6)

The following two identities are basic consequences of the definition of
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)
which are often useful. They are stated in
[
156
, Sect. 5.15]
and
[
155
, Eq. (2.2)]
, respectively.

Lemma II.4.10
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
. For all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, there holds

\varphi(A)=\varphi\bigl(A\,\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)=\varphi\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\varphi)A\bigr)=\varphi\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\varphi)A\,\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)\ .

(II.7)

Proof
.

By linearity of
\varphi
, the definition of
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)
, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
II.5
) from
Proposition

II.4.2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\big|{\varphi(A)-\varphi\bigl(A\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)}\big|^{2}=\big|{\varphi\bigl(A(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\varphi))\bigr)}\big|\leq\varphi(AA^{\ast})\,\varphi\bigl(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)=0

which shows the first equality. The second identity is proved similarly, and third one follows from the first two identities.

Lemma II.4.11
.

For all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, it holds that
\varphi(A^{\ast}A)=0\Leftrightarrow A\,\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=0
.

Proof
.

1.
Assume that
\varphi(A^{\ast}A)=0
. From
II.4.9
, it follows that
\varphi\bigl(\mathbf{s}(A^{\ast}A)\bigr)=0
. Since
\mathbf{s}(A^{\ast}A)=\mathbf{r}(A)

[
156
, p. 26]
, where
\mathbf{r}(A)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\bigl[\ker(A)\bigr]
is the right support of
A
(
B.1.13
), and
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\varphi)
is the largest projection in
\mathfrak{M}
contained in the kernel of
\varphi
, one obtains

\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\bigl[\ker(A)\bigr]\leq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\ .

This implies
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\leq\bigl[\ker(A)\bigr]
, hence
\ran\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)\subseteq\ker(A)
by definition of the order relation for projection operators (
B.1.12
). It is now an immediate consequence that
A\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=0
.

2.
Conversely, if one assumes that the last identity is satisfied for arbitrary
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, one computes using
Eq.

II.7
that

\displaystyle\varphi(A^{\ast}A)=\varphi\bigl(A^{\ast}A\,\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)=\varphi(0)=0\ .

One of the most important applications of the support projection is to detect whether a normal functional is faithful. The following is stated in
[
155
, p. 10]
and
[
156
, p. 123]
.

Proposition II.4.12
.

The normal functional
\varphi
is faithful if and only if
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof
.

1.
Assume first that
\varphi
is faithful,
i.e.
, for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
the implication
\varphi(A^{\ast}A)=0\,\Rightarrow\,A=0
holds true. By
Lemma

II.4.11
, this is equivalent to
A\,\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=0\,\Rightarrow\,A=0
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
. In particular, one can choose any projection
P\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})
in place of
A
. But then the identity
P\,\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=0
is equivalent to the projections
P
and
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)
being orthogonal
[
112
, Prop. 3.17]
, hence the previous implication asserts that
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)
is only orthogonal to the zero projection, that is,
\ran(\mathbf{s}(\varphi))^{\perp}=\{0\}
.
Corollary

B.1.10

(b)
now implies that
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

2.
Conversely, assume that
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Relying once again on
Lemma

II.4.11
, it follows that for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
,
\varphi(A^{\ast}A)=0\Rightarrow A=0
, thus
\varphi
is faithful. This completes the proof.

As another important application, the support of vector functionals, introduced in
Examples

II.4.3

(c)
, shall be computed; this will yield cyclic projections (
II.3.17
)
[
156
, p. 131]
.

Proposition II.4.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra, let
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary, and let
\omega_{\xi}=\langle{\xi,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \xi}\rangle
be the vector functional induced by
\xi
on
\mathfrak{M}
. Denote the same functional on the commutant
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
of the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
by
\omega_{\xi}^{\prime}=\langle{\xi,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \xi}\rangle
