. Then

\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})=P_{\xi}=[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi}^{\prime})=P_{\xi}^{\prime}=[\mathfrak{M}\xi]\ .

Proof
.

First, it is clear that
\omega_{\xi}(P_{\xi})=\omega_{\xi}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
since
\xi\in[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]
, hence
P_{\xi}
satisfies the same property as the support
\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})
. Therefore, it only remains to show that
P_{\xi}\leq\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})
which, by the minimality of the support projection, then implies
P_{\xi}=\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})
. Recall from
B.1.12
that the relation
P_{\xi}\leq\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})
is equivalent to
\ran(P_{\xi})\subseteq\ran\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})\bigr)
. Let
\eta\in\ran(P_{\xi})
be arbitrary, that is,
\eta=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,A_{n}^{\prime}\xi
for a sequence
(A_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. One computes

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})\eta=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})A_{n}^{\prime}\xi=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,A_{n}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})\xi=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,A_{n}^{\prime}\xi=\eta\ ,

where first continuity of the support, then the fact that
\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, and finally the identity
\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})\xi=\xi
were used. (Note that the last two properties hold by construction of the support projection.) From
Lemma

B.1.7
, it now follows that
\eta\in\ran\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\omega_{\xi})\bigr)
, and thus the proof of the first identity is complete. For the second relation, one proceeds analogously.

Corollary II.4.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra, and let
\varphi=\omega_{\xi}
be a vector functional on
\mathfrak{M}
induced by
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
\varphi
is faithful if and only if
\xi
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
.

Proof
.

By
Proposition

II.4.12
, the normal functional
\varphi
is faithful if and only if
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and by
Proposition

II.4.13
, the support takes the form
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]
. According to
II.3.17
, the condition
[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\xi]=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
is equivalent to
\xi
being separating for
\mathfrak{M}
.

II.4.d  The GNS-Construction

Definition II.4.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra. A
\ast
-representation
of
\mathfrak{A}
is pair
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
consisting of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a
\ast
-homomorphism
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. It is called
faithful
iff
\pi
is injective. Two
\ast
-representations
(\mathcal{H}_{1},\pi_{1})
and
(\mathcal{H}_{2},\pi_{2})
are said to be
unitarily equivalent
iff there exists a unitary operator
U:\mathcal{H}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that

\forall A\in\mathfrak{A}\ :\ \pi_{2}(A)=U\pi_{1}(A)U^{\ast}\ .

A
cyclic representation
of
\mathfrak{A}
is a triple
(\mathcal{H},\pi,\varOmega)
consisting of a
\ast
-representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
\mathfrak{A}
and a cyclic vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
for the set
\pi(\mathfrak{A})\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, that is, a vector such that
[\pi(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega]=\mathcal{H}
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbbm{1})
be a unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
be an arbitrary state. The goal of this subsection is to construct a specific cyclic representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega_{\omega}}
for a suitable vector
\varOmega_{\omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
. The following presentation closely follows
[
29
, Sect. 2.3.3 & 2.3.4]
.

II.4.16
Construction of the representation.

Step 1.
The idea to construct
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
is the following: by definition,
\mathfrak{A}
is a Banach space, and according to
Proposition

II.4.2
, the state
\omega
may be used to define a semi-inner product
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}
on
\mathfrak{A}
, given for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
by

\langle{A,B}\rangle_{\omega}\coloneqq\omega(A^{\ast}B)\ .

(II.8)

This is not positive-definite because
\langle{A,A}\rangle_{\omega}=\omega(A^{\ast}A)=0
does not, in general, imply that
A=0
. (Indeed, this is only the case for a
faithful
state.) One has to factor out the set

\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}\coloneqq\{{A\in\mathfrak{A}\ :\ \omega(A^{\ast}A)=0}\}

(II.9)

in order for
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}
to become a proper inner product on
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
. It turns out that
\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
is a left ideal of the algebra
\mathfrak{A}

[
29
, p. 54]
,
[
17
, p. 19]
.

For
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, let
[A]_{\omega}\coloneqq\{{A+B:B\in\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}}\}
be the equivalence class of
A
in
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
. As usual, one defines a linear structure on this quotient by setting
[A]_{\omega}+[B]_{\omega}\coloneqq[A+B]_{\omega}
and
\lambda[A]_{\omega}\coloneqq[\lambda A]_{\omega}
which does not depend on the specific representatives of the classes. For all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
, define

\big\langle{[A]_{\omega},[B]_{\omega}}\big\rangle_{\omega}\coloneqq\langle{A,B}\rangle_{\omega}=\omega(A^{\ast}B)\ .

This is an inner product on
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
: it is well-defined because
\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
is a left ideal of
\mathfrak{A}

[
29
, p. 55]
,
[
17
, pp. 19 f.]
, and it is positive definite since
\big\langle{[A]_{\omega},[A]_{\omega}}\big\rangle_{\omega}=0
implies
\omega(A^{\ast}A)=0
, hence
A\in\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
and
[A]_{\omega}=[0]_{\omega}
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
denote the completion of
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
with respect to the norm induced by
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}
. It is well-known that this space is a Hilbert space
[
171
, Thm. V.1.8 (c)]
, and that
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
contains the quotient
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
as a dense linear subspace
[
171
, p. 3 & Cor. III.3.2]
.

Step 2.
The next step is to construct the representatives
\pi_{\omega}(A)\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. First, define
\pi_{\omega}(A)
on
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
by setting for all
B\in\mathfrak{A}
:

\pi_{\omega}(A)[B]_{\omega}\coloneqq[AB]_{\omega}\ .

(II.10)

It holds that
\pi_{\omega}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega})
is a well-defined
\ast
-homomorphism
[
29
, p. 55]
,
[
17
, p. 20]
. From this and the fact that
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, it follows that
\pi_{\omega}(A)\in\mathscr{B}(\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega})
can be uniquely extended to a bounded linear operator on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
(
Theorem

B.1.1
); this operator shall be denoted by the same symbol. Hence, one obtains a
\ast
-representation
\pi_{\omega}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
.

Step 3.
It remains to construct a vector
\varOmega_{\omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
such that
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega_{\omega}}

[
29
, p. 55]
,
[
17
, p. 20]
. Define
\varOmega_{\omega}\coloneqq[\mathbbm{1}]_{\omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
. For all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, it follows that

\langle{\varOmega_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}(A)\varOmega_{\omega}}\rangle_{\omega}=\big\langle{[\mathbbm{1}]_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}(A)[\mathbbm{1}]_{\omega}}\big\rangle_{\omega}=\big\langle{[\mathbbm{1}]_{\omega},[A]_{\omega}}\big\rangle_{\omega}=\omega(\mathbbm{1}^{\ast}A)=\omega(A)\ .

Furthermore, since
\pi_{\omega}(A)\varOmega_{\omega}=[A]_{\omega}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, one obtains that
\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega_{\omega}=\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
. By the very construction of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|_{\omega}}(\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega})
, this shows that the set
\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega_{\omega}
lies dense in the latter. Therefore,
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
is a
cyclic representation
of the
C^{\ast}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.

The following important theorem asserts that the representation constructed above is essentially unique
[
29
, Thm. 2.3.16]
. Motivated by an algebraic description of quantum theory, the theorem was originally obtained by
I. E. Segal
in 1947
[
147
, Thm. 1]
, building on the pioneering work of
I. M. Gelfand
and
M. A. Naimark

[
70
]
.

Theorem II.4.17
(Gelfand-Naimark-Segal construction).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
be a state. Then there exists a cyclic representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that for every
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, there holds
\omega(A)=\langle{\varOmega_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}(A)\varOmega_{\omega}}\rangle_{\omega}
. This representation is, moreover, unique up to unitary equivalence.

Proof
.

The existence of the representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
was proved above in
II.4.16
. Hence, only uniqueness must be shown. To this end, assume that
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega}^{\prime},\pi_{\omega}^{\prime},\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime})
is another cyclic representation for
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}}
. Define an operator
U:\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}^{\prime}
by

U\pi_{\omega}(A)\varOmega_{\omega}\coloneqq\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(A)\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}\quad(A\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

(II.11)

Note that this mapping is clearly linear due to the linearity of
\pi_{\omega}
and
\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}
, and it satisfies the following relation for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
:

\displaystyle\big\langle{U\pi_{\omega}(A)\varOmega_{\omega},U\pi_{\omega}(B)\varOmega_{\omega}}\big\rangle_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\big\langle{\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(A)\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime},\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(B)\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{\omega}=\omega(A^{\ast}B)=\big\langle{\pi_{\omega}(A)\varOmega_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}(B)\varOmega_{\omega}}\big\rangle_{\omega}\ .

Hence,
U
is an isometry (so in particular injective and well-defined) with dense domain
\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega_{\omega}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
and with dense range
\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\omega}^{\prime}
. Therefore, it can be extended uniquely to a unitary operator
U:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\omega}^{\prime}
(
Theorem

B.1.1
). Finally, for
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
one computes using
U^{\ast}U=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{\omega}}
that

\displaystyle\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(A)\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(B)\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=U\pi_{\omega}(AB)\varOmega_{\omega}=U\pi_{\omega}(A)U^{\ast}U\pi_{\omega}(B)\varOmega_{\omega}=U\pi_{\omega}(A)U^{\ast}\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(B)\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}\ ,

hence
\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(A)=U\pi_{\omega}(A)U^{\ast}
on the dense subspace
\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega_{\omega}^{\prime}
; by continuous extension, this identity holds true also on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}^{\prime}
which shows that the two cyclic representations are unitarily equivalent.

Definition II.4.18
(GNS-representation).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
be a state. The unique cyclic representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
constructed from
\omega
in
Theorem

II.4.17
is termed
GNS-representation
or
canonical cyclic representation
of
\mathfrak{A}
associated with
\omega
.

The following two corollaries can be obtained immediately from the GNS-construction. They concern states with additional properties, namely
faithful
and
invariant states
, and they will be needed later on. The second corollary is from
[
29
, Cor. 2.3.17]
.

Corollary II.4.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra and
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
be a state. If
\omega
is faithful, then the GNS-representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
is faithful.

Proof
.

By
Definition

II.4.15
, it has to be verified that
\pi_{\omega}
is injective. To this end, let
A\in\ker(\pi)\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
be arbitrary. Then it follows that
\omega(A^{\ast}A)=\langle{\varOmega_{\omega},\pi(A^{\ast}A)\varOmega_{\omega}}\rangle_{\omega}=\|\pi(A)\varOmega_{\omega}\|^{2}=0
, hence one may conclude that
A=0
because
\omega
is faithful. This shows that
\ker(\pi)=\{0\}
.

Corollary II.4.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra, let
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
be a state on
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
\tau\in\Aut(\mathfrak{A})
be a
\ast
-automorphism on
\mathfrak{A}
which leaves
\omega
invariant, that is,
\omega\bigl(\tau(A)\bigr)=\omega(A)
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Then there exists a uniquely defined unitary operator
U_{\omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
is the representation space of the cyclic representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
of
\mathfrak{A}
, such that

U_{\omega}\varOmega_{\omega}=\varOmega_{\omega}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{\omega}\pi_{\omega}(A)U_{\omega}^{\ast}=\pi_{\omega}\bigl(\tau(A)\bigr)\quad(A\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

Proof
.

This is a consequence of the uniqueness of the cyclic representation, applied to the representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}^{\prime},\varOmega_{\omega})
with
\pi_{\omega}^{\prime}\coloneqq\pi_{\omega}\circ\tau
. Note, in particular, that
Eq.

II.11
determines the unitary operator
U_{\omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
uniquely due to the fact that
\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{A})\varOmega_{\omega}
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
.

The following theorem, which was first obtained in
[
70
, Thm. 1]
and which is a cornerstone of
C^{\ast}
-algebra theory, shows that
\ast
-representations actually exist by combining the GNS-representation with the Hahn-Banach theorem. The proof can be found,
e.g.
, in
[
29
, Thm. 2.1.10]
or
[
171
, Thm. IX.3.15]
.

Theorem II.4.21
(Gelfand-Naimark; non-commutative version).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra. Then there exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathfrak{A}
is isometrically
\ast
-isomorphic to a uniformly closed
\ast
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

Remark II.4.22
.

In
Examples

II.1.3

(h)
, a
concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra
was defined to be a uniformly closed
\ast
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, and it was shown that such an algebra is an abstract
C^{\ast}
-algebra in the sense of
Definition

II.1.2
. Conversely, the fundamental
Theorem

II.4.21
asserts that
every abstract
C^{\ast}
-algebra is
\ast
-isomorphic to a concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebra
. Therefore, combining these results, it follows that abstract and concrete
C^{\ast}
-algebras are equivalent notions.

Examples II.4.23
.

(a)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a commutative unital
C^{\ast}
-algebra. According to the commutative version of the Gelfand-Naimark theorem (see
[
70
, Lem. 1]
as well as
[
29
, Thm. 2.1.11]
and
[
171
, Thm. IX.3.4]
), there exists a compact Hausdorff space
X
such that
\mathfrak{A}\cong C^{0}(X)
. Let
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
be an arbitrary state on
\mathfrak{A}
. Then from the Riesz-Markov-Kakutani representation theorem
[
45
, Thm. C.17]
,
[
171
, Thm. II.2.5]
, one obtains a unique measure
\mu
on the space
\bigl(X,\mathcal{B}(X)\bigr)
such that
\mu(X)=1
and
\omega=\omega_{\mu}
(
cf.

Examples

II.4.3

(b)
), that is

\omega(f)=\int\nolimits_{X}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\quad(f\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

To obtain the GNS-representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\varOmega_{\omega})
of
\mathfrak{A}
, note that the ideal
\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
from
Eq.

II.9
is given by
\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}=\{{f\in\mathfrak{A}\,:\,\int_{X}|f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=0}\}
, and that the inner product
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}
on
\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
takes the form
\langle{f,g}\rangle_{\omega}=\int_{X}\overline{f}g\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
. Since this is the usual
L^{2}
-inner product, it follows that
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}=L^{2}(X,\mu)
. According to
Eq.

II.10
, the representation
\pi_{\omega}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
acts like
\pi_{\omega}(f)g=fg
for
f\in\mathfrak{A}
and
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, that is,
\pi_{\omega}(f)=M_{f}
is the multiplication operator with the function
f
. Finally, the vector
\varOmega_{\omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
is identified with the constant function
\mathbf{1}_{X}\in\mathfrak{A}
. Thus, the GNS-representation of
(C^{0}(X),\omega_{\mu})
is given by

\bigl(L^{2}(X,\mu),\,M_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}},\,\mathbf{1}_{X}\bigr)\ .

(b)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathfrak{A}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Consider a state
\omega\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{A})
given by a density matrix
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
as in
Examples

II.4.3

(d)
, that is,

\omega(A)=\tr(\rho A)\quad(A\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

Assume, for simplicity, that
\rho
is invertible,
i.e.
, that
\omega
is faithful. The more general case of singular
\rho
is discussed,
e.g.
, in
[
131
, Exa. 4.32]
.

The pre-inner product
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}
from
Eq.

II.8
takes the form

\langle{A,B}\rangle_{\omega}=\omega(A^{\ast}B)=\tr(\rho A^{\ast}B)=\tr\bigl((A\rho^{1/2})^{\ast}\,B\rho^{1/2}\bigr)=\big\langle{A\rho^{1/2},B\rho^{1/2}}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}

(II.12)

for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
, where in the last step (
B.1
) was used. Note that since
\rho
is trace-class,
\rho^{1/2}\in\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, hence the above expression is well-defined because
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is an ideal (
Proposition

B.1.4
). The left ideal
\mathfrak{J}_{\omega}
from
Eq.

II.9
used in the GNS-construction is trivial since
\omega
is faithful. Therefore, the GNS-representation space
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
is given by the completion of
\mathfrak{A}
with respect to the norm induced by
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}
, the
\ast
-homomorphism
\pi_{\omega}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
can be defined by
\pi_{\omega}(A)B=AB
for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
, and finally the vector representative of
\omega
is given by
\varOmega_{\omega}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
.

Define the following linear and unitary mapping:

U:\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} \bigl(\mathfrak{A},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega}\bigr)&\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigr)\ ,\\
A&\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A\rho^{1/2}\ .\end{aligned}\end{cases}

The inverse
U^{-1}:\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}
is given by
U^{-1}B=B\rho^{-1/2}
, and
U
is an isometry by (
II.12
). Hence, by uniqueness of the GNS-representation (
Theorem

II.4.17
), one may identify
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\omega})
with
(\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}})
, the transformed
\ast
-automorphism
\widetilde{\pi}_{\omega}:\mathfrak{A}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\bigr)
,
\widetilde{\pi}_{\omega}(A)=U\pi_{\omega}(A)U^{-1}
, still takes the form
\widetilde{\pi}_{\omega}(A)B=AB
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
,
B\in\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, and the vector representative
\varOmega_{\omega}
of
\omega
is transformed to
U\varOmega_{\omega}=\rho^{1/2}
. Thus, the GNS-representation of
(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\omega_{\rho})
is given by

\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),\,M_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}},\,\rho^{1/2}\bigr)\ .

Chapter III
Modular Theory in von Neumann Algebras

This chapter is devoted to
Tomita-Takesaki modular theory
and related notions which have revolutionized both the purely mathematical study of von Neumann algebras
[
156
, p. 276]
as well their applications to algebraic quantum field theory
[
26
]
,
[
32
, p. 5]
and quantum statistical mechanics
[
30
]
. First,
Section

III.1
introduces the
modular data
for
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebras. This framework is then used to define the
standard form
representation in
Section

III.2
. Building on that,
Section

III.3
discusses
relative modular operators
and some of their properties. Finally,
Section

III.4
treats the
spatial derivative
which is a generalization of the relative modular operator.

References
.

The main sources are the recent book
[
83
]
as well as
[
29
, Ch. 2.5]
,
[
118
, Ch. 4]
,
[
155
, Ch. I]
,
[
156
, Ch. 10]
.

III.1
Tomita-Takesaki Modular Theory

In this section, the modular theory of von Neumann algebras, which was developed first by
M. Tomita
in two unpublished notes in 1967 and then presented in a didactically enriched and mathematically extended version by
M. Takesaki
in 1970
[
158
]
, will be outlined.

III.1.a
\sigma
-finite von Neumann Algebras

For applications to mathematical physics, the following type of von Neumann algebras turns out to be particularly relevant
[
29
, p. 84]
.

Definition III.1.1
(
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra).

A von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is said to be
\sigma
-finite
iff any family
(P_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})\setminus\{0\}
of mutually orthogonal non-zero projections in
\mathfrak{M}
(that is,
P_{i}P_{j}=0
for all
i\neq j
) is at most countable.

\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebras are characterized by the following theorem
[
29
, Prop. 2.5.6]
.

Theorem III.1.2
(Characterization of
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebras).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra. The following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{M}
is
\sigma
-finite;

(b)

there exists a countable subset which is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
;

(c)

there exists a faithful normal state
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
;

(d)

\mathfrak{M}
is isomorphic to a von Neumann algebra which contains a cyclic and separating vector.

Example III.1.3
.

Consider the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
over a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Every normal state
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
is of the form
\omega=\tr(\rho\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
for some
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
(
Theorem

II.4.7
). Furthermore, in
Examples

II.4.3

(d)
it was mentioned that
\omega
is faithful if and only if
\rho
is invertible. One can proceed to show that such a state exists,
i.e.
, that
\mathfrak{M}
is
\sigma
-finite, if and only if
\mathcal{H}
is separable
[
29
, Exa. 2.5.5]
.

III.1.b  The Tomita Operator

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra. According to
Theorem

III.1.2
, there exists a cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
, and thus the mapping
\mathfrak{M}\owns A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
is a linear bijection. One may use it to transfer the algebra involution
\mathfrak{M}\owns A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{\ast}\in\mathfrak{M}
, which is an isometry, to an operation
A\varOmega\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{\ast}\varOmega
on
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega
which is, in general, not an isometry. The analysis of this mapping is the starting point of Tomita-Takesaki modular theory.

III.1.4
Construction of the Tomita operator.

(
[
83
, pp. 13 f.]
)
Consider two anti-linear operators (
cf.

B.2.4
)
S_{0}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(S_{0})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
F_{0}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(F_{0})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
with domains
\dom(S_{0})\coloneqq\mathfrak{M}\varOmega
and
\dom(F_{0})\coloneqq\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varOmega
; note that by assumption on
\varOmega
(see also
Proposition

II.3.16
), both
S_{0}
and
F_{0}
are densely defined operators. For every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
and every
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
, define their action by

S_{0}A\varOmega\coloneqq A^{\ast}\varOmega\quad\text{and}\quad F_{0}A^{\prime}\varOmega\coloneqq(A^{\prime})^{\ast}\varOmega\ .

(III.1)

Statement
.

S_{0}
and
F_{0}
are closable anti-linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof
.

From the definition (
III.1
) and the properties of the Hilbert-space adjoint, anti-linearity immediately follows. Let
A\in\mathfrak{M}
and
B^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
be arbitrary. One computes

\displaystyle\langle{A\varOmega,F_{0}B^{\prime}\varOmega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{A\varOmega,(B^{\prime})^{\ast}\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{B^{\prime}A\varOmega,\varOmega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{AB^{\prime}\varOmega,\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{B^{\prime}\varOmega,A^{\ast}\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{B^{\prime}\varOmega,S_{0}A\varOmega}\rangle\ .

By the definition of the adjoint of an anti-linear operator (
B.2.4
), this shows that
A\varOmega\in\dom(F_{0}^{\ast})
and
F_{0}^{\ast}A\varOmega=S_{0}A\varOmega
; since
A\in\mathfrak{M}
was arbitrary, this is to say that
S_{0}\subseteq F_{0}^{\ast}
. As the adjoint is always a closed operator (
Proposition

B.2.5

(a)
), it follows that
S_{0}
is closable. By reading the above chain of equations backwards, one sees that
F_{0}\subseteq S_{0}^{\ast}
, hence
F_{0}
is closable as well.

Consider the anti-linear operators

S\coloneqq\overline{S_{0}}=S_{0}^{\ast\ast}\quad\text{and}\quad F\coloneqq S^{\ast}=S_{0}^{\ast}\ ,

(III.2)

where the bar denotes the closure of the operator
S_{0}
, see
B.2.2
. (Recall also
Proposition

B.2.5

(d)
for the second identities in both definitions.) The operator
S
is called the
Tomita operator
of the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
with respect to the cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega
, and it is also denoted by
S_{\varOmega}
or
S_{\omega}
to emphasize the dependence on
\varOmega
or
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}
.

Lemma III.1.5
.

The operator
S
and its adjoint
S^{\ast}
enjoy the following properties:

(a)

S
is invertible with
S^{-1}=S
.

(b)

The ranges of
S
and
S^{\ast}
lie dense in
\mathcal{H}

Proof
.

Ad

(a)
. By definition, it holds that
\ran(S_{0})=\dom(S_{0})
and
S_{0}^{2}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Therefore,
S_{0}
is invertible with
S_{0}^{-1}=S_{0}
. From
Proposition

B.2.5

(f)
, it follows that
S_{0}^{-1}
is closable with

S^{-1}=(\overline{S_{0}})^{-1}=\overline{(S_{0}^{-1})}=S\ .

Ad

(b)
. First, it will be shown that
\dom(S_{0})\subseteq\ran(S)
. To this end, let
\xi\in\dom(S_{0})
be arbitrary,
i.e.
,
\xi=A\varOmega
for some
A\in\mathfrak{M}
. Since
S
is an extension of
S_{0}
, it follows that
\dom(S_{0})\subseteq\dom(S)
and
SB\varOmega=S_{0}B\varOmega=B^{\ast}\varOmega
for all
B\in\mathfrak{M}
. Thus, since
\eta=A^{\ast}\varOmega\in\dom(S_{0})
, one obtains
S\eta=S_{0}\eta=A\varOmega=\xi
, hence
\xi\in\ran(S)
.

To see that the range of the adjoint
S^{\ast}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
as well, first note that since
S_{0}
is closable, it follows from
Proposition

B.2.5

(c)
that
\dom(S_{0}^{\ast})=\dom(S^{\ast})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. Hence, one can use
(b)
of the same proposition and the definition of
S
to conclude that
\ran(S^{\ast})^{\perp}=\ker(S^{\ast\ast})=\ker{S}=\{{0}\}
by assertion
(a)
proved above. From
Corollary

B.1.10

(b)
, the claim now follows.

III.1.6
Polar decomposition of the Tomita operator.

One can compute the polar decomposition of the closed operator
S
(
cf.

Theorem

B.2.6
) to obtain a unique partial isometry
J:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
and a unique positive self-adjoint linear operator
\varDelta:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(\varDelta)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that

S=J\varDelta^{1/2}\quad\text{with}\quad\varDelta\coloneqq S^{\ast}S=FS\ .

(III.3)

Statement
.

J
is an anti-linear unitary operator, and
\varDelta
is invertible.

Proof
.

The operator
J
is anti-linear and maps its initial space
\ker(S)^{\perp}=[\ran(S^{\ast})]
isometrically onto its final space
\ker(S^{\ast})^{\perp}=[\ran(S)]
, and it satisfies
J\equiv 0
on
\ker(S)^{\perp\perp}=[\ker(S)]
. From
Lemma

III.1.5
, it follows that both initial and final space of
J
are given by
\mathcal{H}
, and that
\ker(S)=\{{0}\}
. Therefore,
J
maps
\mathcal{H}
isometrically to
\mathcal{H}
and is thus a unitary operator
[
45
, p. 20]
. To see that
\varDelta=S^{\ast}S
is invertible, note that from
Proposition

B.2.5

(e)
, it follows that
S^{\ast}
is invertible with
(S^{\ast})^{-1}=(S^{-1})^{\ast}
. Hence,
\varDelta
is invertible with
\varDelta^{-1}=S^{-1}(S^{\ast})^{-1}=S^{-1}(S^{-1})^{\ast}=SS^{\ast}
.

III.1.c  Modular Data and Tomita’s Theorem

The observations made in the previous
III.1.6
motivate the following definition.

Definition III.1.7
(Modular data).

The operator
J
is called the
modular conjugation
with respect to the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and the cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
, and the operator
\varDelta
is called the
modular operator
with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega)
. To emphasize the dependence on the vector
\varOmega
or the functional
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}
, one also writes
J_{\varOmega},J_{\omega}
and
\varDelta_{\varOmega},\varDelta_{\omega}
.

The next proposition contains some of the most important properties of the modular data; they will be used very often in the following discussions (sometimes implicitly). The proof is an elaborated version of
[
83
, Lem. 2.1]
with some insights from
[
156
, p. 278]
.

Proposition III.1.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra with cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
, and let
(J,\varDelta)
denote the modular data with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega)
.

(a)

J^{\ast}=J
and
J^{2}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

(b)

\varDelta=FS
and
\varDelta^{-1}=SF
.

(c)

S=J\varDelta^{1/2}=\varDelta^{-1/2}J
and
F=J\varDelta^{-1/2}=\varDelta^{1/2}J
.

(d)

\varDelta^{-1}=J\varDelta J
and
J\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}=\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}J
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(e)

J\varOmega=\varOmega
and
\varDelta\varOmega=\varOmega
.

(f)

If
f:[0,+\infty)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is Borel-measurable, then
f(\varDelta)\varOmega=f(1)\varOmega
.

Proof
.

Since
S=S^{-1}
by
Lemma

III.1.5

(a)
and
J^{-1}=J^{\ast}
by
III.1.6
, it follows that

S=J\varDelta^{1/2}=\bigl(J\varDelta^{1/2}\bigr)^{-1}=\varDelta^{-1/2}J^{-1}=\varDelta^{-1/2}J^{\ast}=J^{\ast}J\varDelta^{-1/2}J^{\ast}\ .

From this relation, uniqueness of the polar decomposition, and the fact that
J^{\ast}
is a partial isometry as well
[
144
, p. 162]
, one can conclude that
J=J^{\ast}
and
\varDelta^{1/2}=J\varDelta^{-1/2}J^{\ast}
. The first identity (together with
J^{\ast}=J^{-1}
) establishes
(a)
, and from the second, one can conclude the first part of
(d)
: using that
\varDelta^{1/2}=J\varDelta^{-1/2}J
and
J^{2}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, one computes

J\varDelta J=J(J\varDelta^{-1/2}J)(J\varDelta^{-1/2}J)J=\varDelta^{-1}\ .

Let
f:[0,+\infty)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be any Borel-measurable function. From the previous identity and
Lemma

B.3.5
(together with the observation
J(\lambda\varDelta)J=\overline{\lambda}J\varDelta J=\overline{\lambda}\varDelta^{-1}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
), it follows that

\overline{f}(\varDelta^{-1})=Jf(\varDelta)J\ .

(III.4)

In particular, using this relation with
f(t)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t}
, one obtains
J\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}=\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}J
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
which completes the argument to establish
(d)
.

Next, assertion
(b)
was already established in
III.1.6
by noting that
F=S^{\ast}
,
cf.

Eq.

III.2
. Similarly,
(c)
is an easy consequence of
Eqs.

III.3
and
III.4
:
S=J\varDelta^{1/2}=J\varDelta^{1/2}J^{2}=\varDelta^{-1/2}J
and
F=S^{\ast}=\varDelta^{1/2}J=J^{2}\varDelta^{1/2}J=J\varDelta^{-1/2}
. For assertion
(e)
, first note that
S\varOmega=F\varOmega=\varOmega
by (
III.1
), hence
\varDelta\varOmega=FS\varOmega=\varOmega
by
(b)
. With this observation,
(f)
follows from
Lemma

B.3.6
. Finally, using this and
(c)
, one obtains also the first relation in
(e)
:
J\varOmega=\varDelta^{1/2}S\varOmega=\varDelta^{1/2}\varOmega=\varOmega
.

The following theorem is the fundamental result of the modular theory in von Neumann algebras. The proof is very technical and involved, and thus cannot be reproduced here. For detailed arguments, see
[
29
, 91 – 95]
,
[
83
, 15 – 20]
,
[
156
, 278 – 283]
,
[
158
, 54 – 56]
, or
[
160
, 13 – 17]
.

Theorem III.1.9
(Tomita).

Let
J
and
\varDelta
be the modular data of a
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
with cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
. Then the following identities hold true.

(a)

J\mathfrak{M}J=\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.

(b)

\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}\mathfrak{M}\varDelta^{-\mathrm{i}t}=\mathfrak{M}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition III.1.10
(Modular group).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra with a faithful normal state
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
, and let
\varDelta
be the modular operator associated with
(\mathfrak{M},\omega)
. The one-parameter group
\mathbb{R}\owns t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\sigma_{t}^{\omega}\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
of
\ast
-automorphisms of
\mathfrak{M}
, defined by

\sigma_{t}^{\omega}(A)\coloneqq\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}A\varDelta^{-\mathrm{i}t}

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is called the
modular (automorphism) group
of
(\mathfrak{M},\omega)
.

Examples III.1.11
.

(a)

(
[
83
, Exa. 2.4]
) Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra possessing a faithful normal finite
trace

\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, that is, a functional satisfying
\omega(AB)=\omega(BA)
for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}
. Assume that
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}
for a cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
. For all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, it follows that

\|A\varOmega\|^{2}=\langle{\varOmega,A^{\ast}A\varOmega}\rangle=\omega(A^{\ast}A)=\omega(AA^{\ast})=\langle{\varOmega,AA^{\ast}\varOmega}\rangle=\|A^{\ast}\varOmega\|^{2}=\|SA\varOmega\|^{2}\ .

This shows that the Tomita operator
S
with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\omega)
is an isometry, hence also anti-unitary by
Lemma

III.1.5

(a)
. Therefore, the modular data is given by

J=S\quad\text{and}\quad\varDelta=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\ .

Note that if
\mathfrak{M}
were a commutative von Neumann algebra and
\omega
an arbitrary faithful normal state, the same result would follow because
\omega
is automatically tracial. This discussion suggests that in a sense, the modular operator
\varDelta
measures the
non-tracial character
of the state
\omega
on the algebra
\mathfrak{M}

[
29
, p. 90]
. In particular, Tomita-Takesaki modular theory is trivial in the commutative case. (There has, however, been a suggestion of a
“commutative version”
of Tomita’s theorem in the context of classical statistical mechanics
[
69
]
.)

(b)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. By
Example

III.1.3
, there exists a faithful normal state
\omega
on
\mathfrak{A}
which is represented by an invertible density matrix
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
in the form
\omega(A)=\tr(\rho A)
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
. Furthermore, recall from
Examples

II.4.23

(b)
that the GNS-representation space of
\mathfrak{M}
is given by
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}=\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, with Hilbert-Schmidt inner product
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}
, on which
\mathfrak{M}
acts by multiplication, the GNS-vector representative of
\omega
is
\varOmega=\rho^{1/2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, and
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}=\langle{\varOmega,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \varOmega}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}
. In this case, the modular operator is given by
[
90
, Eq. (2.6)]
:

\varDelta X=\rho X\rho^{-1}\quad(X\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\ .

(III.5)

To see this, note that
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
by the GNS-construction, hence it suffices to check the identity on this subspace. Let
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\langle{B\varOmega,\varDelta A\varOmega}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\langle{B\rho^{1/2},\rho A\rho^{-1/2}}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\tr\bigl((B\rho^{1/2})^{\ast}\rho A\rho^{-1/2}\bigr)=\tr(\rho AB^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\omega(AB^{\ast})=\langle{A^{\ast}\varOmega,B^{\ast}\varOmega}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\langle{SA\varOmega,SB\varOmega}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\langle{B\varOmega,S^{\ast}SA\varOmega}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\ .

In the second to last step, the Tomita operator (
III.1
) was introduced, and in the last step, the definition of the adjoint of an anti-linear operator was used (
cf.

B.2.4
). It follows that
\varDelta
, as given in (
III.5
), agrees with the operator
S^{\ast}S
, hence, by uniqueness of the polar decomposition of the Tomita operator,
\varDelta
must be the modular operator.

III.2
Standard Form Representation

For the discussion of the relative modular operator in the next
Section

III.3
as well as the development of perturbation theory in
Chapter

V
, the theory of the standard form representation of von Neumann algebras is an indispensible tool. It was developed, independently, by
H. Araki

[
8
]
and
A. Connes

[
41
]
for
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebras in 1974, and generalized by
U. Haagerup

[
76
]
in 1975.

III.2.a  Definition and Examples

III.2.1
Natural positive cone.

(
[
83
, p. 25]
)
Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra with cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
. Denote by
\varDelta
and
J
the modular data with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega)
, and let
j:\mathfrak{M}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
be the anti-linear
\ast
-isomorphism defined by

j(A)\coloneqq JAJ\quad(A\in\mathfrak{M})\ .

(
Theorem

III.1.9

(a)
shows that the range of
j
is indeed the commutant
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.) The
natural positive cone

\mathcal{P}
in
\mathcal{H}
associated with
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega)
is defined to be

\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\!\big\{{Aj(A)\varOmega\ :\ A\in\mathfrak{M}}\big\}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\!\big\{{AJA\varOmega\ :\ A\in\mathfrak{M}}\big\}\ .

(III.6)

The set
\mathcal{P}
displays a number of very useful intrinsic properties as well as relations to the modular data. The proof of the next proposition can be found,
e.g.
, in
[
29
, Prop. 2.5.26]
or
[
83
, Thm. 3.2]
.

Proposition III.2.2
.

The natural positive cone
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
enjoys the following properties:

(a)

\mathcal{P}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\varDelta^{1/4}\mathfrak{M}^{+}\varOmega)
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}
is a closed convex cone inside
\mathcal{H}
.

(b)

J\xi=\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{P}
.

(c)

\varDelta^{\mathrm{i}t}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(d)

Aj(A)\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(e)

If
f:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow(0,+\infty)
is a positive-definite function on
\mathbb{R}
, then
f(\log\varDelta)\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

(f)

\mathcal{P}
is self-dual in the sense that
\mathcal{P}=\{{\eta\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,\text{$\langle{\xi,\eta}\rangle\geq 0$ for all $\xi\in\mathcal{P}$}}\}
.

Definition III.2.3
(Standard form).

A quadruple
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
consisting of a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
represented faithfully on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, an anti-unitary involution
J:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
, and a self-dual closed convex cone
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is called a
standard form
of the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
iff the following properties are satisfied:

(a)

J\mathfrak{M}J=\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
;

(b)

JAJ=A^{\ast}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
;

(c)

J\xi=\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{P}
;

(d)

Aj(A)\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
.

III.2.1
and
Proposition

III.2.2
show that every
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra can be represented in standard form. Regarding non-
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebras,
U. Haagerup
proved the following theorem in 1975
[
76
, Thm. 1.6]
. (See also
[
156
, Sect. 10.14 & 10.23]
.)

Theorem III.2.4
(Haagerup).

Every von Neumann algebra possesses a standard form representation.

Examples III.2.5
.

(a)

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space. The von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
acts on
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(X,\mu)
by multiplication (
Examples

II.3.11

(a)
), and this action is faithful. Moreover, by assumption there exists a measurable function
g:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that
g>0
and
\int_{X}g\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu<+\infty

[
94
, p. 21]
. This gives rise to the faithful normal functional
\omega(f)\coloneqq\int_{X}fg\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
on
\mathfrak{M}
(
cf.

Examples

II.4.8

(a)
) with cyclic and separating vector representative
h\coloneqq\sqrt{g}\in\mathcal{H}
.

In
Examples

III.1.11

(a)
, it was shown that
J=S
is given by the Tomita operator. Furthermore, it holds that
j(f)=JfJ
implements the
\ast
-operation on
\mathfrak{M}
: for all
f,g\in\mathfrak{M}
, one has

JfJ(gh)=J(fg^{\ast}h)=gf^{\ast}h=\overline{f}(gh)\ .

Therefore, the natural positive cone (
III.6
) is given by
\mathcal{P}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\{{|f|^{2}h\,:\,f\in\mathfrak{M}}\}=L^{2}(X,\mu)_{+}
, and hence the standard form representation of
\mathfrak{M}
is
[
83
, Exa. 3.6 (1)]

\bigl(L^{\infty}(X,\mu),\,L^{2}(X,\mu),\,J\xi=\overline{\xi},\,L^{2}(X,\mu)_{+}\bigr)\ .

(b)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. This algebra acts faithfully on
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
by multiplication, and one can show that the standard form representation of
\mathfrak{M}
is given by
[
83
, Exa. 3.6 (2)]

\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),JA=A^{\ast},\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{+}\bigr)\ ,

where
J:\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A^{\ast}
, is given by the operation of taking adjoints, and
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{+}
is the cone of positive Hilbert-Schmidt operators.

III.2.b  Properties of the Standard Form Representation

The following proposition, proved in
[
29
, Prop. 2.5.28]
or
[
83
, Prop. 3.7]
, concerns further geometric properties of the cone
\mathcal{P}
. Note the close analogy between the following result and
Proposition

II.1.7
which characterized the cone
\mathfrak{A}^{+}
of positive elements in a
C^{\ast}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.

Proposition III.2.6
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form. The closed convex cone
\mathcal{P}
satisfies
\mathcal{P}\cap(-\mathcal{P})=\{0\}
. Furthermore, if
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
such that
J\xi=\xi
, then
\xi
can be uniquely decomposed as
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}
, where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}
with
\xi_{1}\perp\xi_{2}
. Finally,
\mathcal{H}
is linearly spanned by
\mathcal{P}
.

One can show that the data
(J,\mathcal{P})
does not depend on the choice of a cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
, hence the objects
J
and
\mathcal{P}
are
universal

[
29
, Prop. 2.5.30]
,
[
83
, Prop. 3.10]
. Furthermore, the elements of
\mathcal{P}
enjoy some special properties.

Lemma III.2.7
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form. For any
\xi\in\mathcal{P}
, it holds that
\xi
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
\xi
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
.

Proof
.

(
[
83
, p. 30]
)
Let
\xi\in\mathcal{P}
be arbitrary. If
\xi
is assumed to be cyclic for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
, then
\xi=J\xi
is cyclic for
J\mathfrak{M}J=\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
, hence it is also separating for
\mathfrak{M}
by
Proposition

II.3.16

(b)
. The other direction is proved analogously.

The following theorem, which is the main result of this section, establishes a relationship between the natural positive cone of a von Neumann algebra and the space of normal functionals. The proof can be found in
[
29
, Thm. 2.5.31]
or
[
83
, Thm. 3.12]
.

Theorem III.2.8
(Correspondence between
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}
).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form. For every
\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, there exists a unique vector
\xi\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\varphi=\omega_{\xi}
. In fact, the mapping
\mathcal{P}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
,
\xi\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\omega_{\xi}
, is a homeomorphism with respect to the norm topology on both spaces.

Remark III.2.9
.

The inverse of the mapping
\mathcal{P}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
of the previous theorem will be denoted by
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{P}
,
\varphi\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\xi_{\varphi}
, and
\xi_{\varphi}
will be called the
standard vector representative
of
\varphi
. Combining
Corollary

II.4.14
and
Lemma

III.2.7
, it follows that

\forall\xi\in\mathcal{P}\quad:\quad\xi\ \text{cyclic}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \xi\ \text{separating}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \omega_{\xi}\ \text{faithful}\ .

An important consequence of
Theorem

III.2.8
is that all
\ast
-automorphisms of the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
are implemented by unitary elements from
\mathcal{H}
. This will be a key technical tool for the investigations in
Chapter

V
. The proof can be found in
[
29
, Cor. 2.5.32]
and
[
131
, Thm. 4.43]
.

Corollary III.2.10
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form. Then there exists a unique unitary representation
\Aut(\mathfrak{M})\owns\alpha\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow U(\alpha)\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
of the group
\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
of
\ast
-automorphisms of the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that

(a)

U(\alpha)\mathfrak{M}U(\alpha)^{\ast}=\mathfrak{M}
and
U(\alpha)\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}U(\alpha)^{\ast}=\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
;

(b)

U(\alpha)\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
;

(c)

\forall A\in\mathfrak{M}\,:\,\alpha(A)=U(\alpha)AU(\alpha)^{\ast}
;

(d)

\forall\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}\,:\,U(\alpha)^{\ast}\xi_{\varphi}=\xi_{\varphi\circ\alpha}
;

(e)

[U(\alpha),J]=0
.

Furthermore, the mapping
\Aut(\mathfrak{M})\owns\alpha\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow U(\alpha)\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
is a homeomorphism when both spaces are equipped with the norm topology.

The final result of this section establishes uniqueness of the standard form representation up to unitary equivalence
[
83
, Thm. 3.13]
.

Corollary III.2.11
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
and
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}},\widetilde{\mathcal{H}},\widetilde{J},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})
be two standard forms of von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}
, respectively. If
\varPhi:\mathfrak{M}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}
is a
\ast
-isomorphism, then there exists a uniquely defined unitary operator
U:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
such that

(1)

\forall A\in\mathfrak{M}\,:\,\varPhi(A)=UAU^{\ast}
;

(2)

\widetilde{J}=UJU^{\ast}
;

(3)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}=U\mathcal{P}
.

III.3
Relative Modular Operators

The construction of the modular operator with respect to a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and a cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
can be generalized to the situation in which two (not necessarily faithful) positive normal functionals
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
are given. The resulting object can be seen as a
non-commutative Radon-Nikodým derivative
of these functionals
[
83
, p. 163]
; it was invented by
H. Araki

[
10
,
11
]
who used it to extend the notion of relative entropy to general von Neumann algebras.

III.3.a  Construction

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form and
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
. According to
Theorem

III.2.8
, there exist vectors
\varPhi,\varPsi\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\varphi=\omega_{\varPhi}
and
\psi=\omega_{\varPsi}
. Recall from
Proposition

II.4.13
that the support projections of the functionals
\omega_{\varPhi}
on
\mathfrak{M}
and
\omega_{\varPhi}^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
are given by
[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi]
and
[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]
, respectively. In this and the subsequent chapters, the following notations shall be used:

\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)\equiv\mathbf{s}_{\varphi}\equiv\mathbf{s}_{\varPhi}\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)\equiv\mathbf{s}_{\varphi}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{s}_{\varPhi}^{\prime}\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]\ .

(III.7)

Lemma III.3.1
.

J\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)J=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)
.

Proof
.

(
[
83
, p. 163]
)
Since the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is linearly spanned by the natural positive cone
\mathcal{P}
(
Proposition

III.2.6
) and since
J\xi=\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{P}
(
Definition

III.2.3
), it follows that
J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)J\mathcal{H}=J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)\mathcal{H}
. Using that
J\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}J=\mathfrak{M}
one obtains

\displaystyle J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)J\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)\mathcal{H}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(J\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi)

\displaystyle=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(J\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}J\varPhi)=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathfrak{M}\varPhi)=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)\mathcal{H}\ .

III.3.2
The relative Tomita operator.

(
[
83
, p. 163]
)
For every pair of positive normal functionals
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, define two operators
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
by

S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}(A\varPhi+\eta)\coloneqq\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)A^{\ast}\varPsi\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}(A^{\prime}\varPhi+\zeta)\coloneqq\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)(A^{\prime})^{\ast}\varPsi

(III.8)

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
,
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
,
\eta\in(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\varphi}^{\prime})\mathcal{H}
and
\zeta\in(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\varphi})\mathcal{H}
. Since
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\varphi}^{\prime}
is the orthogonal projection onto
[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}
and
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\varphi}
onto
[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi]^{\perp}
(
Theorem

B.1.8
), it follows that

\dom(S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})=\mathfrak{M}\varPhi+[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}\quad\text{and}\quad\dom(F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})=\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi+[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi]^{\perp}\ .

Statement
.

The operators
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
and
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
are well-defined anti-linear operators. Moreover, their domains lie dense in
\mathcal{H}
, and they are closable.

Proof
.

(
[
83
, Lem. 10.2]
)

1. The operators are well-defined
. Let
A_{i}\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\eta_{i}\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}
,
i\in\{{1,2}\}
, be arbitrary such that
A_{1}\varPhi+\eta_{1}=A_{2}\varPhi+\eta_{2}
. Observe that
(A_{1}-A_{2})\varPhi=\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]\cap[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}=\{{0}\}
, hence
A_{1}\varPhi=A_{2}\varPhi
. By definition of
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)
, this implies
A_{1}\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)=A_{2}\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)
on
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, one may conclude that
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)A_{1}^{\ast}\varPsi=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)A_{2}^{\ast}\varPsi
. Thus,

S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}(A_{1}\varPhi+\eta_{1})=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)A_{1}^{\ast}\varPsi=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)A_{2}^{\ast}\varPsi=S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}(A_{2}\varPhi+\eta_{2})

which shows that the operator
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
is well-defined; an analogous argument establishes the same for the operator
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
.

2. The domains lie dense
. It holds that
\mathcal{H}\supseteq\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\dom(S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathfrak{M}\varPhi+[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp})\supseteq\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathfrak{M}\varPhi)+\clos_{\|\cdot\|}([\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp})=[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]+[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}
(
cf.

Theorem

B.1.8
and note that the orthogonal complement is always closed). This shows that
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
is densely defined; for the operator
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
defined on
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi+[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi]^{\perp}
, the argument is analogous. The anti-linearity of these operators is clear from their definition.

3. Closability
. Let
A\in\mathfrak{M}
,
\eta\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}
and
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
,
\zeta\in[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\varPhi]^{\perp}
be arbitrary. One computes

\displaystyle\big\langle{A\varPhi+\eta,F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}(A^{\prime}\varPhi+\zeta)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{A\varPhi+\eta,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)(A^{\prime})^{\ast}\varPsi}\big\rangle=\big\langle{A\varPhi,(A^{\prime})^{\ast}\varPsi}\big\rangle=\big\langle{A^{\prime}\varPhi,A^{\ast}\varPsi}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)(A^{\prime}\varPhi+\zeta),A^{\ast}\varPsi}\big\rangle=\big\langle{A^{\prime}\varPhi+\zeta,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)A^{\ast}\varPsi}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{A^{\prime}\varPhi+\zeta,S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}(A\varPhi+\eta)}\big\rangle\ .

From the definition of the adjoint of an anti-linear operator (
B.2.4
), it follows that
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}\subseteq(F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})^{\ast}
and
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}\subseteq(S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0})^{\ast}
, hence
Proposition

B.2.5
shows that both
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
and
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
are closable.

Let
S_{\psi,\varphi}
and
F_{\psi,\varphi}
denote the closures of the operators
S_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
and
F_{\psi,\varphi}^{0}
, respectively. One refers to
S_{\psi,\varphi}
as the
relative Tomita operator
associated with the functionals
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
. If
\psi=\varphi
, one simply writes
S_{\varphi}
and
F_{\varphi}
for these operators. Note that if
\psi=\varphi
is faithful, then
S_{\varphi}
agrees with the Tomita operator defined in
III.1.4
: indeed, the vector representative
\varPhi
of
\varphi
is cyclic and separating (
Remark

III.2.9
), hence
[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]=\mathcal{H}
and
[\mathfrak{M}\varPhi]^{\perp}=\{0\}
so that
S_{\varphi}^{0}A\varPhi=A^{\ast}\varPhi=SA\varPhi
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
.

Definition III.3.3
(Relative modular operator).

The
relative modular operator

\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}
with respect to the positive normal functionals
\psi
and
\varphi
on
\mathfrak{M}
is defined to be

\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}\coloneqq S_{\psi,\varphi}^{\ast}S_{\psi,\varphi}\ .

(III.9)

In the case that
\psi=\varphi
, one simply writes
\varDelta_{\varphi}
for
\varDelta_{\varphi,\varphi}
and calls this the
modular operator
of
\varphi
. (Note that by the above remark, it holds that if
\varphi
is faithful, then
\varDelta_{\varphi}
agrees with the operator
\varDelta
from
Definition

III.1.7
.) One also writes

\varDelta_{\varPsi,\varPhi}\equiv\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}\quad\text{and}\quad S_{\varPsi,\varPhi}\equiv S_{\psi,\phi}\ .

The following examples
[
83
, Exa. 10.4]
shed some light on the abstract concept of the relative modular operator. Note, in particular, that
(a)
shows that this concept is non-trivial for commutative von Neumann algebras which is in contrast to the situation for the modular operator found in
Examples

III.1.11

(a)
.

Examples III.3.4
.

(a)

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space and
\mathfrak{M}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, acting on
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(X,\mu)
. Recall from
Examples

II.4.8

(a)
that there is a one-to-one correspondence
\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}\cong L^{1}(X,\mu)_{+}
; in particular, every
\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
gives rise to a measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\psi}=h_{\psi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
. For every pair of normal functionals
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, it holds that the relative modular operator
\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}
is given by the multiplication operator with the function

\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\cdot\frac{h_{\psi}}{h_{\varphi}}\ .

To see this, let
f,g\in\mathfrak{M}
be arbitrary and note that the vector representative of
\varphi
in the natural positive cone is
\varPhi\coloneqq\sqrt{h_{\varphi}}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)_{+}
. The support projection of
\varphi
is given by
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)=\mathbf{1}_{\{{\varphi>0}\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}
. Indeed, if there were
P\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})
such that
\varphi(P)=\varphi(\mathbf{1}_{X})
and
P<\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)
, then

\int_{X}h_{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\varphi(\mathbf{1}_{X})=\varphi(P)=\int_{X}Ph_{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu<\int_{X}\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}h_{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{X}h_{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\ ,

a contradiction. Since
\mathfrak{M}
is commutative, it holds that
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)=\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}
as well. Let
\eta,\zeta\in(\mathbf{1}_{X}-\mathbf{s}_{\varphi}^{\prime})\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary, and observe that
\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\eta=\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\zeta=0
. One then computes

\displaystyle\big\langle{f\,\varPhi+\eta,\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}(g\,\varPhi+\zeta)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{X}\overline{(f\,\varPhi+\eta)}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\,\frac{h_{\psi}}{h_{\varphi}}\,(g\,\varPhi+\zeta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int\nolimits_{X}\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\overline{f}gh_{\psi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=\big\langle{\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\overline{g}\,\varPsi,\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\overline{f}\,\varPsi}\big\rangle=\big\langle{S_{\psi,\varphi}(g\,\varPhi+\zeta),S_{\psi,\varphi}(f\,\varPhi+\eta)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{f\,\varPhi+\eta,S_{\psi,\varphi}^{\ast}S_{\psi,\varphi}(g\,\varPhi+\zeta)}\big\rangle\ ,

where
\varPsi\coloneqq\sqrt{h_{\psi}}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)_{+}
is the vector representative of
\psi
. From the uniqueness of the polar decomposition of the relative Tomita operator, the claim now follows.

It holds that
\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}=\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\,\frac{h_{\psi}}{h_{\varphi}}
is equal to the Radon-Nikodým derivative
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\psi}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\nu_{\varphi}}}
of the measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\psi}=h_{\psi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
with respect to the measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\varphi}=h_{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
. (This is the reason for calling the relative modular operator a non-commutative Radon-Nikodým derivative.) Indeed, for all
A\in\varSigma
, one easily computes that

\nu_{\psi}(A)=\int_{A}h_{\psi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{A}\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\,\frac{h_{\psi}}{h_{\varphi}}\,h_{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{A}\mathbf{1}_{\{{h_{\varphi}>0}\}}\,\frac{h_{\psi}}{h_{\varphi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\varphi}\ .

(b)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. Consider the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
which is represented in standard form on the Hilbert space
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
(
Examples

III.2.5

(b)
). For every
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, there exist positive
\rho_{\varphi},\rho_{\psi}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\varphi=\tr(\rho_{\varphi}\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
and
\psi=\tr(\rho_{\psi}\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
. Let
\rho_{\psi}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{i}P_{i}
and
\rho_{\varphi}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{j}Q_{j}
be the spectral decompositions of
\rho_{\varphi}
and
\rho_{\psi}
, where
\lambda_{i},\mu_{j}>0
and
P_{i},Q_{j}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
are finite-rank projections for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then one shows, similarly to the previous example and
Examples

III.1.11

(b)
that the relative modular operator
\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}
on
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
is given by
1
1

1

The operator
\rho_{\varphi}^{-1}
is defined with restriction to the support
\supp(\rho_{\varphi})
of
\rho_{\varphi}
,
i.e.
, it is the generalized inverse of
\rho_{\varphi}
; see
[
83
, p. 167]
,
[
84
, p. 164]
,
[
19
, Sect. 9.3]
.

\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}X=\rho_{\psi}X\rho_{\varphi}^{-1}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}\mu_{j}^{-1}P_{i}XQ_{j}\ .

III.3.b  Properties of Relative Modular Operators

The most important properties of the relative modular operator are contained in the next proposition whose proof shall be skipped as it is quite involved; see
[
83
, Prop. 10.3]
.

Proposition III.3.5
.

Let
\psi,\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
be two positive normal functionals on a von Neumann algebra
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
in standard form.

(a)

The support projection (cf.
B.1.13
) of the relative modular operator
\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}
is

\mathbf{s}(\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi})=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\varphi)=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varphi)J\ .

(b)

The polar decomposition of
S_{\psi,\varphi}
takes the form
S_{\psi,\varphi}=J\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}^{1/2}
.

(c)

The inverse operator
\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}^{-1}
, defined on the support of
\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}
, is given by
\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}^{-1}=J\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}J
.

The next two lemmata will be useful for arguments in the following chapters. These properties are mentioned, for example, in
[
50
, Thm. 4.1]
. Let again
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form and
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
.

Lemma III.3.6
.

If
B\in\mathfrak{M}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
belongs to the center of
\mathfrak{M}
, then
B
commutes with
\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}
.

Proof
.

Let
A,C\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\eta,\zeta\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\big\langle{C\varPsi+\zeta,\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}B(A\varPsi+\eta)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{S_{\varphi,\psi}B(A\varPsi+\eta),S_{\varphi,\psi}(C\varPsi+\zeta)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)(BA)^{\ast}\varPhi,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)C^{\ast}\varPhi}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)A^{\ast}\varPhi,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)(B^{\ast}C)^{\ast}\varPhi}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{S_{\varphi,\psi}(A\varPsi+\eta),S_{\varphi,\psi}(B^{\ast}C\varPsi+B^{\ast}\zeta)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{C\varPsi+\zeta,B\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}(A\varPsi+\eta)}\big\rangle\ .

First, the definition of the adjoint of an anti-linear operator was employed (
B.2.4
). In the second line, it was used that
\eta\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}=\ker(\mathbf{s}_{\psi}^{\prime})
implies
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\psi)B\eta=B\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\psi)\eta=0
, which shows that
B\eta\in\ker(\mathbf{s}_{\psi}^{\prime})
. Since the space
\mathfrak{M}\varPsi+[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}
, the claim follows from the above identity.

Lemma III.3.7
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in(0,+\infty)
be positive real numbers. Then

\varDelta_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\,\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}\ .

Proof
.

The vector representative in
\mathcal{P}
of the functional
\lambda\psi
, which is defined by
(\lambda\psi)(A)\coloneqq\lambda\psi(A)
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, is given by
\sqrt{\lambda}\,\varPsi
, and similarly the vector representative of
\mu\varphi
is
\sqrt{\mu}\,\varPhi
.

Observe that the support projection of the functional
\lambda\psi
satisfies the identity
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)
. To see this, note that on the one hand,

\psi\bigl(\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)\bigr)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\,(\lambda\psi)\bigl(\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)\bigr)\overset{\eqref{eq:operatorAlgebras_supportProjection}}{=}\frac{1}{\lambda}(\lambda\psi)(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=\psi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\ ,

hence
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)\geq\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)
by definition of the support projection (
II.4.9
). On the other hand,
(\lambda\psi)\bigl(\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)\bigr)=(\lambda\psi)(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
, implying
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)\geq\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)
.

With these two observations, one can now compute the relative Tomita operator for the functionals
\mu\varphi
and
\lambda\psi
as follows: for
A\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\eta\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}
, there holds

\displaystyle S_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}^{0}(A\varPsi+\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\,S_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}\bigl(A\sqrt{\lambda}\,\varPsi+\sqrt{\lambda}\,\eta\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)A^{\ast}\sqrt{\mu}\,\varPhi=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)A^{\ast}\varPhi

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}\,S_{\varphi,\psi}^{0}(A\varPsi+\eta)\ .

Observe that
\sqrt{\lambda}\,\eta\in(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\lambda\psi}^{\prime})\mathcal{H}
was tacitly used which holds true since
\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\lambda\psi)=J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\lambda\psi)J=J\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\psi)J=\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}}(\psi)
according to
Lemma

III.3.1
. The above relation implies
S_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}^{0}=\sqrt{\mu/\lambda}\,S_{\varphi,\psi}^{0}
; since this identity is stable under taking closures, it follows that

\varDelta_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}=S_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}^{\ast}S_{\mu\varphi,\lambda\psi}=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\,S_{\varphi,\psi}^{\ast}S_{\varphi,\psi}=\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\,\varDelta_{\varphi,\psi}\ .

The following result is also standard in modular theory; it can be found, for example, in
[
11
, Rem. 3.3]
, and the proof given below is a more detailed version of the one given in this reference.

Lemma III.3.8
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form, let
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
be positive normal functionals with vector representatives
\varPhi,\varPsi\in\mathcal{P}
, and let
U\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
be a unitary operator such that
UAU^{\ast}=A
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
and
U\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. Then

\varDelta_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}=U\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi}U^{\ast}\ .

Proof
.

Define
\varPhi^{\prime}\coloneqq U\varPhi
and
\varPsi^{\prime}\coloneqq U\varPsi
. By assumption, these are still vectors in the natural positive cone. Let
\psi^{\prime}\coloneqq\omega_{\varPsi^{\prime}}
be the vector functional induced by
\varPsi^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{M}
, and let
\eta^{\prime}\in(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{\psi^{\prime}})\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary. The first assumption on
U
implies that
U\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
; since
U
is also continuous, it follows that
\ran(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{\psi^{\prime}})=[\mathfrak{M}U\varPsi]=U[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]
, and hence that
2
2

2

Let
(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
be a Hilbert space,
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a subset,
U\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
be a unitary operator, and
\xi\in\mathcal{K}
be arbitrary. Then
\eta\in(U\mathcal{K})^{\perp}

\Leftrightarrow

\langle{U\xi,\eta}\rangle=0

\Leftrightarrow

\langle{\xi,U^{\ast}\eta}\rangle=0

\Leftrightarrow

U^{\ast}\eta\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

\Leftrightarrow

\eta\in U\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. Therefore,
(U\mathcal{K})^{\perp}=U\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.

\eta^{\prime}\in U[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}
. Consequently, there is
\eta\in[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}
such that
\eta^{\prime}=U\eta
. Furthermore, observe that for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, there holds

\psi^{\prime}(A)=\langle{U\varPsi,AU\varPsi}\rangle=\langle{\varPsi,U^{\ast}AU\varPsi}\rangle=\psi(A)\ ,

hence
\psi^{\prime}=\psi
on
\mathfrak{M}
and, in particular,
\mathbf{s}(\psi^{\prime})=\mathbf{s}(\psi)
. Thus, one obtains

S_{\varPhi^{\prime},\varPsi^{\prime}}(A\varPsi^{\prime}+\eta^{\prime})=S_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}(AU\varPsi+U\eta)=S_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}U(A\varPsi+\eta)

on the one hand, and

S_{\varPhi^{\prime},\varPsi^{\prime}}(A\varPsi^{\prime}+\eta^{\prime})=\mathbf{s}(\psi^{\prime})A^{\ast}\varPhi^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}(\psi)A^{\ast}U\varPhi=U\mathbf{s}(\psi)A^{\ast}\varPhi=US_{\varPhi,\varPsi}(A\varPsi+\eta)

on the other hand. Combining these two relations, it follows that
S_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}U=US_{\varPhi,\varPsi}
and, consequently,
S_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}=US_{\varPhi,\varPsi}U^{\ast}
on
\dom(S_{\varPhi,\varPsi})
. Therefore, the modular operator
\varDelta_{\varPhi^{\prime},\varPsi^{\prime}}
takes the following form:

\varDelta_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}=S_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}^{\ast}S_{U\varPhi,U\varPsi}=(US_{\varPhi,\varPsi}^{\ast}U^{\ast})(US_{\varPhi,\varPsi}U^{\ast})=US_{\varPhi,\varPsi}^{\ast}S_{\varPhi,\varPsi}U^{\ast}=U\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi}U^{\ast}\ .

This section shall be concluded with a result concerning convergence properties of the relative modular operator which will be used in the perturbation theory of KMS-states in
Chapter

V
. The proof presented below is a more detailed version of
[
50
, Thm. 4.2]
.

Lemma III.3.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a von Neumann algebra represented in standard form
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
, and let
(\varPhi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and
(\varPsi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be two sequences. Assume that the following properties are satisfied:

(a)

\varDelta_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}\rightarrow M
in the strong resolvent sense (
Definition

B.3.8
);

(b)

\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi}
in the strong operator topology;

(c)

\varPhi_{n}\rightarrow\varPhi
weakly in
\mathcal{H}
;

(d)

\varPsi_{n}\rightarrow\varPsi
weakly in
\mathcal{H}
.

Then it follows that
M=\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi}
.

Proof
.

Let
A\in\mathfrak{M}
be arbitrary. From the representation
S_{\varPhi,\varPsi}=J\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi}^{1/2}
of the relative Tomita operator (
Proposition

III.3.5

(b)
) and the definition (
III.8
) of the latter, it follows that

\varDelta_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}^{1/2}A\varPsi_{n}=JS_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}A\varPsi_{n}=J\,\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}A^{\ast}\varPhi_{n}\ .

Furthermore,
A\varPsi_{n}\rightarrow A\varPsi
weakly in
\mathcal{H}
since
\langle{\xi,A\varPsi_{n}}\rangle=\langle{A^{\ast}\xi,\varPsi_{n}}\rangle\rightarrow\langle{A^{\ast}\xi,\varPsi}\rangle=\langle{\xi,A\varPsi}\rangle
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
according to assumption
(d)
; similarly, the assumptions
(b)
and
(c)
together imply that
J\,\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}A^{\ast}\varPhi_{n}\rightarrow J\,\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi}A^{\ast}\varPhi
weakly in
\mathcal{H}
. Finally, it holds that
\varDelta_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}^{1/2}\rightarrow M^{1/2}
in the strong resolvent sense by
Lemma

B.3.14
and assumption
(a)
.

Therefore, applying
Proposition

B.3.12
(with
T_{n}\equiv\varDelta_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}^{1/2}
,
T\equiv M^{1/2}
,
\varOmega_{n}\equiv A\varPsi_{n}
,
\varOmega\equiv A\varPsi
while noting that
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,T_{n}\varOmega_{n}
exists so that
Remark

B.3.13
applies), it follows that
A\varPsi\in\dom(M^{1/2})
and

M^{1/2}A\varPsi=\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\varDelta_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}^{1/2}A\varPsi_{n}=\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,J\,\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}A^{\ast}\varPhi_{n}=J\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi}A^{\ast}\varPhi\ .

(III.10)

Let
\eta\in(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi}^{\prime})\,\mathcal{H}=[\mathfrak{M}\varPsi]^{\perp}
be arbitrary and define
\eta_{n}\coloneqq(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}^{\prime})\,\eta
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It holds that
\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi}^{\prime}
strongly because
\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}^{\prime}=J\,\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}J
by
Lemma

III.3.1
and
\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbf{s}_{\varPsi}
strongly by assumption
(b)
. Therefore, it follows that
\eta_{n}\rightarrow\eta
strongly in
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover, since

\varDelta_{\varPhi_{n},\varPsi_{n}}^{1/2}\eta_{n}=0\quad(n\in\mathbb{N})

according to the construction of the relative Tomita operator (
III.8
), one obtains
\eta\in\dom(M^{1/2})
and
M^{1/2}\eta=0
by applying
Proposition

B.3.12
once more. Together with the above identity (
III.10
) and uniqueness of the polar decomposition of
S_{\varPhi,\varPsi}
, this yields that
M^{1/2}=\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi}^{1/2}
.

III.4
Spatial Derivatives

The relative modular operator
\varDelta_{\psi,\varphi}
generalized the modular operator
\varDelta_{\omega}
of a faithful normal functional
\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, and
Section

III.3
demonstrated that it always acts on the Hilbert space from the standard form representation. In this section, the spatial derivative operator, a generalization of the relative modular operator which was introduced by
A. Connes
in 1980
[
42
]
, will be constructed. It is defined with respect to a
semi-finite normal weight

\varphi
on
\mathfrak{M}
and a
faithful semi-finite normal weight

\psi
on
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. The advantage of the spatial derivative over the relative modular operator is that it is defined in
any representation
space for
\mathfrak{M}
, not just the one from the standard form representation.

III.4.a  Weights

As mentioned, the spatial derivative is defined with respect to a so-called normal weight on the commutant; this is, in a sense, an
“unbounded normal functional”

[
156
, p. 318]
. The definitions and results presented here can be found, for example, in
[
83
, Sect. 6.1 & 11.1]
or
[
155
, Sect. 1 & 7]
.

Definition III.4.1
(Weight).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra. A
weight
on
\mathfrak{M}
is a functional
\varphi:\mathfrak{M}^{+}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty]
satisfying
\varphi(A+B)=\varphi(A)+\varphi(B)
and
\varphi(\lambda A)=\lambda\varphi(A)
for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}^{+}
and
\lambda\geq 0
. Define the following set which is actually a left ideal of
\mathfrak{M}
:

\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}\coloneqq\big\{{A\in\mathfrak{M}\ :\ \varphi(A^{\ast}A)<+\infty}\big\}\ .

Then the weight
\varphi
is called

(a)

faithful
iff
\varphi(A^{\ast}A)=0
implies
A=0
for any
A\in\mathfrak{M}
;

(b)

semi-finite
iff
\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
is
\sigma
-weakly dense in
\mathfrak{M}
;

(c)

normal
iff
\varphi(\sup_{i\in I}A_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\varphi(A_{i})
for any increasing net
(A_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{+}
.

Analogously as for normal functionals, one defines the
support
of a normal weight
\varphi
to be the smallest projection
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})
such that
\varphi\bigl(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)=0

[
155
, p. 10]
. As in the previous case, it holds that
\varphi
is faithful if and only if
\mathbf{s}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Below, the following notation will be used:

\supp(\varphi)\coloneqq\ran\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\varphi)\bigr)\ .

Example III.4.2
.

(
[
83
, Exa. 6.3]
)
Let
\mathfrak{M}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
for a
\sigma
-finite measure space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
, and define
\varphi:\mathfrak{M}^{+}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty]
by
\varphi(f)\coloneqq\int_{X}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
. Then
\varphi
is a faithful semi-finite normal weight on
\mathfrak{M}
with
\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}=\mathfrak{M}\cap L^{2}(X,\mu)
. Next, consider
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
for a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then the trace functional
\tr:\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty]
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tr(A)
, is a faithful semi-finite normal weight with
\mathfrak{N}_{\tr}=\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.

III.4.3
The GNS-construction.

(
[
83
, p. 88]
,
[
155
, p. 2]
)
Similarly as for a positive linear functional on a
C^{\ast}
-algebra (
cf.

Section

II.4.d
), one can also perform a GNS-construction for a weight
\varphi
on a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Define the inner product
\langle{X,Y}\rangle_{\varphi}\coloneqq\varphi(X^{\ast}Y)
on
\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
, and let
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
denote the completion of
\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
with respect to
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\varphi}
. Furthermore, let
\eta_{\varphi}:\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\eta_{\varphi}(A)
, be the canonical injection. Since
\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
is a left-ideal of
\mathfrak{M}
, and since

\varphi(X^{\ast}A^{\ast}AX)\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\langle{X,X}\rangle_{\varphi}

(III.11)

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
,
X\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
, it follows that there exists a
\ast
-representation
\pi_{\varphi}:\mathfrak{M}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi})
which is uniquely determined by
\pi_{\varphi}(A)\eta_{\varphi}(X)\coloneqq\eta_{\varphi}(AX)
. This representation will be faithful if the weight
\varphi
is faithful, and in this case one may consider
\mathfrak{M}
as a von Neumann algebra on
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
.

The next theorem, taken from
[
83
, Thm. 6.2]
, was originally discovered by
U. Haagerup
in 1975
[
75
]
. A proof may be found in
[
155
, Thm. 1.3 & Cor. 5.9]
.

Theorem III.4.4
(Haagerup).

For a weight
\varphi
on a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, the following properties are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi
is normal;

(b)

\varphi
is
\sigma
-weakly lower semi-continuous;

(c)

\varphi(A)=\sup\{{\omega(A):\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+},\omega\leq\varphi}\}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}^{+}
;

(d)

\varphi(A)=\sum_{i\in I}\langle{\xi_{i},A\xi_{i}}\rangle
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}^{+}
, where
(\xi_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
.

The object to be introduced next is required for the construction of the spatial derivative operator; the terminology is borrowed from
[
118
, Ch. 4]
.

Definition III.4.5
(Lineal).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra, let
\psi
be a semi-finite normal weight on
\mathfrak{M}
, and let
(\mathcal{H}_{\psi},\pi_{\psi},\eta_{\psi})
be the GNS-representation of
\mathfrak{M}
with respect to
\psi
. Define the
lineal
of
\psi
to be the set

D(\mathcal{H},\psi)\coloneqq\big\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\ :\ \exists C_{\xi}>0\ \forall A\in\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}:\|A\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{\xi}\,\|\eta_{\psi}(A)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}}\big\}

(III.12)

consisting of so-called
\psi
-bounded vectors
; this terminology is due to the fact that
\|\eta_{\psi}(A)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}=\psi(A^{\ast}A)^{1/2}
. Note that the lineal is a linear subspace of
\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, if
\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
happens to be a normal functional on
\mathfrak{M}
, then the lineal is given by

D(\mathcal{H},\psi)=\big\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\ :\ \exists C_{\xi}>0\ \forall A\in\mathfrak{M}:\|A\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq C_{\xi}\,\psi(A^{\ast}A)}\big\}\ .

(III.13)

The proof of the following result follows
[
83
, Lem. 11.2]
and
[
155
, Sect. 7.1]
.

Proposition III.4.6
.

In the terminology of
Definition

III.4.5
, the following assertions hold true.

(a)

The lineal
D(\mathcal{H},\psi)
is invariant under the commutant
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.

(b)

If
\psi
is assumed to be faithful, then
D(\mathcal{H},\psi)
