, such that
\xi_{n}\rightarrow\xi
and
\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{n}\rightarrow\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
. From
Eq.

III.15
and the definition of the operator
T
from above, it follows for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|{\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{m}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}&=\big\|{\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}T\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}\bigr)}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}=\big\|{\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}\\
&=\mathfrak{q}_{2}\bigl(\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}\bigr)=\varphi_{2}\Bigl(\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}\bigl(\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .\end{split}

(IV.16)

(Observe that
\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}\varOmega_{2}=D(\mathcal{H}_{2},\omega_{2}^{\prime})=\dom(\mathfrak{q}_{2})
, hence
Eq.

III.15
could indeed be applied; similarly, it was used that
\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}
is defined for elements in
D(\mathcal{H},\omega_{2}^{\prime})
, see
III.4.8
.) According to
Proposition

III.4.10
, the operator
\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}
takes the following form:

\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}\bigl(\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}\bigr)=\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\bigl[\mathfrak{M}_{2}^{\prime}\varOmega_{2}\bigr]\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}\ .

(IV.17)

1. Case
(U1)
.
If
\varOmega_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
,
Proposition

II.3.16

(b)
shows that it is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}_{2}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\bigl[\mathfrak{M}_{2}^{\prime}\varOmega_{2}\bigr]=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
according to
Corollary

B.1.10

(b)
. Using now the Schwarz inequality from
Lemma

II.2.11
as well as the assumption (
IV.14
) on
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, one finds that

\displaystyle\big\|{\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{m}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{2}\bigl(\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{1}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}\bigr)\ .

Similarly, since
\varOmega_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
, one finds that
\varTheta^{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}(A\varOmega_{1})=A\bigl[\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{\prime}\varOmega_{1}\bigr]A^{\ast}=AA^{\ast}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. Thus, the last expression obtained before can be recast into the form

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{1}\Bigl(\varTheta^{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}\bigr)\Bigr)=\mathfrak{q}_{1}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\big\|{\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}=\big\|{\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{m}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}\ .

Combining the previous observations, one obtains the following inequality for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
:

\|\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}\leq\|\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}\ .

(IV.18)

2. Case
(U2)
.
Define the projections
Q\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{\prime}\varOmega_{1}]\equiv\mathbf{s}(\omega_{1})\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
P\coloneqq[\mathfrak{M}_{2}^{\prime}\varOmega_{2}]\equiv\mathbf{s}(\omega_{2})\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. From
\omega_{1}\bigl(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}-Q\bigr)=0
,
cf.

Eq.

II.6
, and the assumption
\omega_{2}\circ\alpha\leq\omega_{1}
, it follows that
\omega_{2}\bigl(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}-\alpha(Q)\bigr)=0
. This shows that
P\leq\alpha(Q)
(by definition of the support projection
P
), so the assumptions of
Lemma

IV.3.6
are satisfied: with
Eq.

IV.12
, one obtains for the operator
\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}
from
Eq.

IV.17
that

\displaystyle\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}\bigl(\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})P\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\,Q\,(A_{n}-A_{m})^{\ast}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\alpha\bigl(\varTheta^{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}\bigr)\bigr)\ .

Using this inequality, one can now estimate
Eq.

IV.16
for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
as follows:

\displaystyle\big\|{\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{m}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{2}\Bigl(\varTheta^{\omega_{2}^{\prime}}\bigl(\alpha(A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{2}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha\Bigl(\varTheta^{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{1}\Bigl(\varTheta^{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}\bigl((A_{n}-A_{m})\varOmega_{1}\bigr)\Bigr)=\big\|{\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{n}-\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{m}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}\ ,

where in the third step the assumption
\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha\leq\varphi_{1}
from
Eq.

IV.14
was used. This is precisely the same inequality as obtained before in
Eq.

IV.18
.

3. Conclusion.
It was shown that assuming either
(U1)
or
(U2)
, the inequality (
IV.18
) holds true; with its help, the proof of the statement can be completed. Since the sequence
(\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}
converges to
\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi
by construction, it is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Therefore, (
IV.18
) shows that
(\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}T\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{2}
has the Cauchy property as well, hence it converges to some element
\eta\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Furthermore,

(T\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\bigl(\alpha(A_{n})\varOmega_{2}\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}\varOmega_{2}=\dom(\mathfrak{q}_{2})\subseteq\dom(\varDelta_{2}^{1/2})

as discussed above. It holds that
T\xi_{n}\rightarrow T\xi
in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
because
\xi_{n}\rightarrow\xi
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
by assumption and boundedness of
T
,
cf.

Statement

1
. Since the operator
\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}
is, in particular, closed, the convergence of the sequences
(T\xi_{n})_{n}
and
(\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}T\xi_{n})_{n}
implies that
T\xi\in\dom(\varDelta_{2}^{1/2})
and
\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}T\xi=\eta

[
144
, Prop. 1.4]
. As
\xi\in\dom(\varDelta_{1}^{1/2})
was arbitrary, the former shows that

T\bigl(\dom(\varDelta_{1}^{1/2})\bigr)\subseteq\dom(\varDelta_{2}^{1/2})

which is the first assertion of the statement. On the other hand, one also finds

\|\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\leq\|\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}

by noting that the argument leading to
Eq.

IV.18
can be repeated analogously with
\xi_{m}\equiv 0
, giving
\|\varDelta_{2}^{1/2}\,T\xi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}\leq\|\varDelta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and then taking the limit
n\rightarrow+\infty
and using the convergences discussed before. This is the second assertion of the statement, hence its proof is complete.

Statement

2
shows that the assumptions of
Lemma

IV.3.4
are satisfied for the operators
A_{i}=\varDelta_{i}^{1/2}
on
\mathcal{H}_{i}
and
T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
as constructed in
Statement

1
. Therefore, it follows that

\|\varDelta_{2}^{t/2}\,T\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\leq\|\varDelta_{1}^{t/2}\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}

for all
t\in(0,1)
and
\xi\in\dom(\varDelta_{1}^{1/2})
. Choosing
\xi=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\varOmega_{1}
, which is possible since
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\varOmega_{1}\subseteq\dom(\varDelta_{1}^{1/2})
, yields
T\xi=\varOmega_{2}
because
\alpha
is unital. Furthermore, by assumption it holds that
\|\varOmega_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}=\|\varOmega_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
. Therefore, the above inequality implies

\frac{1}{t}\Bigl(\|\varDelta_{2}^{t/2}\varOmega_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}-\|\varOmega_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{t}\Bigl(\|\varDelta_{1}^{t/2}\varOmega_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}-\|\varOmega_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}\Bigr)

(IV.19)

for all
0<t<1
. Multiplying both sides by minus one, taking the limit
t\searrow 0
, and using Uhlmann’s representation (
IV.7
) of the relative entropy from
Theorem

IV.2.4
finally yields the desired
Eq.

IV.15
.

Remarks IV.3.8
.

(a)

The assertion of the theorem also holds true for the relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}
in standard form which can be shown by an almost analogous proof, see
[
50
, Thm. 4.4]
for details.

(b)

The two different assumptions
(U1)
and
(U2)
might be interesting in different situations. On the one hand, for the local algebras of quantum field theory, the Reeh-Schlieder theorem
[
74
, Sect. II.5.3]
,
[
90
, p. 26]
,
[
135
]
provides cyclic and separating vectors. On the other hand, many of the Schwarz mappings that appear in applications are, in fact, completely positive (
cf.

Section

II.2.b
).

(c)

The proof of
Theorem

IV.3.7
given in
[
118
, Thm. 5.3]
seems to contain some inaccuracies: first, it is not very precise regarding the necessary assumptions on
\varOmega_{1},\varOmega_{2}
and
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. While it is clear that
\varOmega_{i}
has to be cyclic for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{{1,2}\}
, in order for the spatial derivative to be well-defined, in
[
118
]
it is not explicitly assumed that
\varOmega_{i}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}_{i}
as well. (This assumption is mentioned explicitly in
[
123
, Thm. 2]
, corresponding to
(U1)
, whereas
[
122
, Thm. 4]
assumes
\alpha
to be 2-positive, corresponding to
(U2)
.) Secondly, it appears that
[
118
]
implicitly assumes the von Neumann algebras to be in standard form; this somewhat contradicts using the spatial form of the relative entropy in the first place. The proof of
Theorem

IV.3.7
provided here is truly valid in any representation of the von Neumann algebras, with the cost of having to pose additional assumptions on the vector representatives of
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
or on the Schwarz mapping
\alpha
.

IV.3.c  Some Consequences of Monotonicity

In the following, a couple of consequences of
Theorem

IV.3.7
shall be derived; they are all mentioned in
[
118
, p. 82]
. By
Remarks

IV.3.8

(a)
, these corollaries are also valid for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}
.

Corollary IV.3.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be two von Neumann algebras acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, and let
\varphi,\omega\in(\mathfrak{M}_{2})_{\ast}^{+}
be two normal functionals on
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
such that
\omega
is induced by a cyclic vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. Then

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}^{\mathrm{spa}}\bigl(\omega\big|_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}},\varphi\big|_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}\bigr)\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)\ .

(IV.20)

Proof
.

Let
\omega_{1}\coloneqq\omega\big|_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}
and
\varphi_{1}\coloneqq\varphi\big|_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}
denote the restrictions of the the states
\omega,\varphi
to the subalgebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. Define
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
to be the inclusion
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A
which is a completely positive mapping satisfying
\omega\bigl(\alpha(A)\bigr)=\omega_{1}(A)
and
\varphi\bigl(\alpha(A)\bigr)=\varphi_{1}(A)
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. This shows that the assumptions of
Theorem

IV.3.7
are satisfied, hence
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{1},\varphi_{1})\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)
.

Corollary IV.3.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\varphi,\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
be two normal functionals with
\omega
being induced by a cyclic vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\varOmega\in\supp(\varphi)
. Then

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)\geq-\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varphi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}{\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}}}\right)\ .

Proof
.

Define the subalgebra
\mathfrak{M}_{0}\coloneqq\{{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
and note that
\varphi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}\equiv\varphi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
and
\omega|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}\equiv\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
. If
\mathbf{1}:\mathfrak{M}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow 1
, denotes the constant functional, one can write
\varphi_{0}\coloneqq\varphi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}=\varphi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\,\mathbf{1}
and
\omega_{0}\coloneqq\omega|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}=\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\,\mathbf{1}
. Using
Eq.

IV.20
and the scaling property of the relative entropy from
Proposition

IV.1.9
(which is proved analogously for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}
), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{0},\varphi_{0})=\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\,\mathord{\underbrace{\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})}_{=0}}-\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varphi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}{\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varphi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}{\omega(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}}}\right)\ .

Corollary IV.3.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\varphi,\omega\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
with
\omega
being induced by a cyclic vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)\geq 0
.

Corollary IV.3.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra, let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
be arbitrary, and let
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
be induced by a cyclic vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
. Then the the relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)
does not depend on the choice of
\varOmega
.

Proof
.

Suppose that
\varOmega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
is another cyclic vector for
\mathfrak{M}
such that
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega^{\prime}}
. Then, since
\omega_{\varOmega}=\omega_{\varOmega^{\prime}}
, it follows from
Theorem

IV.3.7
by choosing
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A
, that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega},\varphi)\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega^{\prime}},\varphi)
and also
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega^{\prime}},\varphi)\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega},\varphi)
, hence
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega},\varphi)=\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega^{\prime}},\varphi)
.

IV.4
Monotonicity on the Hilbert-Space Level

Theorem

IV.3.7
is the most general formulation of the monotonicity property of the relative entropy; this is highlighted, for example, by the various corollaries mentioned above. In this section, further consequences of Uhlmann’s theorem shall be studied, namely monotonicity properties of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega,\varphi)
for the case in which
\varphi=\omega_{\varPhi}
is a functional induced by some vector
\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
, too. (Recall from
Definition

IV.2.1
that this is not a necessary assumption in the definition of the
spatial form
of the relative entropy.)

IV.4.1
Motivation.

In applications, this setting is interesting for the following reasons.
First
, one usually does not consider completely arbitrary normal states on a von Neumann algebra, but rather (faithful) normal states induced by (cyclic and separating) vectors; therefore, it is interesting to examine how the monotonicity of the relative entropy is reflected in terms of the inducing vectors,
i.e.
, investigate what kind of mapping on the Hilbert-space level increases or decreases the relative entropy when applied to the vector representatives.
Second
, if one is given a vector on a Hilbert space which induces a state on a certain von Neumann algebra (
e.g.
, the vacuum vector or coherent vectors, to which much attention has been paid recently regarding the computation of relative entropies
[
35
,
68
,
86
,
106
]
), it might be handy to have a more specialized, concrete monotonicity inequality at hand, rather than having to rely on the very general but abstract
Theorem

IV.3.7
.
Third
, in the applications mentioned before, it is often the case that one starts with a given vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
inducing a state, and then transforms it via an operator
V\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
to another vector
\varOmega^{\prime}\coloneqq V\varOmega
which is then interpreted to induce a new, transformed state. Therefore, it might be interesting to see the monotonicity property of the relative entropy explicitly in terms of this transformation
V
.

The strategy for obtaining monotonicity results along these lines will be the following: given von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and an element
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
with certain properties, a Schwarz mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
will be constructed satisfying the additional assumptions of
Theorem

IV.3.7
; thus, the proof of the proposed results will be reduced to applying Uhlmann’s much more general theorem.

Notation
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\varPsi_{1},\varPsi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
be some vectors such that
\varPsi_{1}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
. Define a functional
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
by

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varPsi_{1},\varPsi_{2})\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varPsi_{1}},\omega_{\varPsi_{2}})\ .

IV.4.a  A First Result

The following proposition contains the first result in the direction described above.

Proposition IV.4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be von Neumann algebras such that
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, and assume that both algebras act on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, let
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be an isometry, and let
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
be two vectors such that
V\varOmega
and
\varOmega
are cyclic for the algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, respectively. Then the following inequality is satisfied:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

(IV.21)

Proof
.

Denote by
\omega_{1}=\omega_{V\varOmega}
and
\varphi_{1}=\omega_{V\varPhi}
the vector functionals on the algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
induced by
V\varOmega
and
V\varPhi
, and let
\omega_{2}=\omega_{\varOmega}
and
\varphi_{2}=\omega_{\varPhi}
be the vector functionals on
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
corresponding to
\varOmega
and
\varPhi
. Note that since
V\varOmega
and
\varOmega
are assumed to be cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, respectively, the spatial derivatives
\varDelta(\varphi_{1}/\omega_{1}^{\prime})
and
\varDelta(\varphi_{2}/\omega_{2}^{\prime})
are well-defined, hence are the relative entropies
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{2},\varphi_{2})
.

Let
\iota:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
,
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow A
, denote the inclusion mapping which is a well-defined continuous injective
\ast
-homomorphism by the assumption
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. Define
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
by

\alpha(A)\coloneqq V^{\ast}\iota(A)V\quad(A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1})\ .

(IV.22)

Observe that this defines a Schwarz mapping between the von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
: first,
\alpha
is a completely positive mapping by
Examples

II.2.8

(a)
, hence it satisfies the Schwarz inequality according to
Proposition

II.2.12
, and also assumption
(U2)
of
Theorem

IV.3.7
. Second,
\alpha
is unital because
\alpha(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=V^{\ast}V=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
by the isometry property of
V
.

Next, observe that the following identity holds true for the functional
\omega_{2}
composed with the Schwarz mapping
\alpha
: for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\circ\alpha(A)=\langle{\varOmega,\alpha(A)\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{\varOmega,V^{\ast}\iota(A)V\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{V\varOmega,A\,V\varOmega}\rangle=\omega_{1}(A)\ .

Analogously, one also obtains
\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha(A)=\varphi_{1}(A)
. Furthermore, it holds that
\|V\varOmega\|=\|\varOmega\|
due to the isometry property, hence
\omega_{1}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=\omega_{2}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
. Therefore, all assumptions of
Theorem

IV.3.7
are satisfied, and it follows that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{1},\varphi_{1})\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{2},\varphi_{2})
. In the notation introduced above, this is exactly the inequality (
IV.21
), hence the proposition is proved.

In the following, the assumptions on the vectors
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
and on the operator
V\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
shall be discussed; the form of the mapping
\alpha
from
Eq.

IV.22
will be commented on below.

Remarks IV.4.3
.

(a)

The assumption that
V\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
should be cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
appears to be rather strong since it restricts the admissible isometries
V
in the above proposition. One may modify the assumptions on
\varOmega
and
V
slightly as to still guarantee cyclicity of
V\varOmega
for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
; see
Proposition

IV.4.6
below.

(b)

The isometry property of the operator
V
was only used to establish that the Schwarz mapping defined in (
IV.22
) is unital. Going back to
Theorem

IV.3.7
, one recognizes that this property of
\alpha
is used in two places. First, in the case of assumption
(U2)
, to apply
Lemma

IV.3.6
which requires the mapping
\alpha
to be unital. Second, to derive
Eq.

IV.19
which uses that the operator
T:\mathcal{H}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
T(A\varOmega_{1})=\alpha(A)\varOmega_{2}
, defined in the proof of the theorem satisfies
T(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\varOmega_{1})=\alpha(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}})\varOmega_{2}=\varOmega_{2}
. Looking at this last expression, one may relax the assumption on
\alpha
, at least under the assumption
(U1)
of
Theorem

IV.3.7
which does not rely on
Eq.

IV.12
, as follows:

•

The assertion of Uhlmann’s theorem, assuming
(U1)
, still holds true if
\alpha
satisfies the Schwarz inequality and the identity
\alpha(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}})\varOmega_{2}=\varOmega_{2}
.

One class of mappings
V
, more general than isometries as considered in the previous proposition but still interesting enough for applications, for which this relaxed condition on
\alpha
might be feasible to verify is considered below in
Proposition

IV.4.7
.

(c)

The properties of the vector
\varPhi
also have a strong influence on the usefulness of the inequality (
IV.21
): recall from
Definition

IV.2.1
that
\varOmega\in\supp(\varphi)
is a necessary condition for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{\varOmega},\varphi)
to be finite. Therefore, in the context of
Proposition

IV.4.2
, the cases
V\varOmega\in\supp(\omega_{V\varPhi})
and
\varOmega\in\supp(\omega_{\varPhi})
are relevant. By
Proposition

II.4.13
, this is to say that

V\varOmega\in\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{\prime}V\varPhi)\quad\text{and}\quad\varOmega\in\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathfrak{M}_{2}^{\prime}\varPhi)\ .

It is noteworthy to observe that if
V\varPhi
and
\varPhi
are separating for the algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, respectively, then the above conditions are automatically satisfied by
Proposition

II.3.16

(b)
.

Before turning to some modifications of the assumptions of
Proposition

IV.4.2
, some immediate consequences of
Eq.

IV.21
shall be recorded. The first one is nothing but the monotonicity statement of
Corollary

IV.3.9
for the special case of restricting vector functionals to a subalgebra.

Corollary IV.4.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be von Neumann algebras acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. If
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
are arbitrary vectors such that
\varOmega
is cyclic for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
, then

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

Corollary IV.4.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra, let
V\in\mathfrak{M}
be an isometry, and let
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary vectors such that
V\varOmega
and
\varOmega
are cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
. Then

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

IV.4.b  Modification of the Assumptions

The next two propositions concern modifications of the setting of
Proposition

IV.4.2
.

Proposition IV.4.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, let
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
be an isometry, and let
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
be two vectors such that
\varOmega
is cyclic for the algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. Then

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

Proof
.

The argument establishing the inequality is identical to that of
Proposition

IV.4.2
, the only difference being that it has to be verified whether
V\varOmega
and
\varOmega
are cyclic for the algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, respectively. The latter property is clear since
\varOmega
being cyclic for the subalgebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
implies that it is also cyclic for the larger algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. For the former, let
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary. By cyclicity of
\varOmega
for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
, there exists a sequence
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{1}
such that
\xi=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,B_{n}\varOmega
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define an operator
A_{n}\coloneqq B_{n}V^{\ast}\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. From the isometry property of
V
, it follows that
\xi=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,B_{n}V^{\ast}V\varOmega=\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,A_{n}V\varOmega
, showing cyclicity of
V\varOmega
since
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
was arbitrary.

Proposition IV.4.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, let
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be a partial isometry, and let
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\varOmega
is an element of the initial subspace of
V
, and
V\varOmega
and
\varOmega
are cyclic and separating for the algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, respectively. Then

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

Proof
.

The argument is again analogous to that of
Proposition

IV.4.2
with the following changes: since the vectors
V\varOmega,\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
are now assumed to be cyclic and separating for the respective algebra, assumption
(U1)
of
Theorem

IV.3.7
is satisfied. Moreover, the mapping
\alpha(A)\coloneqq V^{\ast}\iota(A)V
is still completely positive, hence it satisfies the Schwarz inequality. Finally, one has that

\alpha(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\varOmega=V^{\ast}V\varOmega=\varOmega

because by assumption,
\varOmega
is in the initial subspace of
V
onto which
V^{\ast}V
is the unique orthogonal projection (
cf.

Definition

B.1.14
), hence
Lemma

B.1.7
applies. The validity of the relations
\omega_{2}\circ\alpha=\omega_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha=\varphi_{1}
is not influenced by the specific properties of
V
, hence all the assumptions of Uhlmann’s
Theorem

IV.3.7
are satisfied, and the asserted inequality follows.

If the element
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
is not only an isometry but a unitary operator, it turns out that there is a
“reversed”
form of
Eq.

IV.21
; this is the content of the next result.

Proposition IV.4.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, let
U\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be a unitary operator, and let
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
be two vectors such that
\varOmega
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. Then

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(U\varOmega,U\varPhi)\ .

Proof
.

Let
\omega_{1}
and
\varphi_{1}
be the vector functionals on
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
induced by
\varOmega
and
\varPhi
, and let
\omega_{2}
and
\varphi_{2}
be the vector functionals on
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
coming from
U\varOmega
and
U\varPhi
, respectively. From the assumption on
\varOmega
and
U^{\ast}U=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, it follows that
U\varOmega
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
(see the proof of
Proposition

IV.4.7
). This shows that both of the relative entropies are well-defined.

Consider now the mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
given by
\alpha(A)\coloneqq U\iota(A)U^{\ast}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
, where
\iota:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
is the inclusion. Since
U
is assumed to be unitary, it follows that
\alpha
is unital. Moreover, invoking
Examples

II.2.8

(a)
once more,
\alpha
is completely positive, hence satisfies the Schwarz inequality; in fact, one can directly compute that

\alpha(A^{\ast}A)=UA^{\ast}AU^{\ast}=UA^{\ast}U^{\ast}UAU^{\ast}=\alpha(A)^{\ast}\alpha(A)

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. Finally, there holds

\omega_{2}\circ\alpha(A)=\langle{U\varOmega,\alpha(A)\,U\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{U^{\ast}U\varOmega,A\,U^{\ast}U\varOmega}\rangle=\langle{\varOmega,A\varOmega}\rangle=\omega_{1}(A)\ ,

and similarly
\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha=\varphi_{1}
. The assumptions of Uhlmann’s
Theorem

IV.3.7
are satisfied; therefore,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{1},\varphi_{1})\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}^{\mathrm{spa}}(\omega_{2},\varphi_{2})
which is the asserted inequality.

Combining
Corollary

IV.4.5
and
Proposition

IV.4.8
yields the following result which is actually a special case of invariance of the relative entropy under
\ast
-automorphisms
[
10
, Eq. (6.6)]
.

Corollary IV.4.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a von Neumann algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, let
U\in\mathfrak{M}
be a unitary operator, and let
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
be vectors such that
\varOmega
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
. Then

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(U\varOmega,U\varPhi)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

Proof
.

First, observe that both relative entropies are well-defined due to the assumption on
\varOmega
. (The argument is analogous to that given in the proof of
Proposition

IV.4.7
.) Now, on the one hand, choosing
\mathfrak{M}_{1}=\mathfrak{M}_{2}=\mathfrak{M}
in
Proposition

IV.4.8
shows that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(U\varOmega,U\varPhi)
. On the other hand,
Corollary

IV.4.5
implies
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(U\varOmega,U\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)
. Thus,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(U\varOmega,U\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}}(U\varOmega,U\varPhi)
which proves equality.

Having discussed modifications on the assumptions on the vectors
\varOmega
and
\varPhi
, and on the operator
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, now, the structure of the mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
defined in
Eq.

IV.22
, and the assumptions on the algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
shall be commented on.

Remarks IV.4.10
.

(a)

The assumption
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, which might seem artificial or very restrictive at first glance, appears to be rather natural in the context of algebraic quantum field theory
[
12
,
66
,
74
]
. There, one considers nets
\mathfrak{A}:\mathcal{O}\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O})
of unital
C^{\ast}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O})
for every open and bounded region
\mathcal{O}\subseteq M
of the spacetime manifold
M
. Each algebra
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O})
is interpreted as the algebra of observables of the region
\mathcal{O}
,
i.e.
, it should contain the observables which can be measured within
\mathcal{O}
.

The local nets have to satisfy certain axioms, one of them being the axiom of
isotony

[
66
, p. 2]
: if
\mathcal{O}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{2}
for two open and bounded regions
\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}\subseteq M
, then there exists an inclusion
\iota:\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{2})
which implies that
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\subseteq\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{2})
. Choosing a representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
of the local algebras on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, it follows that
\pi\bigl(\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\bigr)\subseteq\pi\bigl(\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{2})\bigr)
, hence

\pi\bigl(\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\bigr)^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\pi\bigl(\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{2})\bigr)^{\prime\prime}

since the operation of forming the commutant is order-reversing
[
112
, Rem. 6.2]
. With this observation, the setting of
Proposition

IV.4.2
is recovered: suppose one is interested in calculating the relative entropy
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)
on a local algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\coloneqq\pi\bigl(\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\bigr)^{\prime\prime}
, where
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
are elements in the representation space and
V\in\pi\bigl(\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{O}_{2})\bigr)^{\prime\prime}\eqqcolon\mathfrak{M}_{2}
is an isometry. If this turns out to be difficult or not analytically possible at all, one can consider the relative entropy
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)
on the larger algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
which one might be able to compute. Then, by virtue of
Eq.

IV.21
, one obtains at least an upper bound for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)
.

(b)

A crucial step in the proofs of the above propositions consisted in invoking
Examples

II.2.8

(a)
which showed that the conjugation of a
\ast
-homomorphism is a completely positive mapping. In fact, Stinespring’s dilation theorem
[
153
, Thm. 1]
,
[
154
, Thm. 1.2.7]
implies that all completely positive mappings on
C^{\ast}
-algebras are of this form. It turns out that there is a generalization of this theorem to von Neumann algebras
[
105
, Thm. 2.10]
,
[
22
, Thm. 2.3]
,
[
43
, Ch. V, App. B, Cor. 9]
which shows that
Proposition

IV.4.2
actually examines the implications of Uhlmann’s
Theorem

IV.3.7
for the special case of a completely positive map
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. Therefore, one might further investigate the manifestation of monotonicity of the relative entropy on the Hilbert-space level in the case of a general Schwarz mapping. To this end, it would be desirable to obtain a dilation theorem for Schwarz maps.

A final generalization of
Proposition

IV.4.2
that shall be considered here is relaxing the assumption
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}
. This is the content of the next proposition.

Proposition IV.4.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Assume that there exists a
\ast
-homomorphism
\rho:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
such that
A-\rho(A)\geq 0
in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. Let
V\in\mathfrak{M}_{2}
be an isometry and
\varPhi,\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
such that
V\varOmega
and
\varOmega
are cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, respectively. Then it holds that

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(V\varOmega,V\varPhi)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\varOmega,\varPhi)\ .

Proof
.

Define
\omega_{1}=\omega_{V\varOmega}
,
\varphi_{1}=\omega_{V\varPhi}
on
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
and
\omega_{2}=\omega_{\varOmega}
,
\varphi_{2}=\omega_{\varPhi}
on
\mathfrak{M}_{2}
, and consider the mapping
\alpha:\mathfrak{M}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{2}
given by
\alpha(A)\coloneqq V^{\ast}\rho(A)V
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
. It still satisfies the Schwarz inequality since it is completely positive. Moreover, for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}_{1}
it holds that

\omega_{2}\circ\alpha(A)=\langle{V\varOmega,\rho(A)V\varOmega}\rangle\leq\langle{V\varOmega,A\,V\varOmega}\rangle=\omega_{1}(A)\ ,

and analogously
\varphi_{2}\circ\alpha\leq\varphi_{1}
. The claim now follows again from Uhlmann’s
Theorem

IV.3.7
.

Chapter V
Perturbation Theory in Operator Algebras

In this chapter, another aspect in the theory of operator algebras shall be illuminated: the perturbation of automorphism groups and KMS-states. This is a cornerstone in the theory of operator algebras and important for applications in quantum field theory and quantum statistical mechanics. In
Section

V.1
, the necessary background material is discussed, namely the
Liouvillian
of a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system
and the important notion of
KMS-states
.
Section

V.2
gives a brief introduction to general perturbation theory and provides some results for unbounded perturbations. After that,
Section

V.3
focuses on
perturbations of KMS-states
using the framework developed in
[
50
]
. Finally, these methods are used in
Section

V.4
to extend the so-called
two-sided Bogoliubov inequality
to arbitrary von Neumann algebras.

References
.

The main sources are
[
29
, Ch. 3]
,
[
30
, Ch. 5]
,
[
118
, Ch. 12]
,
[
50
]
, and
[
131
]
.

V.1
Dynamical Systems, Liouvillians, and KMS-States

Before the study of perturbation theory can commence, some technical background will be introduced in this section: continuous one-parameter groups of
\ast
-automorphisms, their associated Liouvillians, and the notion of KMS-states on von Neumann algebras.

V.1.a  The Liouvillian of a
W^{\ast}
-Dynamical System

Definition V.1.1
(
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system).

Let
\tau:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
,
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tau_{t}
, be a one-parameter group of
\ast
-automorphisms of a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
, that is,
\tau_{0}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{M}}
and
\tau_{s+t}=\tau_{s}\circ\tau_{t}
for all
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
. The group
(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is said to be
pointwise
\sigma
-weakly continuous
iff for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
,
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tau_{t}(A)
is continuous in the
\sigma
-weak topology on
\mathfrak{M}

[
50
, p. 453]
. In this case, the pair
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
is called a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system
, and the mapping
\tau
is called
W^{\ast}
-dynamics
on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Examples V.1.2
.

(a)

Consider a
finite quantum system
, that is, the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
over a complex separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and let
H:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(H)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be the self-adjoint
Hamiltonian
of
\mathfrak{M}
. Define a one-parameter group of
\ast
-automorphisms
\tau=(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathfrak{M}
by

\tau_{t}(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}\quad(t\in\mathbb{R},\,A\in\mathfrak{M})\ .

One can show that
\tau
is pointwise
\sigma
-weakly continuous
[
131
, Exa. 4.16]
, hence
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
is a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system. Furthermore, it holds that
\tau
is strongly continuous on
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if the Hamiltonian
H
is
bounded

[
131
, p. 133]
. In this case, the infinitesimal generator of
\tau
, that is, the operator
\delta:\mathfrak{M}\supseteq\dom(\delta)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
satisfying
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{t}}_{\upharpoonright\,t=0}\,\tau_{t}(A)=\delta(A)

[
59
, Lem. II.1.3]
, is given by

\delta(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{i}\,[H,A]{\ ,\quad}A\in\dom(\delta)=\mathfrak{M}\ .

(b)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{\ast}
-algebra. A
\ast
-automorphism
\alpha\in\Aut(\mathfrak{A})
of
\mathfrak{A}
is called
inner
iff there exists a unitary element
U\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\alpha(A)=UA\,U^{\ast}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. If one is given a one-parameter group
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of unitary operators
U_{t}\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
, then one obtains a one-parameter group
\tau=(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\ast
-automorphisms on
\mathfrak{A}
by setting
[
131
, p. 133]

\tau_{t}(A)\coloneqq U_{t}\,A\,U_{t}^{\ast}\quad(t\in\mathbb{R},\,A\in\mathfrak{A})\ .

Groups of
\ast
-automorphisms of the above type are called
spatial
; one also says that the group
\tau:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\Aut(\mathfrak{A})
is
implemented
by the group
U:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
.

V.1.3
Standard Liouvillian.

(
[
50
, pp. 456 f.]
,
[
131
, pp. 149 f.]
)
Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system.
Theorem

III.2.4
implies that there is a standard form
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
, and
Corollary

III.2.10
yields for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
a unique unitary operator
U_{t}\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\tau_{t}(A)=U_{t}\,A\,U_{t}^{\ast}
and
U_{t}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. One can show that the group
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is strongly continuous
[
131
, p. 149]
, hence Stone’s theorem
[
144
, Thm. 6.2]
implies that there exists a unique self-adjoint operator
L:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(L)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
, called the
standard Liouvillian
of
\tau
, such that
U_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, it follows from the properties of the standard implementation of
\ast
-automorphisms given in
Corollary

III.2.10
that

(a)

JL+LJ=0
;

(b)

L\xi_{\psi}=0
for all
\tau
-invariant
\psi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
.

Proposition V.1.4
(
[
50
, Thm. 2.9]
,
[
131
, Prop. 4.46]
).

The standard Liouvillian is the unique self-adjoint operator
L
on
\mathcal{H}
such that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, there holds

(a)

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
;

(b)

\tau_{t}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}
.

Examples V.1.5
.

(a)

(
[
131
, Exa. 4.51]
) Consider the finite quantum system from
Examples

V.1.2

(a)
. According to
Examples

III.2.5

(b)
, the standard form of
\mathfrak{M}
is given by
\bigl(\mathfrak{M},\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),J,\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{+}\bigr)
. The standard Liouvillian
L:\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\supseteq\dom(L)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
is determined by the identity

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}X=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}X\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}{\ ,\quad}X\in\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\ .

(V.1)

This can be seen as follows: on the one hand, the above relation for
L
results in a unitary implementation of the dynamics
\tau_{t}
since

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}X=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\bigl(A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}X\bigr)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\bigl(A\,(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}X\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH})\bigr)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}X\ .

On the other hand, the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}
acting as in (
V.1
) preserves the natural positive cone
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{+}
. Therefore, by
Proposition

V.1.4
, this determines
L
uniquely.

(b)

(
[
50
, p. 455]
) Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra with cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
, and standard form representation
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
induced by
\varOmega
(
cf.

III.2.1
). Let
\varDelta_{\varOmega}
be the modular operator of
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega)
. Then the modular group (see
Definition

III.1.10
)
\sigma_{t}:\mathfrak{M}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
,

\sigma_{t}(A)=\varDelta_{\varOmega}^{\mathrm{i}t}A\,\varDelta_{\varOmega}^{-\mathrm{i}t}\quad(t\in\mathbb{R},\,A\in\mathfrak{M})\ ,

is a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
L_{\varOmega}=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})
. To see this, note that
Proposition

III.2.2

(c)
implies that
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{\varOmega}}\mathcal{P}=\varDelta_{\varOmega}^{\mathrm{i}t}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}
. Moreover, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, one finds that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{\varOmega}}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL_{\varOmega}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\log\varDelta_{\varOmega}}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\log\varDelta_{\varOmega}}=\varDelta_{\varOmega}^{\mathrm{i}t}A\,\varDelta_{\varOmega}^{-\mathrm{i}t}=\tau_{t}(A)\ .

Thus, according to
Proposition

V.1.4
,
L_{\varOmega}=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})
must be the unique standard Liouvillian of the
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system
(\mathfrak{M},\sigma)
.

V.1.b  KMS-States on von Neumann Algebras

In the following, thermal equilibrium states for systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom will be introduced. Their mathematical description reveals a deep connection with modular theory.

V.1.6
Motivation.

The
Gibbs variational principle

[
118
, Prop. 1.10]
shows that on a finite quantum system
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
at inverse temperature
1
1

1

In this text, Boltzmann’s constant
k_{B}
is set equal to one; thus, the entropy is dimensionless, and temperature is measured in units of Joule.

\beta=1/T
with Hamiltonian
H\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
such that
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, there exists a unique normal state
\varphi_{\beta}\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
, called
canonical Gibbs state
and given by

\displaystyle\rho_{\beta}=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{\beta}=\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H})\ ,

which minimizes the
free energy
functional
F_{\beta}(\varphi)=\varphi(H)+\beta^{-1}\tr(\rho_{\varphi}\log\rho_{\varphi})
and is hence a thermal equilibrium state
[
56
, p. 40]
. (The specific form of
\rho_{\beta}
is implied by the
principle of maximum entropy
and the conservation of internal energy
[
72
, p. 242]
.)

For systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom, canonical Gibbs states do not exist because the operator
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}
will not be trace-class since
H
has continuous spectrum
[
32
, p. 4]
,
[
74
, pp. 206 f.]
,
[
111
, p. 597]
. There is, however, an alternative description of equilibrium states based on their strong relationship with the time evolution
[
131
, p. 169]
. To make this precise, consider the
W^{\ast}
-dynamics
\tau_{t}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}
on
\mathfrak{M}
,
cf.

Examples

V.1.2

(a)
, and define for arbitrary
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}
the function
[
83
, p. 21]
,
[
90
, p. 19]

F_{\beta,A,B}(z)\coloneqq\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\,\tr\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}A\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}zH}B\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}zH}\bigr)\quad(z\in\mathbb{C})

(V.2)

which is analytic in the strip
S_{\beta}\coloneqq\{{z\in\mathbb{C}\ :\ 0<\operatorname{Im}(z)<\beta}\}
. For
z=t\in\mathbb{R}
, that is,
\operatorname{Im}(z)=0
, there holds
F_{\beta,A,B}(t)=\varphi_{\beta}\bigl(A\tau_{t}(B)\bigr)
, and for
z=t+\mathrm{i}\beta
, that is,
\operatorname{Im}(z)=\beta
, one finds

\displaystyle F_{\beta,A,B}(t+\mathrm{i}\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\,\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}B\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}\mathrm{e}^{\beta H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\tr\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}B\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\beta}\bigl(\tau_{t}(B)A\bigr)\ .

Thus, in a certain sense, the function
F_{\beta,A,B}
encodes the
non-commutativity
of the product
A\tau_{t}(B)
in the thermal state
\varphi_{\beta}

[
131
, p. 169]
.

The boundary behavior of (
V.2
) and its analyticity properties were first pointed out by
R. Kubo
,
P. C. Martin
, and
J. Schwinger

[
98
,
110
]
. The following generalization of this observation to arbitrary normal states on von Neumann algebras is due to
R. Haag
,
N. M. Hugenholtz
, and
M. Winnink

[
73
]
who postulated these conditions to be the defining property of equilibrium states
[
74
, pp. 200 ff.]
.

Definition V.1.7
(KMS-state).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system and
\beta>0
. A normal state
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
is called a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state
iff for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}
there exists a function
F_{\beta,A,B}:\mathbb{C}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
which is analytic in the strip
S_{\beta}=\{{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,0<\operatorname{Im}(z)<\beta}\}
and continuous on its closure, and which satisfies the following
KMS-boundary conditions
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
:

F_{\beta,A,B}(t)=\omega\bigl(A\tau_{t}(B)\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\beta,A,B}(t+\mathrm{i}\beta)=\omega\bigl(\tau_{t}(B)A\bigr)\ .

A thorough discussion of KMS-states, their properties, and arguments in favor of using them as equilibrium states can be found in
[
30
, Ch. 5]
or
[
74
, Sect. V.1 & V.3]
. Here, only a few results which are needed below in the development of perturbation theory shall be mentioned. To begin with, the following definition, taken from
[
50
, p. 460]
, introduces a class of elements
A\in\mathfrak{M}
for which
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tau_{t}(A)
extends to an entire analytic function on
\mathbb{C}
. A proof of the subsequent important
Theorem

V.1.9
can be found in
[
29
, Prop. 2.5.22]
,
[
83
, Lem. 2.13]
, or
[
131
, p. 170]
.

Definition V.1.8
(
\tau
-entire element).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system. An element
A\in\mathfrak{M}
is called
\tau
-entire
iff there exists a function
f:\mathbb{C}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
such that

(a)

f(t)=\tau_{t}(A)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\mathbb{C}\owns z\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\varphi\bigl(f(z)\bigr)\in\mathbb{C}
is analytic for all
\varphi\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}
.

If these conditions are satisfied, one also writes
f(z)\equiv\tau_{z}(A)
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
. The set of all
\tau
-entire elements of
\mathfrak{M}
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
.

Theorem V.1.9
(Approximation by
\tau
-entire elements).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system. Then the set of all
\tau
-entire elements
\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
forms a
\tau
-invariant
\sigma
-weakly and strongly dense
\ast
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{M}
.

The next proposition gives a characterization of the KMS-condition which is often more convenient to use than
Definition

V.1.7
; it indicates that the dynamics
\tau
measures the non-tracial character of a KMS-state
[
30
, p. 78]
. The proof can be found in
[
30
, Prop. 5.3.7]
or
[
131
, Thm. 5.4]
.

Proposition V.1.10
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system and
\beta>0
. A normal state
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
is a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state if and only if there exists a
\sigma
-weakly dense
\tau
-invariant
\ast
-subalgebra
\mathfrak{D}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
of
\tau
-entire elements of
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
such that for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{D}
:

\omega\bigl(A\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta}(B)\bigr)=\omega(BA)\ .

The following profound theorem
[
30
, Thm. 5.3.10]
,
[
83
, Thm. 2.14]
, originally due to
M. Takesaki

[
158
, Thm. 13.1 & 13.2]
, relates the KMS-condition with the modular group.

Theorem V.1.11
(Takesaki).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a
\sigma
-finite von Neumann algebra with cyclic and separating vector
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
and corresponding faithful normal functional
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
, and let
\varDelta
,
J
be the modular data and
(\sigma_{t}^{\omega})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the modular automorphism group associated with
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega)
. Then the following assertions hold true:

(a)

\omega
satisfies the
(\sigma,-1)
-KMS-condition (with respect to the strip
\{{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,\beta<\operatorname{Im}(z)<0}\}
).

(b)

The modular group is uniquely determined as the
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
for which
\omega
satisfies the KMS-condition at
\beta=-1
.

Example V.1.12
.

(
[
16
, Prop. 4.7]
,
[
100
, Prop. 9.25]
,
[
131
, Exa. 5.5]
)
Consider the finite quantum system
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
from
Examples

V.1.2

(a)
. Let
\beta>0
and
\omega
be an arbitrary
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state on
\mathfrak{M}
given by a density matrix
\rho_{\omega}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
, and assume that the Hamiltonian
H
is chosen such that
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.

For any two vectors
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, consider the operator
A\coloneqq\langle{\xi,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ }\rangle\,\eta\in\mathfrak{M}
, and let
B\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
be an arbitrary
\tau
-entire element. Observe that

\omega\bigl(A\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta}(B)\bigr)=\tr\bigl(A\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta}(B)\rho_{\omega}\bigr)=\langle{\xi,\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta}(B)\rho_{\omega}\eta}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\omega(BA)=\tr\bigl(\rho_{\omega}BA\bigr)=\langle{\xi,\rho_{\omega}B\eta}\rangle\ .

The characterization of KMS-states from
Proposition

V.1.10
now implies that

\langle{\xi,\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta}(B)\rho_{\omega}\eta}\rangle=\langle{\xi,\rho_{\omega}B\eta}\rangle\ .

Since
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
were arbitrary, this shows that the operator relation
\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta}(B)\rho_{\omega}=\rho_{\omega}B
holds true for all
\tau
-entire elements
B
in
\mathfrak{M}
; by definition of
\tau
, it can be written as

B(\mathrm{e}^{\beta H}\rho_{\omega})=(\mathrm{e}^{\beta H}\rho_{\omega})B\ .

Since
B\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
was arbitrary and
\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
lies strongly dense in
\mathfrak{M}
by
Theorem

V.1.9
, it follows that the above relation is valid for all
B\in\mathfrak{M}
. Therefore, one may conclude that
\mathrm{e}^{\beta H}\rho_{\omega}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, hence there exists
c\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\rho_{\omega}=c\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}
. Since
\rho_{\omega}
must be a density matrix, it follows that

\rho_{\omega}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}}{\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H})}\ .

This argument, together with the computations in
V.1.6
, shows that the
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
admits a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state if and only if
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}
is a trace-class operator, and that in this case the KMS-state is given by the canonical Gibbs state.

The next result, taken from
[
50
, Thm. 2.13]
, gives a characterization of the KMS-condition expressed on the Hilbert-space level using the Liouvillian of the dynamics.

Proposition V.1.13
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form and
\varOmega\in\mathcal{P}
be a unit vector. Furthermore, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
L
. Then
\omega_{\varOmega}
is a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS state if and only if
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega\subseteq\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2})
and

\forall A\in\mathfrak{M}\ :\ \mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2}A\varOmega=JA^{\ast}\varOmega\ .

(V.3)

In this case, if
\varOmega
is cyclic and
\varDelta_{\varOmega}
denotes the corresponding modular operator, then

\varDelta_{\varOmega}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L}\ .

(V.4)

This section shall be concluded by providing certain results regarding the convergence of
W^{\ast}
-dynamics and their associated Liouvillians, invariant states, and KMS-states. The proof of the following proposition can be found in
[
50
, Thm. 2.14]
.

Proposition V.1.14
.

Consider a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
in standard form
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
.

(a)

Let
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
W^{\ast}
-dynamics
\tau_{n}=(\tau_{n,t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
\mathfrak{M}
, let
L_{n}
denote the standard Liouvillian of
\tau_{n}
, and assume that
L
is a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
such that
L_{n}\rightarrow L
in the strong resolvent sense (
Definition

B.3.8
). Then

\tau_{t}(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\quad(t\in\mathbb{R},\,A\in\mathfrak{M})

defines a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian given by
L
.

(b)

Assume, in addition to the previous statement, that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\omega_{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\ast}^{+}
is a
\tau_{n}
-invariant normal functional with standard vector representative
\varOmega_{n}\in\mathcal{P}
, and that
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\varOmega_{n}=\varOmega
. Then
\varOmega\in\mathcal{P}
and the vector functional
\omega_{\varOmega}
is
\tau
-invariant.

(c)

Let
\beta>0
. In the situation of the previous statement, assume additionally that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\omega_{n}
is a
(\tau_{n},\beta)
-KMS-state, and that
\varOmega\neq 0
. Then
\omega_{\varOmega/\|\varOmega\|}
is a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state.

V.2
Foundations of Perturbation Theory

This section begins with the presentation of perturbation theory in operator algebras. The focus lies on unbounded perturbations since they are especially interesting in the context of the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality, and bounded perturbations are more commonly treated in the existing literature.

V.2.a  Bounded Perturbations

V.2.1
Motivation.

Consider a finite quantum system
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
with bounded self-adjoint Hamiltonian
H_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
generating the dynamics
\tau_{t}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{0}}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{0}}
. It was mentioned in
Examples

V.1.2

(a)
that the infinitesimal generator of
\tau
is given by
\delta_{0}(A)=\mathrm{i}\,[H_{0},A]
for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
.

A natural question to ask in this context is how the dynamics change if
H_{0}
is
perturbed
by some bounded self-adjoint operator
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
; physically speaking, this corresponds to adding an interaction to the Hamiltonian
H_{0}
describing a free quantum system. In this case, it follows that the generator
\delta
for the dynamics coming from the Hamiltonian
H=H_{0}+V
is given by

\delta(A)=\delta_{0}(A)+\mathrm{i}\,[V,A]\quad(A\in\mathfrak{M})\ .

(V.5)

For more general
W^{\ast}
-dynamical systems, where there is no fixed Hamiltonian which could be perturbed, one may take (
V.5
) as a
definition
for the perturbed dynamics since this identity does not require any particular form for the unperturbed generator
\delta_{0}
, and it is a purely algebraic identity, hence perfectly suited for the operator-algebraic setting. This approach will be outlined below.

Bounded perturbation theory in operator algebras was initiated by
H. Araki
in 1973
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
; his work was inspired by the perturbative expansions used in quantum electrodynamics. A different approach to the theory was developed by
M. J. Donald
in
[
55
]
which deals with semi-bounded perturbations; a review can be found in
[
118
, Ch. 12]
.

V.2.2
Perturbed dynamics.

(
[
30
, pp. 147 f.]
,
[
131
, Sect. 4.8]
)
Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system and
\delta_{0}:\mathfrak{M}\supseteq\dom(\delta_{0})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
be the infinitesimal generator of
\tau
. A
bounded perturbation
of the dynamics
\tau
is obtained by perturbing
\delta_{0}
with
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
as follows:

\delta_{V}\coloneqq\delta_{0}+\mathrm{i}\,[V,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,]\quad\text{with}\quad\dom(\delta_{V})=\dom(\delta_{0})\ .

Indeed, using the Hille-Yosida theorem
[
29
, Thm. 3.2.50]
,
[
131
, Prop. 4.15]
, it follows that
\delta_{V}
is the generator of a pointwise
\sigma
-weakly continuous one-parameter group of
\ast
-automorphisms
\tau^{V}:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
on
\mathfrak{M}

[
131
, p. 165]
, called the
perturbed dynamics
.

One can show that for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, the perturbed dynamics
\tau_{t}^{V}(A)
can be expressed in terms of the following series expansion, where the integrals exist in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology and define a norm-convergent series of bounded operators
[
30
, Prop. 5.4.1]
,
[
131
, p. 165]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau_{t}^{V}(A)&=\tau_{t}(A)\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{i}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,\dotsb\int_{0}^{t_{n-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{n}\,\Bigl[\tau_{t_{n}}(V),\bigl[\dotsb,\bigl[\tau_{t_{2}}(V),[\tau_{t_{1}}(V),\tau_{t}(A)]\bigr]\dotsb\bigr]\Bigr]\ .\end{split}

(V.6)

An important tool in perturbation theory is the following one-parameter family
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
,
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)
, of elements in
\mathfrak{M}
, called
Araki-Dyson expansional
, which is defined by

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{M}}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{i}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,\dotsb\int_{0}^{t_{n-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{n}\,\tau_{t_{n}}(V)\dotsm\tau_{t_{2}}(V)\tau_{t_{1}}(V)\ .

(V.7)

One can show that
\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)\bigr)_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
forms a one-parameter family of unitary elements in
\mathfrak{M}
, and that the following relationship with the perturbed dynamics holds true
[
30
, Prop. 5.4.1]
:

\tau_{t}^{V}(A)=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)\,\tau_{t}(A)\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)^{\ast}\ .

If
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and the dynamics
\tau
is unitarily implemented on
\mathcal{H}
, the same follows for the perturbed dynamics
\tau^{V}
, and also the cocycle
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}
is given in terms of a concrete expression.

Proposition V.2.3
(
[
30
, Cor. 5.4.2]
).

Assume that
\tau
is given by
\tau_{t}(A)=U_{t}AU_{t}^{\ast}
, where
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow U_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}
is a strongly continuous unitary group on
\mathcal{H}
with
L:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(L)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
a self-adjoint operator. Then the perturbed dynamics
\tau^{V}
and the expansional
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}
are given for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
A\in\mathfrak{M}
by

\tau_{t}^{V}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\ .

(V.8)

V.2.b  Analytic and Unbounded Perturbations

V.2.4
Analytic perturbations.

(
[
50
, Sect. 3.2]
)
Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system and
V
be a self-adjoint,
\tau
-entire element of
\mathfrak{M}
. In analogy to the expansion (
V.6
) for the perturbed dynamics
\tau^{V}
, one can define for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
A\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
:

\tau_{z}^{V}(A)\coloneqq\tau_{z}(A)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\mathrm{i}z)^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\dotsb\int_{0}^{s_{n-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{n}\,\Bigl[\tau_{s_{n}z}(V),\bigl[\dotsb,[\tau_{s_{1}z}(V),\tau_{z}(A)]\dotsb\bigr]\Bigr]\ .

Note that by assumption, the elements
\tau_{s_{n}z}(V)
and
\tau_{z}(A)
are well-defined. Therefore, it immediately follows that the perturbed dynamics
\tau^{V}
possesses the same analytic elements as
\tau
, that is,
\mathfrak{M}^{\tau^{V}}=\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
. Furthermore, one can define the Araki-Dyson expansionals for all
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
by

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathrm{i}z)^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\dotsb\int_{0}^{s_{n-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{n}\,\tau_{s_{n}z}(V)\dotsm\,\tau_{s_{2}z}(V)\tau_{s_{1}z}(V)\ .

Both of the above series converge uniformly in the norm topology for all
z
in compact sets, and they define analytic functions with values in
\mathfrak{M}

[
50
, p. 461]
.

Proposition V.2.5
(
[
50
, Thm. 3.2]
).

Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and assume that there is a self-adjoint operator
L
on
\mathcal{H}
such that
\tau_{t}(B)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}B\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}
for all
B\in\mathfrak{M}
. Furthermore, let
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
,
A\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
, and
z,z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
be arbitrary. Then the following assertions hold true:

(a)

E_{\tau}^{V}(z)\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
.

(b)

\tau_{z}^{V}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(L+Q)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}z(L+Q)}
.

(c)

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(L+Q)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}zL}
.

(d)

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z)^{-1}=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\overline{z})^{\ast}=\tau_{z}\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-z)\bigr)
.

(e)

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z_{1}+z_{2})=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z_{1})\,\tau_{z_{1}}\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z_{2})\bigr)
.

V.2.6
Unbounded perturbations.

(
[
50
, Sect. 3.3]
)
Let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a von Neumann algebra and
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
implemented by a self-adjoint operator
L
on
\mathcal{H}
(
i.e.
,
\tau_{t}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
A\in\mathfrak{M}
). Consider a self-adjoint operator
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
affiliated with the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
(
cf.

Definition

II.3.12
). The following property shall be assumed in the sequel:

(A1)

L+V
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)
.

Based on this assumption, one may construct the perturbed dynamics also for unbounded perturbations, as the next result illustrates. Its proof is an expanded version of
[
50
, Thm. 3.3]
. In the following, denote the self-adjoint closure
\overline{L+V}
(
cf.

B.2.2
) also by
L+V
for simplicity.

Proposition V.2.7
.

In the setting described above, define for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
A\in\mathfrak{M}
:

\tau_{t}^{V}(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\ .

(V.9)

Then
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tau_{t}^{V}
is a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
which, in the case that the operator
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
is bounded, coincides with the expression (
V.6
).

Proof
.

Let
A\in\mathfrak{M}
be arbitrary. By assumption
(A1)
, one may apply the Trotter product formula (
Theorem

B.3.7
) to obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{V}(A)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)^{n}A\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)^{n}\ .

Since
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}
is unitary, one can insert
n
identity operators
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}ktL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}ktL/n}
,
k\in\{{1,\dotsc,n}\}
, in between the
n
factors of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}
in the first term of the above limit:

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)^{n}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\dotsm\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-(n-1)\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{(n-1)\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\,\tau_{2t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\dotsm\tau_{(n-1)t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\,\tau_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}

\displaystyle=\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tau_{kt/n}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\biggr]\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}\ .

For the third term in the expression for
\tau_{t}^{V}(A)
, one may proceed similarly (but this time inserting the identity expression in a
“descending”
order) and write

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)^{n}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-(n-1)\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{(n-1)\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\dotsm\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL/n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\,\tau_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n})\,\tau_{(n-1)t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n})\dotsm\tau_{t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\,\biggl[\,\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\tau_{(n-k)t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n})\biggr]\ .

Therefore, combining these expression with the limit representation, it follows that

\tau_{t}^{V}(A)=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tau_{kt/n}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\biggr]\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\,\biggl[\,\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\tau_{(n-k)t/n}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV/n})\biggr]\ .

It holds that
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}tV/n}\in\mathfrak{M}
because
V
is affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
, hence
Proposition

II.3.13
applies. Thus, since von Neumann algebras are strongly closed, it follows that
\tau_{t}^{V}(A)\in\mathfrak{M}
. This, together with
Examples

V.1.2

(a)
, shows that
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\tau_{t}^{V}
is a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
, establishing the first assertion. The second statement now follows immediately from
Proposition

V.2.3
.

V.2.8
Expansionals for unbounded perturbations.

(
[
50
, p. 462]
)
Consider the situation described in
V.2.6
, in particular, assume that
(A1)
holds true. Based on
Eq.

V.8
, define the Araki-Dyson expansional
t\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)
for an unbounded self-adjoint
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
by

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\ .

(V.10)

Using again the Trotter product formula,
Theorem

B.3.7
, and performing a similar computation as in the proof of
Proposition

V.2.7
, it follows that
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)
takes the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n}\bigr)^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tau_{kt/n}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV/n})\biggr]\ ,

hence
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)\in\mathfrak{M}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Using the explicit definition (
V.10
) of the expansionals, one can verify the statements of
Proposition

V.2.5
also in the unbounded case
[
50
, Thm. 3.4]
.

V.2.c  Perturbation of Liouvillians

V.2.9
Assumptions.

(
[
50
, Sect. 3.4]
)
Let
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamical system and
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
be a self-adjoint operator affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
. Assume that the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
is represented in standard form
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
, and that
L
is the standard Liouvillian of
\tau
(
cf.

V.1.3
). Define

L_{V}\coloneqq L+V-JVJ\ ,

and suppose that the following assumption is satisfied in addition to
(A1)
from
V.2.6
:

(A2)

L_{V}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\cap\dom(JVJ)
.

The following proposition regarding the operator
L_{V}
is essential for the next section. The proof is a slightly expanded version of
[
50
, Thm. 3.5]
. As before, the closure
\overline{L_{V}}
will be denoted simply by
L_{V}
.

Proposition V.2.10
.

Under the assumptions
(A1)
and
(A2)
, it follows that
L_{V}
is the standard Liouvillian of the perturbed dynamics
\tau^{V}
from (
V.9
).

Proof
.

It will be verified that the operator
L_{V}
satisfies the two properties of
Proposition

V.1.4
. To this end, first note that from the functional calculus for the self-adjoint operator
V
(see
Lemma

B.3.5
), the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV}\in\mathfrak{M}
, and
Definition

III.2.3

(a)
, it follows that

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tJVJ}=J\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tV}J\in\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\ .

(V.11)

Moreover, by definition it holds that
\dom(L+V)=\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)
, hence
\dom(L+V)\cap\dom(JVJ)=\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\cap\dom(JVJ)
. Assumption
(A2)
implies that
L_{V}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L+V)\cap\dom(JVJ)
. Therefore, one may apply the Trotter product formula from
Theorem

B.3.7
to write

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\bigr)^{n}\ .

From this limit representation, the above observation (
V.11
), and the fact that
\tau^{V}
, as defined in
Eq.

V.9
, is indeed a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics (
Proposition

V.2.7
), it follows for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\bigr)^{n}A\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\bigr)^{n}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\biggr]\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\underbrace{\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}A\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\bigr)}_{=A}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n-2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\biggr]\,\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\biggl[\,\prod_{k=1}^{n-2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tJVJ/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}\biggr]

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{{}={}}][c]{\vdots}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathrm{e}^{n\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-n\mathrm{i}t(L+V)/n}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}=\tau_{t}^{V}(A)\ .

This establishes the second property of
Proposition

V.1.4
. To see also the first one, observe that since the operators
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tJVJ}
commute by (
V.11
) and the assumption on
V
, the same follows for
V
and
JVJ

[
134
, Thm. VIII.13]
, hence one can write

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(V-JVJ)}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tJVJ}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV}J\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV}J=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV}j(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tV})\ .

Therefore,
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(V-JVJ)}\,\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
by
Definition

III.2.3

(d)
. Furthermore, from
Proposition

V.1.4
it follows that
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL}\,\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
as
L
is the standard Liouvillian of
\tau
. Since
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V-JVJ)=\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\cap\dom(JVJ)
, the operator
L_{V}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V-JVJ)
, and hence one can apply Trotter’s formula (
B.2
) once more to write

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(V-JVJ)/n}\bigr)^{n}\ .

(V.12)

This, together with the previous observations regarding
\mathcal{P}
and the fact that the latter is a closed subset of
\mathcal{H}
, implies that
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. Therefore,
L_{V}
also satisfies the first property in
Proposition

V.1.4
, hence it must be the unique standard Liouvillian of the dynamics
\tau^{V}
.

The following auxiliary lemma, whose proof is inspired by
[
131
, Thm. 4.70]
, will be used below in the perturbation theory of KMS-states.

Lemma V.2.11
.

Assume that
(A1)
is satisfied. Then the operator
L-JVJ
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(JVJ)
.

Proof
.

For brevity, introduce the notation
W\coloneqq JVJ
. Then it holds that

\dom(W)=J\dom(V)\ .

To see this, first let
\eta\in\dom(W)=\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}:J\xi\in\dom(V)}\}
. Since
J^{2}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
(
Proposition

III.1.8

(a)
), it follows that
\eta=J(J\eta)
with
J\eta\in\dom(V)
, hence
\eta\in J\dom(V)
. Conversely, if
\eta\in J\dom(V)
, then
\eta=J\xi
for some
\xi\in\dom(V)
. Therefore,
J\eta=J^{2}\xi=\xi\in\dom(V)
, and so
\eta\in\dom(W)
. This shows equality of the domains. Similarly, there holds the relation

\dom(L)=J\dom(L)\ .

For its proof, recall the relation
JL+LJ=0
from
V.1.3

(a)
. Then, if
\xi\in\dom(L)
, it follows that
JL\xi=-LJ\xi
, hence
J\xi\in\dom(L)
and
\xi=J(J\xi)\in J\dom(L)
. Conversely,
\eta\in J\dom(L)
implies
\eta=J\xi
for
\xi\in\dom(L)
, hence
L\eta=LJ\xi=-JL\xi
. This shows that
\eta\in\dom(L)
, and thus equality of the domains. Furthermore, from the relation
JL+LJ=0
one also obtains that

L-W=-JLJ-JVJ=-J(L+V)J\ ,

where the domain of this operator is given by

\dom(L-W)=\dom(L)\cap\dom(W)=J\bigl(\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\bigr)\ ,

and if
\eta=J\xi\in\dom(L-W)
for
\xi\in\dom(L+V)
, then the action of the operator can be written as
(L-W)\eta=-J(L+V)J\eta=-J(L+V)\xi
. By assumption
(A1)
from
V.2.6
, the operator
L+V
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)
. Since
J
is a bounded, self-adjoint, bijective operator, this shows that
L-W
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(W)

[
152
, Prop. 8.11]
.

The following properties of the complex expansional will also be needed in the sequel.

Proposition V.2.12
(
[
50
, Thm. 3.6]
).

Let
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. Then for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}zL_{V}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}z(L-JVJ)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}zL_{V}}=J\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\overline{z})J\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}zL}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-z)^{-1}\ .

(V.13)

V.3
Perturbation of KMS-States

In this section, given a
faithful

(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
and a perturbation
V
, a state
\omega_{V}\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
shall be constructed which is a KMS-state for the
perturbed
dynamics
\tau^{V}
. In the first step, this will be done for analytic perturbations
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
, and then the framework will be extended successively to bounded self-adjoint
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
and unbounded self-adjoint
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
.

V.3.a  Bounded Perturbations
