Below, the main results for bounded perturbations are collected; they are taken from
[
50
, Thm. 5.1]
, and their proofs will be given in
Sections

V.3.b
and
V.3.c
. Originally, these results were obtained by
H. Araki
in 1973
[
5
,
6
]
. However, the method of proof employed in
Section

V.3.c
was developed by
J. Dereziński
,
V. Jakšić
, and
C.-A. Pillet
in
[
50
, Sect. 5]
.

Proposition V.3.1
(Existence of bounded perturbations).

Let
\beta>0
,
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form,
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
L
, and
\omega
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state with vector representative
\varOmega\in\mathcal{P}
. Then for every
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, it holds that

\varOmega\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2})\ .

Definition V.3.2
(Perturbed KMS-state).

In the notation of the previous proposition, define the following vector
\varOmega_{V}\in\mathcal{H}
and state
\omega_{V}\in\varSigma(\mathfrak{M})
:

\varOmega_{V}\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\,\varOmega\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{V}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}}\,\langle{\varOmega_{V},\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \varOmega_{V}}\rangle\ .

Theorem V.3.3
(Properties of
\varOmega_{V}
and
\omega_{V}
for bounded
V
).

Consider the situation described in
Proposition

V.3.1
. Then for every
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, the following properties of the perturbed vector
\varOmega_{V}
and the perturbed state
\omega_{V}
hold true:

(a)

\varOmega_{V}\in\mathcal{P}
.

(b)

\varOmega_{V}
is cyclic and separating for
\mathfrak{M}
.

(c)

The state
\omega_{V}
is a
(\tau^{V},\beta)
-KMS-state on
\mathfrak{M}
.

(d)

The modular operator of
(\mathfrak{M},\varOmega_{V})
satisfies
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}})=-\beta L_{V}
.

(e)

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V
.

(f)

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}})+\beta V
.

(g)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\omega_{V})=\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)+\beta\omega(V)
.

(h)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{V},\omega)=-\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)-\beta\omega_{V}(V)
.

Another very important identity involving the perturbed vector
\varOmega_{V}
is the famous
Golden-Thompson inequality
which is a crucial element for the proof of the above results.

Proposition V.3.4
(Golden-Thompson inequality).

Consider the situation of
Proposition

V.3.1
. For every
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, the following inequality holds true:

\|\varOmega_{V}\|\leq\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|\ .

(V.14)

Remark V.3.5
.

The Golden-Thompson inequality, which was first discussed in
[
71
,
157
,
161
]
and extended to the form (
V.14
) by
H. Araki

[
5
, Thm. 2]
, is an important tool in statistical mechanics
[
18
]
,
[
150
, Sect. 8.1]
. It is usually formulated as follows: for two positive self-adjoint operators
A
and
B
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
B
is relatively
A
-bounded with
A
-bound strictly less than one
[
144
, Def. 8.1]
, and such that
\mathrm{e}^{-A},\mathrm{e}^{-B}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, there holds
[
31
, Thm. 1]

\tr\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-(A+B)}\bigr)\leq\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-A}\,\mathrm{e}^{-B})\ .

The plan for the proof of the above results, which will be followed in the next two subsections, can be summarized as follows:

V.3.1
for analytic
V
V.3.3
for analytic
V
V.3.4
for analytic
V
V.3.4
for bounded
V
V.3.3
for bounded
V
V.3.1
for bounded
V

V.3.b  Proof for Analytic Perturbations

Let
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
, and let
\tau^{V}
and
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}
be the perturbed dynamics and Araki-Dyson expansional from
V.2.4
, respectively. Recall that according to
Proposition

V.2.10
,
L_{V}=L+V-JVJ
is the standard Liouvillian of
\tau^{V}
. The following proofs are taken from
[
50
, Sect. 5.2]
, except the one of
Theorem

V.3.3

(f)
which is independent.

Proof
(of
Proposition

V.3.1
).

The standard Liouvillian
L
of
\tau
satisfies
L\varOmega=0
,
cf.

V.1.3

(b)
, hence
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\varOmega=\varOmega
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
by
Lemma

B.3.6
. Thus, using the spatial representation
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}
of the Araki-Dyson expansional from
Eq.

V.8
, one obtains

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\varOmega\ .

According to
V.2.4
, the expansional
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(t)
possesses an analytic continuation to an entire function
\mathbb{C}\owns z\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z)\in\mathfrak{M}
. Hence, it follows that
\varOmega\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(L+V)})
and
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(z)\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(L+V)}\varOmega
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
. In particular, choosing
z=\mathrm{i}\beta/2
, this shows that

\varOmega_{V}\coloneqq\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\,\varOmega

(V.15)

is a well-defined vector in
\mathcal{H}
, so it must be an element of the domain of the operator
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}
.

Proof
(of
Theorem

V.3.3
).

Ad

(a)
. Consider the element
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
. From
Proposition

V.2.5

(d)
and
(e)
, it follows by choosing
z_{1}=z_{2}=\mathrm{i}\beta/4
that

\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4+\mathrm{i}\beta/4)=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\,\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta/4}\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\bigr)=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\,\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta/2}\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)^{\ast}\bigr)\ .

Combining this identity with
Eq.

V.15
, one obtains

\displaystyle\varOmega_{V}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\,\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta/2}\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)^{\ast}\bigr)\varOmega

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)^{\ast}\,\varOmega

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\,J\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)J\varOmega\ .

In the second step, it was used that
\mathrm{e}^{\beta L/2}\varOmega=\varOmega
, and in the third,
Proposition

V.1.13
was employed. This shows that
\varOmega_{V}=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\,j\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/4)\bigr)\varOmega
, hence
\varOmega_{V}\in\mathcal{P}
according to
Proposition

III.2.2

(d)
.

Ad

(b)
.
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)
is an invertible element in
\mathfrak{M}
by
Proposition

V.2.5

(d)
. Therefore, since
\varOmega
is cyclic and separating for
\mathfrak{M}
by assumption (recall that
\omega
is faithful, hence
Remark

III.2.9
applies), it follows from
Eq.

V.15
that
\varOmega_{V}
is cyclic and separating, too.

Ad

(c)
&
(d)
. From the second identity in (
V.13
), one obtains by choosing
z=\mathrm{i}\beta/2
that

\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}/2}=J\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,J\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{-1}\ .

Proposition

V.1.13
implies that
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega\subseteq\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2})
since
\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}
is a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state, hence
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega\subseteq\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}/2})
as well by the above identity. Since
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega_{V}=\mathfrak{M}\varOmega
according to (
V.15
), one can conclude that
\mathfrak{M}\varOmega_{V}\subseteq\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}/2})
. Furthermore, for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
one computes

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}/2}A\varOmega_{V}

\displaystyle=J\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,J\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2}\,\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{-1}A\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\bigr)\varOmega

\displaystyle=J\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,J^{2}\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{-1}A\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\bigr)^{\ast}\varOmega

\displaystyle=J\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{-1}A^{\ast}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,\varOmega

\displaystyle=JA^{\ast}\varOmega_{V}\ ,

where in the second line
Eq.

V.3
, and in the third line
Proposition

V.2.5

(d)
was used. This shows that both conditions of
Proposition

V.1.13
are satisfied, hence
\omega_{V}
is a
(\tau^{V},\beta)
-KMS-state. In light of
(b)
proved above, the aforementioned proposition also shows that
\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}}
.

Ad

(e)
. Using the definition (
III.8
) of the relative Tomita operator
S_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega}
and the definition (
III.1
) of the Tomita operator
S_{\varOmega}
(noting that both are well-defined because
\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}\in\mathcal{P}
are cyclic and separating), as well as the definition (
V.15
) of the perturbed vector, one obtains for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
:

S_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega}A\varOmega=A^{\ast}\varOmega_{V}=A^{\ast}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,\varOmega=\bigl(\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}A\bigr)^{\ast}\varOmega=S_{\varOmega}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}A\varOmega\ .

This implies that
S_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega}=S_{\varOmega}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}
. Therefore, using the definitions (
III.9
) and (
III.3
) of the relative modular operator
\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega}
and modular operator
\varDelta_{\varOmega}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega}

\displaystyle=\bigl(S_{\varOmega}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}\bigr)^{\ast}S_{\varOmega}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,S_{\varOmega}^{\ast}S_{\varOmega}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)\,\varDelta_{\varOmega}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}\ .

Introducing the identity
\varDelta_{\varOmega}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L}
from
Proposition

V.1.13
and writing, as before,
\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta L/2}
according to
Proposition

V.2.5

(c)
, one furthermore obtains

\displaystyle\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta L/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta L/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\bigr)=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)}\ .

Thus, taking the logarithm of this expression and inserting again
Eq.

V.4
yields the assertion:

\displaystyle\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega})

\displaystyle=-\beta(L+V)=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V\ .

Ad

(f)
. Proceeding similarly as in the previous part of the proof, it follows from
Eq.

V.15
and
Proposition

V.2.5

(d)
that

\displaystyle S_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}}A\varOmega_{V}

\displaystyle=A^{\ast}\varOmega=A^{\ast}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{-1}\varOmega_{V}=A^{\ast}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}\varOmega_{V}=S_{\varOmega_{V}}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)A\varOmega_{V}

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, hence
S_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}}=S_{\varOmega_{V}}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)
. Therefore, using the first identity in
Eq.

V.13
and the relation
\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}}
proved above in
(d)
, one computes

\displaystyle\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)^{\ast}\,S_{\varOmega_{V}}^{\ast}S_{\varOmega_{V}}\,\mathrm{E}_{\tau}^{V}(-\mathrm{i}\beta/2)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\beta L_{V}/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L-JVJ)/2}\bigr)^{\ast}\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\beta L_{V}/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L-JVJ)/2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L-JVJ)/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta L_{V}/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta L_{V}/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L-JVJ)/2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L-JVJ)}\ .

Since the perturbed Liouvillian
L_{V}
is given by
L_{V}=L+V-JVJ
, one has
L-JVJ=L_{V}-V
, and this operator is essentially self-adjoint on its natural domain by
Lemma

V.2.11
, thus
\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L_{V}-V)}
; taking the logarithm yields the desired expression:

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}})=-\beta L_{V}+\beta V=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}})+\beta V\ .

Ad

(g)
&
(h)
. Define the operator
\widetilde{V}\coloneqq V+\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
which is still self-adjoint and
\tau
-entire. According to
Eq.

V.15
, the
\widetilde{V}
-perturbation of the vector
\varOmega
is given by

\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+\widetilde{V})/2}\,\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\log\|\varOmega_{V}\|}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)}\varOmega=\frac{\varOmega_{V}}{\|\varOmega_{V}\|}\ ,

and hence the corresponding state
\omega_{\widetilde{V}}
(see
Definition

V.3.2
) takes the form

\omega_{\widetilde{V}}(A)=\frac{1}{\big\|{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\big\|^{2}}\,\big\langle{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}},A\,\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\big\rangle=\langle{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}},A\,\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}}\,\langle{\varOmega_{V},A\,\varOmega_{V}}\rangle=\omega_{V}(A)

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}
. Employing the identity
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V
derived above in
(e)
, one obtains a corresponding relation for
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{\tilde{V}},\varOmega})
:

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{\tilde{V}},\varOmega})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta\widetilde{V}=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V-\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

This and the fact that
\omega_{V}=\omega_{\widetilde{V}}
imply the following identity for the relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\omega_{V})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\omega_{V})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\omega_{\widetilde{V}})=-\langle{\varOmega,\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{\tilde{V}},\varOmega})\varOmega}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle{\varOmega,\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})\varOmega}\rangle+\beta\langle{\varOmega,V\varOmega}\rangle+\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\langle{\varOmega,\varOmega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\beta\omega(V)+\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

To get from the second to the third line, it was used that
\varDelta_{\varOmega}\varOmega=\varOmega
(
Proposition

III.1.8

(e)
), hence
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})\varOmega=0
by
Lemma

B.3.6
, and that the vector
\varOmega
is normalized by assumption. This proves the first identity. For the second one, note that with
\widetilde{V}
as above, it follows from
(f)
that

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{\tilde{V}}})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{\tilde{V}}})+\beta\widetilde{V}=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{\tilde{V}}})+\beta V+\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

Therefore, the relative entropy takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{V},\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{\widetilde{V}},\omega)=-\big\langle{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}},\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{\tilde{V}}})\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\big\langle{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}},\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{\tilde{V}}})\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\big\rangle-\beta\big\langle{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}},V\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\big\rangle-\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\big\langle{\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}},\varOmega_{\widetilde{V}}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\beta\omega_{\widetilde{V}}(V)-\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

Proof
(of
Proposition

V.3.4
).

Denote by
\mathfrak{N}
the commutative von Neumann subalgebra of
\mathfrak{M}
generated by the perturbation
V
. According to monotonicity of the relative entropy under restriction of the functionals to a subalgebra (
Corollary

IV.3.9
), it holds that

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{N}}^{\mathrm{std}}\bigl(\omega_{V}\big|_{\mathfrak{N}},\omega\big|_{\mathfrak{N}}\bigr)\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{V},\omega)\ .

Moreover, because
-\beta V\in\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}
is a self-adjoint element in the center of
\mathfrak{N}
(since
\mathfrak{N}
is Abelian,
\mathfrak{N}\subseteq\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}
), one can employ the inequality (
IV.5
) from
Lemma

IV.1.10
to obtain

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{N}}^{\mathrm{std}}\bigl(\omega_{V}\big|_{\mathfrak{N}},\omega\big|_{\mathfrak{N}}\bigr)+\beta\omega_{V}(V)\geq-\log\omega(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V})\ .

Combining the above two inequalities with
Theorem

V.3.3

(h)
for analytic perturbations, it follows that

\displaystyle\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{V},\omega)-\beta\omega_{V}(V)

\displaystyle\leq-\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{N}}^{\mathrm{std}}\bigl(\omega_{V}\big|_{\mathfrak{N}},\omega\big|_{\mathfrak{N}}\bigr)-\beta\omega_{V}(V)

\displaystyle\leq\log\omega(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V})

\displaystyle=\log\bigl(\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

Exponentiating this inequality and taking the square root,
Eq.

V.14
follows.

V.3.c  Proof for Bounded Perturbations

Now, following
[
50
, Sect. 5.3]
, the assertions of
Section

V.3.a
shall be proved for
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. The general strategy of the proofs consists in taking a sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
converging strongly to
V
, which exists by
Theorem

V.1.9
, and then using certain limit arguments to reduce the problem to the situation of
Section

V.3.b
. In this spirit, first an auxiliary result is needed which is stated in
[
50
, Lem. 5.1]
.

Lemma V.3.6
.

Let
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
be a sequence of self-adjoint,
\tau
-entire elements converging strongly to
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. Then the following two properties are satisfied:

(a)

L+V_{n}\rightarrow L+V
in the strong resolvent sense.

(b)

L_{V_{n}}\rightarrow L_{V}
in the strong resolvent sense.

Proof
.

Only
(a)
shall be proved in detail; the argument to establish
(b)
is similar (see
Lemma

V.3.10
below). Using the second resolvent identity
[
119
, p. 34]
,
[
170
, Thm. 5.13]
, it follows for all
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
that

\displaystyle\big\|{(L+V_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\xi-(L+V-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\xi}\big\|

\displaystyle=\big\|{(L+V_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}(V-V_{n})(L+V-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\xi}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|{(L+V_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\big\|{(V-V_{n})(L+V-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\xi}\big\|\ .

Since
L+V_{n}
is self-adjoint, it holds that
\|(L+V_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1

[
119
, Thm. 2.2.17]
. Furthermore, as
V_{n}\rightarrow V
in the strong operator topology by assumption, the right-hand side in the above inequality tends to zero as
n\rightarrow+\infty
, showing that
(L+V_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\rightarrow(L+V-\mathrm{i})^{-1}
strongly. By
Definition

B.3.8
, this implies
L+V_{n}\rightarrow L+V
in the strong resolvent sense.

Proof
(of
Proposition

V.3.1
).

From
Theorem

V.1.9
, it follows that there exists a sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
of self-adjoint,
\tau
-entire elements such that
V_{n}\rightarrow V
strongly. This implies
L+V_{n}\rightarrow L+V
in the strong resolvent sense by
Lemma

V.3.6

(a)
. Moreover, it holds that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega

(V.16)

since
V_{n}\rightarrow V
also in the strong resolvent sense (
Proposition

B.3.10
), hence
Proposition

B.3.9
applies. Therefore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\big\|{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega}\big\|\leq C\ .

Furthermore, using the Golden-Thompson inequality (
V.14
) for analytic perturbations,

\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|\leq\big\|{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega}\big\|\ .

Combining these two inequalities yields
\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|\leq C
. From
Proposition

B.3.12
applied to
T_{n}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V_{n})/2}
,
T\equiv\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}
,
\varOmega_{n}\equiv\varOmega
, noting that
T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong resolvent sense by
Lemma

V.3.6

(a)
and
Lemma

B.3.14
and
\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V_{n})/2}\varOmega\|\leq C
according to (
V.15
), one obtains
\varOmega\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2})
and

\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V_{n})/2}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\varOmega\ .

Proof
(of
Theorem

V.3.3
).

As in the proof of
Proposition

V.3.1
, one may assume, by virtue of
Theorem

V.1.9
, that there exists a sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
of self-adjoint,
\tau
-entire elements such that
V_{n}\rightarrow V
in the strong operator topology. Note that, as shown above,

\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\varOmega_{V_{n}}=\varOmega_{V}\ .

(V.17)

Ad

(a)
. According to
Section

V.3.b
, it holds that
\varOmega_{V_{n}}\in\mathcal{P}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since

\mathcal{P}
is weakly closed by
Proposition

III.2.2

(a)
and Mazur’s theorem
[
166
, Cor. 2.11]
, (
V.17
) implies the assertion.

Ad

(b)
. Define

P\coloneqq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{s}(\omega_{V})\in\mathscr{P}(\mathfrak{M})
. It holds that
P\varOmega_{V}=0
and
\tau_{t}^{V}(P)=P
. To see the latter identity, note that
\tau^{V}
is implemented by
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}
(
Proposition

V.2.10
), and that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}\varOmega_{V}=\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL_{V_{n}}}\varOmega_{V_{n}}
according to
Lemma

V.3.6

(b)
,
Proposition

B.3.9
, and
Eq.

V.17
. Furthermore, since
L_{V_{n}}\varOmega_{V_{n}}=0
by
Theorem

V.3.3

(d)
for analytic perturbations and
Proposition

III.1.8

(e)
, it follows from
Lemma

B.3.6
that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tL_{V}}\varOmega_{V}=\varOmega_{V}
. Therefore,
\omega_{V}
is
\tau^{V}
-invariant, and hence
\tau_{t}^{V}(\mathbf{s}(\omega_{V}))=\mathbf{s}(\omega_{V})

[
50
, p. 454]
. For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, define a vector

\varOmega(z)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{-z(L+V)}\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}\ .

One can show that the function
z\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\varOmega(z)
is analytic in the strip
0<\operatorname{Re}(z)<\beta/2
and norm continuous on its closure
[
50
, Prop. A.1]
,
[
5
, Lem. 3]
. Furthermore,
\varOmega(\beta/2)=\varOmega_{V}
by
Definition

V.3.2
and

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}P\varOmega(\mathrm{i}t+\beta/2)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}P\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}\varOmega(\beta/2)=\tau_{t}^{V}(P)\varOmega_{V}=P\varOmega_{V}=0\ .

This shows that
P\varOmega(\mathrm{i}t+\beta/2)=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The three-line theorem
[
20
, Lem. 1.1.2]
,
[
44
, Thm. 3.7]
implies
P\varOmega(z)=0
for every
z
in the strip
0\leq\operatorname{Re}(z)\leq\beta/2
. In particular,
P\varOmega(0)=P\varOmega=0
. Since
\varOmega
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
by the assumption on
\omega
(
cf.

Remark

III.2.9
), it follows that
P=0
. Therefore,
\mathbf{s}(\omega_{V})=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and hence
\varOmega_{V}
is separating for
\mathfrak{M}
as well, see
Proposition

II.4.12
and
Corollary

II.4.14
. Finally, since
\varOmega_{V}\in\mathcal{P}
by assertion
(a)
, using
Lemma

III.2.7
one concludes that
\varOmega_{V}
is cyclic for
\mathfrak{M}
.

Ad

(c)
. It was shown in
Section

V.3.b
that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the state
\omega_{V_{n}}
is a
(\tau^{V_{n}},\beta)
-KMS-state. In particular, this implies that
\omega_{V_{n}}
is
\tau^{V_{n}}
-invariant (see
[
30
, Prop. 5.3.3]
,
[
83
, Lem. 2.12]
or
[
131
, Thm. 5.3]
). Moreover,
L_{V_{n}}
is the standard Liouvillian of
\tau^{V_{n}}
(
Proposition

V.2.10
), and it holds that
L_{V_{n}}\rightarrow L_{V}
in the strong resolvent sense according to
Lemma

V.3.6

(b)
. Therefore, all assumptions of
Proposition

V.1.14
are satisfied; it implies that
\omega_{\varOmega_{V}/\|\varOmega_{V}\|}=\omega_{V}
is a
(\tau^{V},\beta)
-KMS-state.

Ad

(d)
. As in the analytic case, the assertion follows from the statements
(b)
and
(c)
, as well as from
Proposition

V.1.13
.

Ad

(e)
&
(f)
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V_{n}},\varOmega})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V_{n}=-\beta(L+V_{n})
as shown in
Section

V.3.b
. From
Lemma

V.3.6

(a)
, it follows that
L+V_{n}\rightarrow L+V
in the strong resolvent sense, hence
\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V_{n}},\varOmega}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V_{n})}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)}
in the strong resolvent sense by
Lemma

B.3.14
. With this observation,
Lemma

III.3.9
implies that
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega})=-\beta(L+V)=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V
. The proof of the other identity proceeds analogously.

Ad

(g)
&
(h)
. These assertions follow from the previously shown identities
(e)
and
(f)
in exactly the same way as in the analytic case.

Proof
(of
Proposition

V.3.4
).

As before, let
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
be a sequence of self-adjoint,
\tau
-entire elements such that
V_{n}\rightarrow V
strongly. It was argued above in the proof of
Proposition

V.3.1
that in this case,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega
,
cf.

Eq.

V.16
. This implies

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega\|=\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|\ .

Furthermore, since
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\varOmega_{V_{n}}=\varOmega_{V}
by
Eq.

V.17
, it follows that

\|\varOmega_{V}\|\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|

since the norm of
\mathcal{H}
is weakly lower semi-continuous (
cf.

Definition

C.1.3
) by the Hahn-Banach theorem.
2
2

2

Let
(X,\|\cdot\|)
be a Banach space, let
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
be a weakly convergent sequence with weak limit
x\in X\setminus\{0\}
, and let
f\in X^{\ast}
such that
\|f\|_{\mathrm{op}}=1
and
\|x\|=f(x)
which exists by the Hahn-Banach theorem
[
171
, Cor. III.1.6]
. Then
\|x\|=f(x)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(x_{n})\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|f\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|x_{n}\|=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|x_{n}\|
.

Applying the Golden-Thompson inequality (
V.14
) for analytic perturbations to the perturbed vector
\varOmega_{V_{n}}
, one obtains

\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|\leq\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega\|\ .

Combining these three identities, the asserted inequality follows:

\|\varOmega_{V}\|\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega\|=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega\|=\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|\ .

V.3.d  Unbounded Perturbations

The results of
Section

V.3.a
shall now be generalized to unbounded self-adjoint perturbations
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
. The strategy is similar as before:
V
will be approximated by a suitable sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
such that one can reduce the problem to an application of the results for bounded perturbations. This framework was developed by
Dereziński
,
Jakšić
, and
Pillet
in
[
50
, Sect. 5.5]
.

V.3.7
Assumptions.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra represented in standard form, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
L
, and let
\beta>0
and
\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
be a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state with vector representative
\varOmega\in\mathcal{P}
. Consider a self-adjoint operator
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
, and assume that the following two properties are satisfied:

(A1)

L+V
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)
.

(A2)

L_{V}\coloneqq L+V-JVJ
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\cap\dom(JVJ)
.

It was shown in
Proposition

V.2.7
that under the first assumption,
\tau_{t}^{V}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(L+V)}
defines a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
. Assuming additionally
(A2)
,
Proposition

V.2.10
showed that
L_{V}=L+V-JVJ
is the standard Liouvillian of
\tau^{V}
. To construct KMS-states also for unbounded perturbations, an additional assumption is required
[
50
, p. 479]
:

(A3)

\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|<+\infty
.

Introduce the following class of
“DJP-perturbations”
to simplify notation:

\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}\ :\ \parbox{207.0021pt}{\centering$V$ self-adjoint and satisfies assumptions \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A1}, \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A2}, and \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A3} with respect to $L$ and $\varOmega$\@add@centering}}}\right\}\ .

To obtain an analogue of
Theorem

V.3.3
, first an approximating sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
for
V
will be constructed, and then a corresponding version of
Lemma

V.3.6
will be proved.

V.3.8
Approximation of unbounded operators.

(
[
50
, p. 479]
)
Let
V:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(V)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be an unbounded self-adjoint operator affiliated with the von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
. Denote by
E_{V}
the unique spectral measure associated with
V
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define an operator

V_{n}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{[-n,n]}(V)V\ .

From
Proposition

B.3.3
, it follows that
V_{n}
can be written as

V_{n}=\mathbf{1}_{[-n,n]}(V)\,\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}(V)=\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{[-n,n]}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}\bigr)(V)=\int_{\sigma(V)\cap[-n,n]}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}E_{V}(\lambda)\ .

(V.18)

Define a function
f_{n}:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
f_{n}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{[-n,n]}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}
. It holds that
\|f_{n}\|_{\infty}=n
, hence
V_{n}=f_{n}(V)
is a bounded operator. In particular, since
V
is affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
, it follows that
V_{n}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
by
Proposition

II.3.13
.

Lemma V.3.9
.

Let
\xi\in\dom(V)
be arbitrary. Then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}V_{n}\xi=V\xi
.

Proof
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the function
g_{n}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-n,n]}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}=\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}-f_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mu_{\xi}^{V}
denote the positive measure on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\sigma(V)
given by
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\langle{\xi,E_{V}(A)\xi}\rangle
. From properties of the functional calculus for the operator
V
(
Proposition

B.3.3
), it follows that

\displaystyle\|V\xi-V_{n}\xi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|{(\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}-f_{n})(V)\,\xi}\big\|^{2}=\int_{\sigma(V)}|g_{n}(\lambda)|^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}^{V}(\lambda)=\int_{\sigma(V)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-n,n]}(\lambda)\,|\lambda|^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}^{V}(\lambda)\ .

Since
\xi\in\dom(V)
, it holds that
\int_{\sigma(V)}|\lambda|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}^{V}(\lambda)<+\infty
(
Definition

B.3.2
). Moreover, as
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-n,n]}
converges pointwise to the zero function as
n\rightarrow+\infty
and
|g_{n}|^{2}\leq|\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}|^{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
on
\sigma(V)
, it follows from Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem
[
40
, Thm. 2.4.5]
that

\|V\xi-V_{n}\xi\|^{2}=\int_{\sigma(V)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-n,n]}\,|\lambda|^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}^{V}(\lambda)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow 0\ .

The first two assertions of the next auxiliary result are from
[
50
, Thm. 5.6]
.

Lemma V.3.10
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega)
and
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an approximating sequence as in
V.3.8
.

(a)

L+V_{n}\rightarrow L+V
in the strong resolvent sense.

(b)

L_{V_{n}}\rightarrow L_{V}
in the strong resolvent sense.

(c)

-L_{V_{n}}+V_{n}\rightarrow-L_{V}+V
in the strong resolvent sense.

Proof
.

The proof of assertion
(a)
is similar to the one for
Lemma

V.3.6

(a)
.

Ad

(b)
Define the set
\mathcal{D}_{0}\coloneqq\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\cap\dom(JVJ)
. According to assumption
(A2)
, the operator
L_{V}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Furthermore, it holds that
L_{V_{n}}\xi\rightarrow L_{V}\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
by
Lemma

V.3.9
. Therefore, the assertion follows from an application of
Proposition

B.3.11
.

Ad

(c)
. By definition of the operators
L_{V_{n}}
and
L_{V}
, it holds that
-L_{V_{n}}+V_{n}=-L+JV_{n}J
and
-L_{V}+V=-L+JVJ
. According to
Lemma

V.2.11
, the second operator is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathcal{D}_{1}\coloneqq\dom(L)\cap\dom(JVJ)=J\bigl(\dom(L)\cap\dom(V)\bigr)
. Furthermore,
Lemma

V.3.9
implies that for all
\xi=J\eta\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, there holds
(-L_{V_{n}}+V_{n})\xi=-L\xi+JV_{n}\eta\rightarrow-L\xi+JV\eta=(-L_{V}+V)\xi
. Hence, the claim follows again from
Proposition

B.3.11
.

The following theorem is taken from
[
50
, Thm. 5.5]
, except assertion
(g)
which is independent.

Theorem V.3.11
(Properties of
\varOmega_{V}
and
\omega_{V}
for unbounded
V
).

Let
\beta>0
be arbitrary, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a von Neumann algebra represented in standard form
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
L
, and let
\omega
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state with vector representative
\varOmega\in\mathcal{P}
. Then for every
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega)
, the following properties are satisfied.

(a)

\varOmega\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2})
. Define
\varOmega_{V}
and
\omega_{V}
as in
Definition

V.3.2
.

(b)

\varOmega_{V}\in\mathcal{P}
.

(c)

\varOmega_{V}
is cyclic and separating for
\mathfrak{M}
.

(d)

\omega_{V}
is a
(\tau^{V},\beta)
-KMS-state.

(e)

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}})=-\beta L_{V}
.

(f)

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V},\varOmega})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega})-\beta V
.

(g)

\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}})+\beta V
.

Proof
.

Ad

(a)
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let the bounded operator
V_{n}=f_{n}(V)\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
be defined as in
V.3.8
. According to assumption
(A3)
, it holds that
\varOmega\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2})
. Therefore, employing essentially the same argument as in the proof of
Lemma

V.3.9
, it follows that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\ .

This shows that there exists
C>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega\|\leq C
. From the Golden-Thompson inequality (
V.14
) for bounded perturbations, one obtains

\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|\leq\big\|{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega}\big\|\ .

Therefore,
\|\varOmega_{V_{n}}\|\leq C
, and applying
Proposition

B.3.12
gives
\varOmega\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2})
as well as

\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V_{n})/2}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\varOmega\ .

By virtue of
Lemma

V.3.10
, all of the remaining assertions follow from their bounded versions in precisely the same way as the corresponding statements of
Theorem

V.3.3
followed from their analytic versions. This shall be illustrated explicitly only for
(g)
.

Ad

(g)
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V_{n}}})=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V_{n}}})+\beta V_{n}=-\beta L_{V_{n}}+\beta V_{n}
according to
Theorem

V.3.3

(d)
and
(f)
. Furthermore,
Lemma

V.3.10

(c)
shows that
-L_{V_{n}}+V_{n}\rightarrow-L_{V}+V
in the strong resolvent sense, hence
\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V_{n}}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V_{n}}+\beta V_{n}}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{-\beta L_{V}+\beta V}
in the strong resolvent sense by
Lemma

B.3.14
. Since
\varOmega_{V_{n}}\rightarrow\varOmega_{V}
weakly in
\mathcal{H}
by
(a)
,
Lemma

III.3.9
and assertion
(e)
imply that
\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega,\varOmega_{V}})=-\beta L_{V}+\beta V=\log(\varDelta_{\varOmega_{V}})+\beta V
. (Note that
\mathbf{s}(\omega_{V_{n}})=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathbf{s}(\omega_{V})=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
since the vectors
\varOmega_{V_{n}},\varOmega_{V}
are separating, hence assumption
(b)
of
Lemma

III.3.9
is also satisfied.)

V.3.12
Additional assumptions.

The generalization of the properties stated in
Theorem

V.3.3

(g)
and
(h)
is a bit more subtle. In fact, another assumption is necessary to obtain the latter identity, while the former can be obtained using only
(A1)
–
(A3)
. Before introducing this additional assumption, an important consequence of
(A3)
shall be discussed.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
be a self-adjoint operator affiliated with
\mathfrak{M}
. Then
V
can be decomposed into the sum
V=V_{+}+V_{-}
, where
V_{+}
is the
positive part
of
V
and
V_{-}
is the
negative part
of
V
which are given by the expression

\displaystyle V_{+}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{(0,+\infty)}(V)V=\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{(0,+\infty)}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}\bigr)(V)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{-}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(V)V=\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}\bigr)(V)\ .

Consider
\beta>0
and a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state
\omega
on
\mathfrak{M}
as given in
Theorem

V.3.11
. Using
Proposition

B.3.3

(a)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\beta\omega(V_{-})=\big\langle{\varOmega,\bigl(\beta\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{\sigma(V)}\bigr)(V)\varOmega}\big\rangle=\int_{\sigma(V)\cap(-\infty,0]}\beta\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varOmega}^{V}(\lambda)\ .

One can estimate the integral by using that
x>-\mathrm{e}^{-x}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
which is, in essence, a consequence of Bernoulli’s inequality.
3
3

3

Indeed, from the limit representation
\mathrm{e}^{x}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}(1+x/n)^{n}
and the Bernoulli inequality
(1+x)^{n}\geq 1+nx
, which is valid for all
x\geq-1
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

[
65
, § 3, Thm. 2]
, it follows that
\mathrm{e}^{x}\geq 1+x
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. (The validity of this inequality for
x<-1
is clear.) Hence, substituting
x\rightarrow-x
, one obtains
\mathrm{e}^{-x}\geq 1-x
, and so
-\mathrm{e}^{-x}\leq-1+x<x
.

With this, it follows that

\displaystyle\beta\omega(V_{-})

\displaystyle>-\int_{\sigma(V)\cap(-\infty,0]}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\lambda}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varOmega}^{V}(\lambda)\geq-\int_{\sigma(T)}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\lambda}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varOmega}^{V}(\lambda)\ ,

where it was used that
\int_{\sigma(T)\cap(0,+\infty)}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\lambda}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varOmega}^{V}(\lambda)\geq 0
since
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\lambda}\geq 0
and
\mu_{\varOmega}^{V}
is a positive measure. Finally, rewriting the last spectral integral from above, one obtains

\displaystyle\beta\omega(V_{-})

\displaystyle>-\int_{\sigma(T)}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\lambda}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varOmega}^{V}(\lambda)=-\langle{\varOmega,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V}\varOmega}\rangle=-\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|^{2}>-\infty\ .

Note that the last inequality is true because of the assumption
(A3)
from
V.3.7
. The condition
\beta\omega(V_{-})>-\infty
will be essential to establish the formula for the relative entropy of the unperturbed state
\omega
with respect to the perturbed state
\omega_{V}
. Similarly, to obtain the relative entropy for the states in reversed order, the following additional assumption is required:

(A4)

\beta\omega_{V}(V_{+})<+\infty
.

To simplify the notation again, introduce the following class of
“Bogoliubov perturbations”
:

\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}\ :\ \parbox{207.0021pt}{\centering$V$ self-adjoint and satisfies assumptions \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A1}, \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A2}, \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A3}, and \ref{enu:perturbationTheory_A4} with respect to $L$ and $\varOmega$\@add@centering}}}\right\}\ .

Note that the additional assumption
(A4)
does not appear in
[
50
]
and, in fact, the latter does not state the second identity for the relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{V},\omega)
contained in the next proposition.

Proposition V.3.13
.

Let
\beta>0
be arbitrary,
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form,
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with Liouvillian
L
, and
\omega
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state with vector representative
\varOmega\in\mathcal{P}
. The following identities hold true for all
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega)
:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\omega_{V})=\beta\omega(V)+\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{V},\omega)=-\beta\omega_{V}(V)-\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

Proof
.

By the assumptions
(A3)
and
(A4)
, it follows that
\beta\omega(V)=\langle{\varOmega,\beta V\varOmega}\rangle
is a finite number or
+\infty
, and similarly
\beta\omega_{V}(V)=\langle{\varOmega_{V},\beta V\varOmega_{V}}\rangle/\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}
is a finite number or
-\infty
. This shows that the right-hand sides in the above equations cannot be equal to
-\infty
which is necessary to guarantee non-negativity of the relative entropy (
Corollary

IV.3.11
). With this in mind, the identities follow from
Theorem

V.3.11

(f)
and
(g)
in the same way as
Theorem

V.3.3

(g)
and
(h)
were proved for analytic perturbations.

As the identity for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\omega_{V})
in the previous proposition, the Golden-Thompson inequality for unbounded perturbations is also part of
[
50
, Thm. 5.5]
.

Proposition V.3.14
(Golden-Thompson inequality).

For
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega)
, there holds

\|\varOmega_{V}\|\leq\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega\|\ .

Proof
.

Since
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V_{n}/2}\varOmega=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V/2}\varOmega
and
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\varOmega_{V_{n}}=\varOmega_{V}
as observed in the proof of
Theorem

V.3.11

(a)
, where
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is given by
Eq.

V.18
, one can employ the same proof strategy as used for bounded perturbations in
Proposition

V.3.4
which, in particular, relies on using the Golden-Thompson inequality (
V.14
) for bounded perturbations.

V.4
The Two-sided Bogoliubov Inequality

Finally, the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality, which was recently proved for quantum mechanical systems
[
136
]
and applied to the problem of determining finite-size effects in molecular simulations
[
137
,
49
]
, can be generalized to arbitrary von Neumann algebras, relying on the perturbation theory of KMS-states developed above. First, the quantum-mechanical case will be reviewed quickly following
[
136
]
.

V.4.a  The Quantum-Mechanical Case

V.4.1
Mathematical setup.

Consider a finite quantum system
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
at inverse temperature
\beta>0
confined to a region
\varSigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
with Hamiltonian
H:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(H)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. One may characterize the system in terms of the canonical Gibbs state (
cf.

V.1.6
)

\rho=\frac{1}{Z}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}{\ ,\quad}Z=\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H})\ .

(V.19)

Suppose that the region
\varSigma
is divided into
d\in\mathbb{N}
subregions
\varSigma_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
1\leq k\leq d
, such that
\varSigma=\bigcup_{k=1}^{d}\varSigma_{k}
. Then each
\varSigma_{k}
represents a smaller subsystem of the total system
\varSigma
which shall be described independently from the other ones. To implement this idea, one decomposes the the Hamiltonian
H
into the sum

H=H_{0}+U\ ,

where
H_{0}
and
U
are self-adjoint operators such that
H
is again self-adjoint on its domain. Physically, the operator
H_{0}
represents the Hamiltonian of the non-interacting subsystems, and it takes the form

H_{0}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}H_{0}^{(k)}\ ,

with
H_{0}^{(k)}
being the self-adjoint Hamiltonian describing only the
k
-th subsystem located in
\varSigma_{k}
. The operator
U
mediates the interaction between the
d
different subsystems. Assume that also
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{0}}
is trace-class and define the Gibbs state

\rho_{0}=\frac{1}{Z_{0}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{0}}{\ ,\quad}Z_{0}=\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{0}})

(V.20)

representing the thermal state of the uncoupled subsystems.

To quantify the thermodynamic difference between the full system and the collection of uncoupled systems, one defines the
relative free energy
(or:
interface energy
)

\varDelta F\coloneqq-\beta^{-1}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Z}{Z_{0}}}}\right)\ .

(V.21)

This is nothing but the difference between the free energy
F=-\beta^{-1}\log(Z)
of the full system and the free energy
F_{0}=-\beta^{-1}\log(Z_{0})
of the uncoupled subsystems. Physically,
\varDelta F
represents the free energy difference associated with the partitioning of the large system into smaller, independent subsystems.

Proposition V.4.2
(Two-sided Bogoliubov inequality
[
136
, Thm. 4.1]
).

In the situation described above, assume that
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[U]\coloneqq\tr(\rho_{0}U)<+\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U]\coloneqq\tr(\rho U)<+\infty
. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U]\leq\varDelta F\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[U]\ .

(V.22)

Proof
.

Consider the Umegaki relative entropy
S
, defined in
Eq.

I.2
, between the Gibbs states of the uncoupled and coupled system. One computes
[
136
, pp. 8 f.]

\displaystyle S(\rho_{0},\rho)=\beta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[U]-\beta\varDelta F\quad\text{and}\quad S(\rho,\rho_{0})=-\beta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U]+\beta\varDelta F\ .

From non-negativity of
S
(see
Corollary

IV.3.11
), it follows that
\varDelta F\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[U]
, which is the upper bound, and
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U]\leq\varDelta F
, which is the lower bound.

Remarks V.4.3
.

(a)

The upper bound is the famous
Peierls-Bogoliubov inequality

[
121
,
24
,
157
]
which is an important tool in statistical mechanics
[
18
]
,
[
138
, Sect. 2.5]
,
[
150
, Sect. 8.3]
, and which, for two self-adjoint operators
A
and
B
such that
\mathrm{e}^{A},\mathrm{e}^{A+B}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, is usually written
[
33
, Eq. (2.14)]
,
[
118
, Cor. 3.14]

\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\tr(\mathrm{e}^{A+B})}{\tr(\mathrm{e}^{A})}}}\right)\geq\frac{\tr(B\,\mathrm{e}^{A})}{\tr(\mathrm{e}^{A})}\ .

Indeed, choosing
A=-\beta H_{0}
and
B=-\beta U
, the above inequality becomes
\log(Z/Z_{0})\geq-\beta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[U]
which is equivalent to the upper bound in
Eq.

V.22
.

(b)

The two-sided Bogoliubov inequality can be applied in numerical simulations to define and estimate the error due to finite-size effects
[
136
, p. 9]
; a general computational protocol highlighting the utility of (
V.22
) is discussed in
[
136
, Sect. 5]
. In particular, the inequality can be used to justify the simulation of a small subsystem instead of the computationally unfeasible total system
[
137
]
; see also
[
48
,
49
]
.

The inequalities in (
V.22
) can be sharpened by using the upper bound in this equation and the Golden-Thompson trace inequality (
cf.

Remark

V.3.5
). The following result is proved in
[
136
, Sect. 4.1]
.

Proposition V.4.4
(
[
136
, Cor. 4.5]
).

Under the assumptions of
Proposition

V.4.2
, it holds that

\sup_{V\geq 0}\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U-V]-\beta^{-1}\log\tr\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\log\rho_{0}-\beta V}\bigr)\Bigr\}=\varDelta F=\inf_{\gamma\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})}\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[U]+\beta^{-1}S(\gamma,\rho_{0})\Bigr\}\ .

(V.23)

The supremum is taken over all densely defined, positive self-adjoint operators
V
on
\mathcal{H}
, and the infimum ranges over all density matrices
\gamma
on
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, the bounds of
Proposition

V.4.2
are a special case of (
V.23
) obtained by taking
V=0
and
\gamma=\rho_{0}
.

V.4.b  Generalization to Arbitrary von Neumann Algebras

A natural question to ask is whether the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality (
V.22
) can be extended to quantum systems of infinitely many degrees of freedom, that is, to arbitrary von Neumann algebras. A first step towards this goal consists of defining an analogue of the quantity
\varDelta F
from
Eq.

V.21
.

Definition V.4.5
(Relative free energy).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
H_{0}
, and let
\beta>0
and
\omega_{0}
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state on
\mathfrak{M}
with vector representative
\varOmega_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
. Given a Bogoliubov perturbation
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
, define the
relative free energy
of the perturbed state
\omega_{U}
with respect to the state
\omega_{0}
to be

\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})\coloneqq\omega_{U}(U)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})\ .

(V.24)

Remark V.4.6
.

Consider a finite quantum system
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
with Hamiltonian
H_{0}
at inverse temperature
\beta>0
, let
\omega_{0}=\tr(\rho_{0}\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
be the canonical Gibbs state for
H_{0}
, and let
\omega_{U}=\tr(\rho\ \mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,)
be the corresponding one for the Hamiltonian
H=H_{0}+U
, see
Eqs.

V.19
and
V.20
. In the proof of
Proposition

V.4.2
, it was mentioned that

\varDelta F=\tr(\rho U)+\beta^{-1}\tr\bigl(\rho(\log\rho-\log\rho_{0})\bigr)=\omega_{U}(U)+\beta^{-1}S(\rho,\rho_{0})\ ,

where
S(\rho,\rho_{0})
is the Umegaki relative entropy. Since the right-hand side of this expression can be defined in arbitrary von Neumann algebras, this justifies calling (
V.24
)
“relative free energy”
.

A definition of a relative free energy in operator algebras similar to (
V.24
), justified by physical principles and the analogy to finite quantum systems, was already suggested by
M. J. Donald
in 1987
[
53
, Eq. (1.2)]
; he considers a relative free energy of an
arbitrary
normal state with respect to an equilibrium state, both defined with respect to the
same
Hamiltonian of the quantum system.

The next proposition is the main result of this section.

Proposition V.4.7
(Two-sided Bogoliubov inequality in von Neumann algebras).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
H_{0}
, and let
\beta>0
and
\omega_{0}
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state on
\mathfrak{M}
with vector representative
\varOmega_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
. For all
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
, the following two-sided inequality holds true:

\omega_{U}(U)\leq\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})\leq\omega_{0}(U)\ .

(V.25)

Proof
.

From the second identity in
Proposition

V.3.13
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})=-\beta\omega_{U}(U)-\log\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}
, and
Definition

V.4.5
, it follows that the relative free energy can also be written as

\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})=-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}\bigr)\ .

(V.26)

Using that the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})
is non-negative,
cf.

Proposition

IV.1.8
, it follows that
\omega_{U}(U)\leq\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})
which is the lower bound. Similarly, combining the first identity from
Proposition

V.3.13
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{0},\omega_{U})=\beta\omega_{0}(U)+\log\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}
, with non-negativity of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega_{0},\omega_{U})
, one obtains
\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})\leq\omega_{0}(U)
which is the upper bound.

Remarks V.4.8
.

(a)

The inequality (
V.25
) still holds true if
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
is replaced by a bounded perturbation
U\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. This can also be proved directly by employing
Theorem

V.3.3
.

(b)

As in the quantum-mechanical case (
Proposition

V.4.2
), the upper bound
-\beta^{-1}\log\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}\allowbreak\leq\omega_{0}(U)
of
Eq.

V.25
is a version of the
Peierls-Bogoliubov inequality
. For operator algebras, it was established by
H. Araki
in
[
5
, Thm. 1]
for bounded perturbations. In its typical form, this inequality is written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega_{0}(U)}\leq\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}\ .

It is proved for unbounded perturbations
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
also in
[
50
, Thm. 5.5 (8)]
.

The lower bound
\omega_{U}(U)\leq-\beta^{-1}\log\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}
, although obtained in a straightforward fashion, does not seem to have been discussed in the literature so far. Usually, rather the
Golden-Thompson inequality
, see
Proposition

V.3.14
and
Remark

V.3.5
, which can be written in the more familiar form

\displaystyle\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}\leq\omega_{0}(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta U})\ ,

is investigated. Note that together, the Peierls-Bogoliubov and Golden-Thompson inequality yield the following two-sided bound for the relative free energy
\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})
:

-\beta^{-1}\log\omega_{0}(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta U})\leq\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})\leq\omega_{0}(U)\ .

(V.27)

Thus, it needs to be stressed that the lower bound in the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality (
V.25
) does not reproduce the known Golden-Thompson inequality, but is actually a new estimate. Of course, the virtue of the bounds in
Eq.

V.27
is that they require only knowledge of the given state
\omega_{0}
, and not of the perturbed state
\omega_{U}
. However, for many applications in physics,
Eq.

V.25
seems to be more interesting, see
[
48
,
49
,
136
,
137
]
.

(c)

One can rewrite the expression (
V.26
) as follows:

\displaystyle-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{U}\|\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\beta^{-1}\log\big\langle{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(H_{0}+U)/2}\varOmega_{0},\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(H_{0}+U)/2}\varOmega_{0}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\beta^{-1}\log\big\langle{\varOmega_{0},\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(H_{0}+U)}\varOmega_{0}}\big\rangle\ .

If one defines
H\coloneqq H_{0}+U
to be the
“physical Liouvillian”
of the system, then one obtains

\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})=-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{U}\|\bigr)^{2}=-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\langle{\varOmega_{0},\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\varOmega_{0}}\rangle\bigr)\ .

Comparing this expression with some of the works of
R. Longo

[
104
, p. 471]
,
[
105
, Eq. (30)]
,
[
108
, p. 116]
, where the right-hand side of the above expression appears naturally and is termed
relative
or
incremental free energy
, reinforces the interpretation of the quantity
\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})
as the relative free energy between the states
\omega_{U}
and
\omega_{0}
.

In the following, it will be shown that
Proposition

V.4.7
can be considered a proper generalization of the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality for quantum systems,
Proposition

V.4.2
, to unbounded perturbations of the
“free Liouvillian”

H_{0}
on general von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}
.

Corollary V.4.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\beta>0
, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
H_{0}
such that
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{0}}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, let
\omega_{0}
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state, and let
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
be an unbounded perturbation such that
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(H_{0}+U)}\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. Then there exist
\rho_{0},\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
satisfying the inequality

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U]\leq\varDelta F\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[U]\ .

Proof
.

Since
H_{0}
is the standard Liouvillian of
\tau
, it holds that
\tau_{t}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{0}}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{0}}
(
Proposition

V.1.4
). Therefore, as
\omega_{0}
is a
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state on
\mathfrak{M}
,
Example

V.1.12
implies that
\omega_{0}
is given by the density matrix
\rho_{0}
from
Eq.

V.20
. Furthermore, recall that the perturbed dynamics takes the form
\tau_{t}^{U}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(H_{0}+U)}A\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(H_{0}+U)}
(
Proposition

V.2.7
). Thus, as the perturbed state is a
(\tau^{U},\beta)
-KMS-state according to
Theorem

V.3.11

(d)
, it follows again from
Example

V.1.12
that
\omega_{U}
is given by the density matrix
\rho
from
Eq.

V.19
with
H=H_{0}+U
.

As was already discussed in
Remark

V.4.6
(see also
Examples

IV.1.5

(b)
for the proof that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}=S
if
\mathfrak{M}=\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
), the relative free energy
\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})
in the present situation is given by
\varDelta F
from
Eq.

V.21
. Therefore,
Proposition

V.4.7
implies the asserted inequality.

V.4.c  Towards Variational Bounds

In the following, it will be investigated whether the variational bounds for the relative free energy stated in
Proposition

V.4.4
can be extended to the operator-algebraic setting. It turns out that for bounded perturbations, corresponding results are well-known. To extend these to more general unbounded perturbations, first two auxiliary results will be proved. They are both extensions of known result due to
H. Araki
(
cf.

[
5
, Eq. (3.6)]
and
[
11
, Eq. (4.28)]
, respectively).

Lemma V.4.10
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form, let
\varPhi\in\mathcal{P}
be separating, and let
L\coloneqq\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi})
. If
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi)
, then
\varPhi\in\dom(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2})
, hence one may define the following vector:

\displaystyle\varPhi_{V}\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(L+V)/2}\,\varPhi\ .

Proof
.

One can prove this result in an analogous way as the existence of the perturbed
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-vector for unbounded
V
in
Theorem

V.3.11

(a)
.

Let
\varphi=\omega_{\varPhi}
be the faithful normal functional induced by
\varPhi
, and let
\tau=\sigma^{\varphi}
be the modular automorphism group. According to
Examples

V.1.5

(b)
,
L
is the standard Liouvillian of
(\mathfrak{M},\tau)
. With this observation, one establishes the assertion of the lemma first for analytic
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\tau}
as in
Section

V.3.b
by noting that the proof of the latter did not use any KMS-specific properties.

Next, one shows that the claim is true for bounded
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
as was done in
Section

V.3.c
. It is important to note that the argument requires the Golden-Thompson inequality for analytic perturbations whose proof relied on an expression for the relative entropy,
Theorem

V.3.3

(h)
, specific for KMS-states. However, the Golden-Thompson inequality can be proved for arbitrary normal functionals and bounded perturbations without relying on this technique
[
5
, Thm. 2]
, hence the argument proceeds as mentioned.

Finally, one establishes the assertion for self-adjoint
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{(\eta)}
satisfying
(A1)
–
(A3)
by an approximation through bounded operators as in
Theorem

V.3.11

(a)
which makes use of the Golden-Thompson inequality for bounded perturbations which, as mentioned before, is available for non-KMS-states.

Lemma V.4.11
.

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
be two faithful normal states on
\mathfrak{M}
, denote by
\varPhi,\varPsi\in\mathcal{P}
their vector representatives, and let
L=\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi})
and
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi)
. Assume that the operator
\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\dom(\log\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})\cap\dom(V)
. Then

\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi_{V},\varPsi})=\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V\ .

(V.28)

Proof
.

Let
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
be a sequence of bounded self-adjoint elements of
\mathfrak{M}
approximating the operator
V
as constructed in
V.3.8
.

First
, note that for all
\xi\in\mathcal{D}
, it holds that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}V_{n}\xi=V\xi
according to
Lemma

V.3.9
. Therefore, the assumption on
\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V
together with
Proposition

B.3.11
implies that

\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V_{n}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V

in the strong resolvent sense as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.
Second
, observe that by the last identity in the proof of
Theorem

V.3.11

(a)
(which, as mentioned in the proof of
Lemma

V.4.10
, also holds for non-KMS-states), one has
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\varPhi_{V_{n}}=\varPhi_{V}
. Furthermore, since
V_{n}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
is bounded, it is well-known that
\varPhi_{V_{n}}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is a cyclic and separating element of the natural positive cone
\mathcal{P}
. (See
[
6
, Prop. 4.1 & Cor. 4.4]
together with the identity
[
5
, Eq. (3.6)]
, or
[
118
, p. 221 & Cor. 12.7]
together with the remarks regarding the normalization convention of the perturbed functional in
[
55
, Exa. 3.3]
.) Therefore, since
\mathcal{P}
is weakly closed (
cf.

Eq.

V.17
), it follows that
\varPhi_{V}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Third
, for bounded perturbations, the following identity is known to hold true
[
11
, Eq. (4.28)]
,
[
55
, Eq. (3.6) & Thm. A.7]
,
[
118
, Thm. 12.6]
:

\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi_{V_{n}},\varPsi})=\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V_{n}\ .

Since, as argued above, the right-hand side converges to
\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V
in the strong resolvent sense,
Lemma

III.3.9
implies
Eq.

V.28
.

The next proposition contains the Gibbs variational principle for unbounded perturbations. The proof presented here is an adaptation to the unbounded setting of an argument due to
D. Petz
who established the following result for bounded perturbations
[
125
, Prop. 1 & Cor. 2]
. In light of the previous
Lemma

V.4.11
, introduce the following final class of
“Gibbs perturbations”
:

\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi)\ :\ \parbox{137.9979pt}{\centering$\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V$ is essentially self-adjoint on $\dom(\log\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})\cap\dom(V)$\@add@centering}}}\right\}\ .

Proposition V.4.12
(Gibbs variational principle).

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
be two faithful normal states on
\mathfrak{M}
, let
\varPhi,\varPsi\in\mathcal{P}
be their vector representatives, and let
L=\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi})
and
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)
. Then

-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\leq\psi(V)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)\ ,

(V.29)

and equality holds true if and only if
\psi=\omega_{\varPhi_{V}}/\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}
. Moreover,

-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)=\inf_{\gamma\in\varXi(\mathfrak{M},V,\varPhi)}\Bigl(\gamma(V)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\gamma,\varphi)\Bigr)\ ,

(V.30)

where the infimum is taken over the set

\varXi(\mathfrak{M},V,\varPhi)\coloneqq\Big\{{\omega\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})\ :\ \text{$\omega=\omega_{\varOmega}$ faithful and $\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varOmega})-\beta V$ ess. self-adjoint}}\Big\}\ .

Proof
.

1.
Write
\varphi^{V}\coloneqq\omega_{\varPhi_{V}}=\langle{\varPhi_{V},\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\ \varPhi_{V}}\rangle
,
4
4

4

The non-normalized perturbed functional
\varphi^{V}
should not be confused with the
state

\omega_{V}
considered in
Definition

V.3.2
.

and note that
\varPhi_{V}
is cyclic and separating for
\mathfrak{M}
. (This follows from the fact that, as mentioned above,
\varPhi_{V}
is cyclic and separating for bounded
V
, hence it can be proved as in
Theorem

V.3.11

(c)
.) Therefore,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi^{V})
is given by the usual expression. From the assumption on the operator
\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V
and
Lemma

V.4.11
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi^{V})

\displaystyle=-\big\langle{\varPsi,\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi_{V},\varPsi})\varPsi}\big\rangle=-\big\langle{\varPsi,\bigl(\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V\bigr)\varPsi}\big\rangle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)+\beta\psi(V)\ .

(This identity is well-known for bounded perturbations
[
11
, Thm. 3.10]
,
[
55
, Cor. 5.9]
,
[
118
, Cor. 12.8]
.) Using the fundamental inequality for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}
from
Proposition

IV.1.6
, one obtains

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi^{V})\geq-\psi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varphi^{V}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\psi)\bigr)}{\psi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}}}\right)=-\log\bigl(\varphi^{V}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr)\ .

(V.31)

Writing
\varphi^{V}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}
, the relation for the relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi_{V})
derived above together with the previous inequality imply that

-\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\leq\beta\psi(V)+\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)\ .

2.
For the statement regarding equality in (
V.29
), recall from
Corollary

IV.3.10
that the right-hand side of
Eq.

V.31
can be interpreted as the relative entropy
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}},\varphi^{V}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}})
on the subalgebra
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\{{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}}\}
, and hence the inequality (
V.31
) is just a special instance of
Corollary

IV.3.9
. In light of this fact, it follows from a theorem of
D. Petz

[
124
, Thm. 4]
that equality in (
V.31
) is obtained if and only if

\varDelta_{\varphi^{V},\psi}^{\mathrm{i}t}\,\varDelta_{\psi}^{-\mathrm{i}t}=\varDelta_{\varphi^{V}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}},\psi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}}^{\mathrm{i}t}\,\varDelta_{\psi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}}^{-\mathrm{i}t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varphi^{V}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}{\psi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})}}}\right)^{\mathrm{i}t}\quad(t\in\mathbb{R})\ ,

where it was used that
\varDelta_{\psi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
because
\psi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}
is a tracial state (
cf.

Examples

III.1.11

(a)
and the proof of
Corollary

IV.3.10
), and that
\varDelta_{\varphi^{V}|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}},\psi|_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}}=\varphi^{V}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})/\psi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
according to
Lemma

III.3.7
. (In
[
124
, Thm. 4]
, the first equality in the above equation is stated as an equality of
Connes’ cocycle derivatives
,
cf.

[
83
, Sect. 6.2 & 10.2]
for definitions, which was avoided here for simplicity.) It is noted in
[
125
, p. 346]
that the above identity implies
\varphi^{V}=\lambda\psi
for some
\lambda>0
, namely
\lambda=\varphi^{V}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})/\psi(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=\varphi^{V}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
. This shows that equality in (
V.29
) is attained only for the state
\psi=\varphi^{V}/\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}
.

3.
Finally, observe that on the one hand, the inequality (
V.29
) implies that

-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\varXi(\mathfrak{M},V,\varPhi)}\Bigl(\gamma(V)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\gamma,\varphi)\Bigr)

since by assumption on
\psi
and
V
, it holds that
\psi\in\varXi(\mathfrak{M},V,\varPhi)
. On the other hand, the fact that equality in (
V.29
) is obtained if and only if
\psi
takes the form
\widetilde{\psi}\coloneqq\varphi^{V}/\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}
shows that

-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)=\widetilde{\psi}(V)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}\bigl(\widetilde{\psi},\varphi\bigr)\geq\inf_{\gamma\in\varXi(\mathfrak{M},V,\varPhi)}\Bigl(\gamma(V)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\gamma,\varphi)\Bigr)\ .

Together, the previous two inequalities prove the variational principle in
Eq.

V.30
.

Remark V.4.13
.

There is a different approach to perturbation theory in operator algebras, going back to
S. Sakai

[
140
]
and
M. J. Donald

[
55
]
, which deals with semi-bounded perturbations which are modeled as so-called extended-valued self-adjoint operators, see
[
118
, Ch. 12]
and
[
83
, Sect. 7.1]
. In this framework, given a normal state
\varphi\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
and a generalized operator
V
, one
defines
the perturbed state
\varphi^{V}
as the unique minimizer of the Gibbs variational principle (
V.30
); this definition is essentially based on the fact that
\rho_{0}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{0}}/\tr(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{0}})
is the unique minimizer of the free energy according to the Gibbs variational principle
[
118
, Prop. 1.10]
.

Thus, in this alternative approach to perturbation theory, the Gibbs variational principle is already satisfied by construction. In light of this, it is appropriate to remark that the argument presented above has the advantage of relying on the more constructive dynamical approach to perturbation theory presented in
Sections

V.2
and
V.3
, and that it does not employ the abstract concept of extended-valued operators.

Next, a variational expression for the relative entropy in terms of bounded perturbations
V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, also due to
D. Petz

[
125
, Thm. 9]
, shall be proved. This variational principle is the dual form of the Gibbs variational principle (
V.30
), and it is known in the literature as the
Donsker-Varadhan principle
. The proof of this result presented here uses methods from convex analysis and follows
[
118
, p. 228]
.

Proposition V.4.14
(Petz).

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
be two faithful normal states on
\mathfrak{M}
with
\varphi
given by
\varPhi\in\mathcal{P}
. Then the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy of
\psi
and
\varphi
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)=\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}}\Bigl(-\beta\psi(V)-\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .

(V.32)

Proof
.

Let
E\coloneqq\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
and
F\coloneqq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, and define the duality pairing
E\times F\owns(\omega,V)\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\langle{\omega,V}\rangle\coloneqq-\beta\omega(V)
. Equip
E
and
F
with the weak topologies
\sigma(E,F)
and
\sigma(F,E)
, respectively (
cf.

A.2.4
), and consider the function
g:E\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
given by
g(\omega)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\varphi)
. It holds that the
Legendre-Fenchel conjugate

[
117
, p. 255 f.]

g^{\ast}:F\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
of
g
is given for all
V\in F
by

\displaystyle g^{\ast}(V)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\omega\in E}\Bigl(\langle{\omega,V}\rangle-g(\omega)\Bigr)=\sup_{\omega\in E}\Bigl(-\beta\omega(V)-\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\varphi)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=-\inf_{\omega\in E}\Bigl(\beta\omega(V)+\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\omega,\varphi)\Bigr)=\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\ ,

where in the last step the Gibbs variational principle (
V.30
) (see also
[
125
, Cor. 2]
) was used. Now, since the function
g
is lower semi-continuous and convex due to the respective properties of the relative entropy
[
11
, Thm. 3.7 & 3.8]
,
[
118
, Cor. 5.12]
, it follows from the Fenchel-Hörmander-Moreau theorem
[
117
, Thm. 6.1.2]
that
g
is equal to its
biconjugate

g^{\ast\ast}
,
i.e.
, for all
\psi\in E
there holds

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)=g(\psi)=g^{\ast\ast}(\psi)\coloneqq\sup_{V\in F}\Bigl(\langle{\psi,V}\rangle-g^{\ast}(V)\Bigr)=\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}}\Bigl(-\beta\psi(V)-\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .

With the help of the previous proposition and the Gibbs variational principle, one can now establish the Donsker-Varadhan principle for unbounded perturbations.

Proposition V.4.15
(Donsker-Varadhan principle).

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
be two faithful normal states on
\mathfrak{M}
given by
\varPhi,\varPsi\in\mathcal{P}
, and let
L=\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi})
. Then

\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)=\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)}\Bigl(-\psi(V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .

(V.33)

Proof
.

From the inequality (
V.29
) in
Proposition

V.4.12
, it immediately follows that

\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)\geq\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)}\Bigl(-\psi(V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .

On the other hand, since
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)
, the variational expression (
V.32
) from
Proposition

V.4.14
for bounded perturbations shows that

\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\varphi)=\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{sa}}}\Bigl(-\psi(V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\leq\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)}\Bigl(-\psi(V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varPhi_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .

Together, these two inequalities prove the assertion.

Combining the Gibbs variational principle from
Proposition

V.4.12
and the Donsker-Varadhan principle from the previous
Proposition

V.4.15
, one obtains variational bounds for the relative free energy.

Corollary V.4.16
(Variational bounds for the relative free energy).

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{H},J,\mathcal{P})
be a von Neumann algebra in standard form, let
\tau
be a
W^{\ast}
-dynamics on
\mathfrak{M}
with standard Liouvillian
H_{0}
, and let
\omega_{0}
be a faithful
(\tau,\beta)
-KMS-state given by
\varOmega_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
. For all
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0},\varOmega_{U})}\Bigl(\omega_{U}(U-V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad=\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})=\inf_{\psi\in\varXi(\mathfrak{M},U,\varOmega_{0})}\Bigl(\psi(U)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\omega_{0})\Bigr)\ .\end{split}

(V.34)

In particular, there is the following family of two-sided bounds for the relative free energy
\mathcal{F}(\omega,\omega_{0})
which holds for all
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varOmega_{0},\varOmega_{U})
and
\psi\in\varXi(\mathfrak{M},U,\varOmega_{0})
:

\omega_{U}(U-V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\leq\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})\leq\psi(U)+\beta^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{std}}(\psi,\omega_{0})\ .

(V.35)

Proof
.

First, choosing
\varphi=\omega_{0}
in (
V.30
) and noting that
\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})=-\beta^{-1}\log\|\varOmega_{U}\|^{2}
immediately yields the second line of
Eq.

V.34
. Next, combining the definition of the relative free energy from
Eq.

V.24
with (
V.33
) gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\omega_{U},\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\omega_{U}(U)+\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0},\varOmega_{U})}\Bigl(-\omega_{U}(V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0},\varOmega_{U})}\Bigl(\omega_{U}(U-V)-\beta^{-1}\log\bigl(\|\varOmega_{V}\|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\ .

Remark V.4.17
.

The variational principles in
Eq.

V.34
are the generalization of
Proposition

V.4.4
to unbounded perturbations on a general von Neumann algebra. Note also that by choosing
V=0
and
\psi=\omega_{0}
in
Eq.

V.35
, one recovers the bounds of
Proposition

V.4.7
. Thus, the above corollary generalizes the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality (
V.25
).

Chapter VI
Conclusion

VI.1
Summary

Utilizing the mathematically involved theory of operator algebras and, in particular, Tomita-Takesaki modular theory and its relatives, the abstract Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy of two normal functionals was defined for arbitrary von Neumann algebras. A selection of important properties of this functional were discussed, most prominently Uhlmann’s monotonicity theorem which states that the relative entropy is non-increasing under Schwarz mappings between two von Neumann algebras. A complete and detailed proof of this assertion under different assumptions on the involved normal functionals and the Schwarz mapping was given, and certain insights from this proof were used to obtain further monotonicity inequalities for the relative entropy. Namely, the situation of two von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and two vector functionals
\omega_{\varOmega},\omega_{\varPhi}\in(\mathfrak{M}_{2})_{\ast}^{+}
was studied, and it was investigated under what Hilbert-space transformations
V\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
, applied to the vector representatives
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
, the relative entropy is monotonic. A number of corresponding inequalities were obtained, all being derived from Uhlmann’s ultimate monotonicity inequality, under varying assumptions on the operator
V
and the vectors
\varOmega
and
V\varOmega
, see
Propositions

IV.4.2
,
IV.4.6
,
IV.4.7
,
IV.4.8
and
IV.4.11
. In particular, special instances of certain well-known results for the relative entropy,
viz.
, monotonicity under restriction of the functionals to subalgebras and invariance under
\ast
-automorphisms of the underlying algebra, were re-obtained in this framework, see
Corollaries

IV.4.4
and
IV.4.9
.

Furthermore, perturbation theory in operator algebras was developed to some extend, focusing on unbounded perturbations of KMS-states. Results from this area obtained fairly recently in the literature
[
50
]
were presented and re-proved in great detail, and even some slight extensions were suggested (
Lemma

V.3.10

(c)
,
Theorem

V.3.11

(g)
, and
Proposition

V.3.13
). The culmination of the known methods from the literature as well as of the extensions are certain identities for the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy between perturbed and unperturbed KMS-states; they were already used in the past to prove,
e.g.
, the Golden-Thompson inequality in von Neumann algebras for bounded perturbations. Most importantly for the present text, this framework, and the relative entropy identities in particular, were employed to extend the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality, which was previously established for quantum systems (that is,
“type I von Neumann algebras”
), to unbounded perturbations of equilibrium states on general von Neumann algebras (
Proposition

V.4.7
). It was shown that these generalized bounds reproduce the known inequalities from
[
136
, Thm. 4.1]
in case that the von Neumann algebra is
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and the perturbation is some unbounded self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
(
Corollary

V.4.9
). Moreover, under further technical assumptions and by extending certain well-known identities from bounded perturbation theory to unbounded perturbations (
Lemmas

V.4.10
and
V.4.11
), it was possible to prove analoga of the Gibbs and Donsker-Varadhan variational principles for unbounded perturbations (
Propositions

V.4.12
and
V.4.15
). This facilitated a generalization of the known variational bounds for the relative free energy
[
136
, Sect. 4.1]
to unbounded perturbations on von Neumann algebras (
Corollary

V.4.16
).

VI.2
Outlook
