Since the investigations in this text were conducted in a purely abstract mathematical setting, the main question that should be settled in future work is the applicability of the derived monotonicity inequalities to concrete situations, for example, to estimate relative entropies in certain models of algebraic quantum field theory,
cf.

Remarks

IV.4.10

(a)
. Theoretically, it is plausible that there are situations where one is given von Neumann algebras
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
and states
\omega_{V\varOmega},\omega_{V\varPhi}\in(\mathfrak{M}_{1})_{\ast}^{+}
, where
\varOmega,\varPhi\in\mathcal{H}
and
V\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
with suitable properties, for which the relative entropy shall be computed. Under certain technical conditions, which can be realized in applications, the bounds of
Section

IV.4
then allow to estimate this relative entropy by the one between
\omega_{\varOmega}
and
\omega_{\varPhi}
on the larger algebra. Similarly, it would be interesting to examine situations in which the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality,
cf.

Eqs.

V.25
and
V.35
, can be applied in quantum field theory. It has already been established in
[
137
,
49
]
that it is a useful inequality for certain applications in quantum statistical mechanics, hence one may investigate whether conceptually similar situations can be approached in the setting of quantum field theory.

Aside from this question, one may also further analyze the theoretical setup leading to the results of this text. Regarding the monotonicity inequalities, it would be interesting to examine whether one can prove inequalities of the form (
IV.21
) also for projection operators, or if one can relax the assumption in
Proposition

IV.4.7
about the vector
\varOmega
being contained in the initial subspace of the partial isometry
V
. This would be a step towards monotonicity results for non-unital Schwarz mappings which are of interest in the context of operational quantum physics
[
47
,
97
]
and measurement theory in quantum field theory
[
62
,
63
]
. There might be a connection to the problem raised in
Remarks

IV.4.10

(b)
, namely deriving a dilation theorem for Schwarz mappings between von Neumann algebras, in the spirit of the theorem of Stinespring for completely positive mappings between
C^{\ast}
-algebras. Furthermore, it might be interesting to investigate whether similar inequalities are valid if the operator
V
is substituted by an unbounded operator, no longer an element of the von Neumann algebra, but affiliated with it. This would greatly increase the situations in which such estimates are applicable. Finally, one might try to eliminate using Uhlmann’s theorem in the proofs of the inequalities of
Section

IV.4
and instead find independent proofs. (Possibly along similar lines as the discussion in
[
173
]
.) This would allow a broader audience, which does not necessarily have all the technical background from von Neumann algebra theory, to be introduced to monotonicity results for the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy.

With regard to the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality and the related variational principles, one should investigate whether the variational bounds (
V.34
) for the relative free energy can be proved under relaxed assumptions on the perturbation. In the present situation, in order to arrive at the final
Corollary

V.4.16
, one has to take the supremum and infimum over somewhat unwieldy sets of operators and normal functionals; this was necessary in order for the inequality (
V.29
) to be satisfied which was central to proving the variational principles. Ideally, one would hope to prove
Eq.

V.34
for the case in which the supremum ranges over the set
\mathfrak{S}_{2}(\mathfrak{M},H_{0},\varOmega_{0})
and the infimum over all normal states
\varSigma_{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
since these seem to be the most natural classes of operators and functionals in light of the variational bounds known from the quantum-mechanical case. In particular, it would be ideal to eliminate the technical assumption on the operator
\log(\varDelta_{\varPhi,\varPsi})-\beta V
which first appeared in
Lemma

V.4.11
, and which then propagated to the variational principles in
Propositions

V.4.12
and
V.4.15
, resulting in the use of the cumbersome classes of perturbations
\mathfrak{S}_{3}(\mathfrak{M},L,\varPhi,\varPsi)
and of normal states
\varXi(\mathfrak{M},V,\varPhi)
. Similarly, one might try to dispense with the assumption of faithfulness of the involved normal states which would considerably increase generality of the results.

Appendix A
Topological Vector Spaces

This first appendix collects some selected notions from general topology and the theory of locally convex spaces, in particular, the concept of weak topologies, which are used in different places throughout the main part of the text.

A.1
Preliminaries from General Topology

A.1.1
Closure.

(
[
166
, pp. 1 f.]
)
Let
X\neq\varnothing
be a non-empty set and
\mathfrak{T}\subseteq\mathfrak{P}(X)
be a topology on
X
. Denote the system of closed sets in
(X,\mathfrak{T})
by
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{T}}\coloneqq\{{C\subseteq X\ :\ X\setminus C\in\mathfrak{T}}\}
. For an arbitrary subset
A\subseteq X
, one defines the
closure
of
A
to be the set

\clos_{\mathfrak{T}}(A)\coloneqq\bigcap\{{C\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{T}}\ :\ A\subseteq C}\}\ .

The closure of
A
is also commonly denoted by
\overline{A}^{\mathfrak{T}}
or
\overline{A}
. It is clear that the closure of
A
is the smallest closed set containing
A
.

A.1.2
Comparison of topologies.

(
[
167
, Def. 2.2.5]
)
Let
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
be two topologies on a set
X
.
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
is said to be
finer
than
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
is said to be
coarser
than
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
, iff
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}_{2}
. For every subset
A\subseteq X
and every topological space
(Y,\mathfrak{S})
, it holds that
[
171
, p. 432]

\clos_{\mathfrak{T}_{2}}(A)\subseteq\clos_{\mathfrak{T}_{1}}(A)\quad\text{and}\quad C^{0}\bigl((X,\mathfrak{T}_{1}),Y\bigr)\subseteq C^{0}\bigl((X,\mathfrak{T}_{2}),Y\bigr)\ .

Let
\mathscr{T}(X)\subseteq\mathfrak{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{P}(X)\bigr)
be the collection of topologies on a set
X
. Define a binary relation
\leq
on
\mathscr{T}(X)
by setting
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\leq\mathfrak{T}_{2}
iff
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}_{2}
. It holds that
(\mathscr{T}(X),\leq)
is a partially ordered set.

Definition A.1.3
(Initial topology
[
132
, Def. 3.12]
).

Let
X
be a non-empty set, let
I
be an index set, and for each
i\in I
let
(X_{i},\mathfrak{T}_{i})
be a topological space and
f_{i}:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow X_{i}
be a map. A topology
\mathfrak{T}
on
X
is called
initial topology
with respect to
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
iff it satisfies the following universal property:

(IT)

For an arbitrary topological space
(Y,\mathfrak{S})
, a mapping
g:Y\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow X
is continuous if and only if
f_{i}\circ g
is continuous for every
i\in I
.

The initial topology is characterized as follows.

Theorem A.1.4
(Characterization of the initial topology
[
132
, Thm. 3.13]
).

Let
X\neq\varnothing
be a set, let
I
be an index set, and for each
i\in I
let
(X_{i},\mathfrak{T}_{i})
be a topological space and
f_{i}:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow X_{i}
be a map. There exists a unique initial topology
\mathfrak{T}
with respect to
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
on
X
, and it is the coarsest topology for which the family of maps
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is continuous. In particular, a net
(x_{k})_{k\in N}
converges in
(X,\mathfrak{T})
to
x\in X
if and only if
f_{i}(x_{k})\rightarrow f_{i}(x)
in
(X_{i},\mathfrak{T}_{i})
for every
i\in I
.

A.2
Linear and Weak Topologies

Definition A.2.1
(Topological vector space).

(
[
166
, p. 4]
)
Let
E
be a vector space over a field
\mathbb{K}
and
\mathfrak{T}
be a topology on the underlying set
E
.
\mathfrak{T}
is called a
vector space topology
(or:
linear topology
), and the pair
(E,\mathfrak{T})
is called a
topological vector space
, iff the vector space operations
(x,y)\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow x+y
and
(\lambda,x)\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\lambda x
are continuous with respect to
\mathfrak{T}
. The (
topological
)
dual space

(E,\mathfrak{T})^{\ast}
of
E
(short:
E^{\ast}
) is defined to be the space of all
\mathfrak{T}
-continuous linear functionals on
E
.

Proposition A.2.2
(
[
166
, Thm. 1.5]
).

Let
E
be a vector space, let
\{{(E_{i},\mathfrak{T}_{i})}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of topological vector spaces, and for every
i\in I
let
f_{i}:E\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow E_{i}
be a linear map. Then the initial topology
\mathfrak{T}
on
E
with respect to the family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is a linear topology.

Proposition A.2.3
(
[
166
, p. 7]
).

Let
E
be a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space and
P
be a set of semi-norms on
E
. Then the initial topology
\mathfrak{T}_{P}
on
E
with respect to the mappings
(\mathrm{Id}_{p})_{p\in P}
, where
\mathrm{Id}_{p}:E\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow(E,p)
,
x\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow x
, is a vector space topology on
E
called the
topology generated
by
P
. In particular, it holds that a net
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
in
E
converges to
x\in E
if and only if

\forall p\in P\ :\ \lim_{i\in I}p(x_{i}-x)=0\ .

A.2.4
Weak topologies.

(
[
166
, p. 6]
)
A
dual pair

\langle{E,F}\rangle
consists of two
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces
E
and
F
and a bilinear mapping
b\equiv\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle:E\times F\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{K}
, called the
dual pairing
. The map
b
induces the two mappings

\displaystyle b_{1}:E\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\Hom(F,\mathbb{K}),\ x\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow b_{1}(x)\coloneqq\langle{x,\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,}\rangle\ ,

\displaystyle b_{2}:F\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\Hom(E,\mathbb{K}),\ y\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow b_{2}(y)\coloneqq\langle{\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\,,y}\rangle\ .

The
weak topology

\sigma(E,F)
on the space
E
with respect to the dual pair
\langle{E,F}\rangle
is defined to be the initial topology with respect to the family
(b_{2}(y))_{y\in F}
; the weak topology
\sigma(F,E)
on
F
is defined analogously as the initial topology with respect to
(b_{1}(x))_{x\in E}
. According to
Proposition

A.2.2
, they both are vector space topologies.

Proposition A.2.5
(
[
166
, Thm. 1.8]
).

Let
\langle{E,F}\rangle
be a dual pair. Then
\bigl(E,\sigma(E,F)\bigr)^{\ast}=b_{2}(F)
.

Example A.2.6
.

(
[
45
, Sect. V.1]
)
Let
E
be a vector space and
\varLambda\subseteq\Hom(E,\mathbb{K})
be a linear subspace of the algebraic dual space of
E
. Define a bilinear mapping
b:E\times\varLambda\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{K}
by setting
b(x,\varphi)\coloneqq\varphi(x)
. Then the weak topology
\sigma(E,\varLambda)
on
E
with respect to
\langle{E,\varLambda}\rangle
is given as the initial topology of
(\varphi)_{\varphi\in\varLambda}
.

Note that for every
\varphi\in\varLambda
, the functional
|\varphi|:E\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty)
,
x\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow|\varphi(x)|
, is a semi-norm on
E
. It holds that the topology
\mathfrak{T}_{P_{\varLambda}}
generated by the family
P_{\varLambda}\coloneqq\{|\varphi|\,:\,\varphi\in\varLambda\}
and the weak topology
\sigma(E,\varLambda)
coincide. Furthermore, by
Proposition

A.2.5
,

\bigl(E,\sigma(E,\varLambda)\bigr)^{\ast}=\varLambda\ .

Let
(E,\mathfrak{T})
be a topological vector space. Then the topology
\sigma(E,E^{\ast})
is called
the weak topology
of
E
. It is the coarsest linear topology on
E
that yields the same dual space as
\mathfrak{T}
.

A.2.7
Weak-
\ast
topology.

(
[
45
, Sect. V.1]
)
Let
(E,\mathfrak{T})
be a topological vector space, let
x\in E
be a point and let
\varphi\in E^{\ast}
be a continuous linear functional. Consider the map
\iota(x):E^{\ast}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by
\iota(x)(\varphi)\coloneqq\varphi(x)
. The weak topology on
E^{\ast}
with respect to
\varLambda\coloneqq\{{\iota(x)\,:\,x\in E}\}\subseteq\Hom(E^{\ast},\mathbb{K})
and
\langle{\varphi,\iota(x)}\rangle\coloneqq\iota(x)(\varphi)
is called the
weak-
\ast
topology
on
E^{\ast}
and denoted by
\sigma(E^{\ast},E)
. Note that according to
Example

A.2.6
,
\sigma(E^{\ast},E)
is generated by the family of semi-norms
\{{|\iota(x)|\,:\,x\in E}\}
on
E^{\ast}
.

Appendix B
Hilbert Space Operators

In this appendix, several definitions and propositions from (bounded and unbounded) operator theory on Hilbert space shall be collected as they are heavily used in the main part of this text. Throughout, let
\mathcal{H}\equiv(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
denote a complex Hilbert space, let
\|\cdot\|=\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle^{1/2}
be the norm on
\mathcal{H}
, and let
\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)
be the Banach space of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
with the operator norm.

B.1
Bounded Operators

Theorem B.1.1
(Bounded linear extension
[
171
, Thm. II.1.5]
).

Let
D\subseteq E
be a dense linear subspace of a normed space
E
, let
F
be a Banach space, and let
T\in\mathscr{B}(D,F)
be a bounded linear operator. Then there exists a unique extension
\widehat{T}\in\mathscr{B}(E,F)
of
T
, that is, a bounded linear operator such that
\widehat{T}|_{D}=T
, which satisfies
\|\widehat{T}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.

Definition B.1.2
(Trace-class and Hilbert-Schmidt operators).

(
[
23
, Def. 26.1]
)
Let
T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be a bounded linear operator and
|T|\coloneqq(T^{\ast}T)^{1/2}
, defined via the functional calculus.
T
is called a
trace-class operator
iff there exists an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
such that

\|T\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in I}\big\langle{e_{i},|T|e_{i}}\big\rangle=\sum_{i\in I}\big\|{|T|^{1/2}e_{i}}\big\|^{2}<+\infty\ .

The set of all trace-class operators is denoted by
\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. Similarly,
T
is called a
Hilbert-Schmidt operator
, and the set of all Hilbert-Schmidt operators is denoted by
\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, iff there exists an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
such that

\|T\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\coloneqq\|T^{\ast}T\|_{\mathrm{tr}}^{1/2}=\bigg(\,{\sum_{i\in I}\|Te_{i}\|^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}=\bigg(\,{\sum_{i\in I}\langle{e_{i},T^{\ast}Te_{i}}\rangle}\bigg)^{1/2}<+\infty\ .

It is evident that
T
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator if and only if
T^{\ast}T
is a trace-class operator. Furthermore,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
are independent of the choice of the orthonormal basis
[
23
, Lem. 26.1]
.

B.1.3
Trace and Hilbert-Schmidt inner product.

(
[
23
, Thm. 26.1 & Cor. 26.3]
)
On the space of trace-class operators, the
trace

\tr:\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{K}
is a well-defined bounded linear functional
[
23
, Cor. 26.3]
which is given for
any
orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
by

\tr(T)\coloneqq\sum_{i\in I}\langle{e_{i},Te_{i}}\rangle{\ ,\quad}T\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\ .

Moreover, one can define an inner product on the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators:

\langle{S,T}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\coloneqq\tr(S^{\ast}T)=\sum_{i\in I}\langle{Se_{i},Te_{i}}\rangle{\ ,\quad}S,T\in\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\ .

(B.1)

Proposition B.1.4
(
[
23
, Thm. 26.1 & 26.2]
).

\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigr)
are normed vector spaces with
\|T^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{tr}}=\|T\|_{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\|S^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|S\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
for all
T\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and
S\in\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, and they are two-sided
\ast
-ideals in
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Furthermore,
\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigr)
is a Banach space and
\bigl(\mathscr{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}),\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigr)
is a Hilbert space.

Definition B.1.5
(Density matrix).

(
[
23
, Def. 26.2]
)
A trace-class operator
\rho\in\mathscr{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
is called a
density matrix
iff
\rho
is self-adjoint (
\rho^{\ast}=\rho
), positive (
\langle{\xi,\rho\xi}\rangle\geq 0
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{H})
and normalized (
\|\rho\|_{\mathrm{tr}}=\tr(\rho)=1
). The space of all density matrices on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted by
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
.

Definition B.1.6
(Orthogonal projection).

An operator
P\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
is called an
orthogonal projection
(or:
projection
for short) iff
P^{2}=P
and
\ker(P)\perp\ran(P)
, equivalently, if
P^{2}=P
and
P^{\ast}=P

[
171
, Thm. V.5.9]
. The set of all orthogonal projections on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted by
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
.

Lemma B.1.7
(Range of a projection).

Let
P\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
. Then
\eta\in\ran(P)
if and only if
P\eta=\eta
.

Proof
.

If
\eta\in\ran(P)
, then there exists
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\eta=P\xi
. Since
P
is idempotent, it follows that
P\eta=P^{2}\xi=P\xi=\eta
. The converse implication is clear.

Theorem B.1.8
(of the orthogonal projection
[
171
, Thm. V.3.4]
).

Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a closed non-empty subspace. Then there exists a unique non-zero orthogonal projection
P=P_{U}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})\setminus\{0\}
such that
\ran(P)=U
and
\ker(P)=U^{\perp}
. Moreover, the operator
Q\coloneqq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-P
is also an orthogonal projection, and its range is given by
\ran(Q)=\ran(P)^{\perp}
. Finally, the Hilbert space decomposes into an orthogonal direct sum
\mathcal{H}=U\oplus U^{\perp}
.

Remark B.1.9
.

It follows from
Theorem

B.1.8
that the set
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
and the set of closed subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
are in one-to-one correspondence by identifying every orthogonal projection
P
with the closed subspace
U\coloneqq\ran(P)\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, and
vice versa
every closed subspace with the unique orthogonal projection
P_{U}
onto it
[
23
, Prop. 23.1 (d)]
. This justifies using the following notation for any subspace
U\subseteq\mathcal{H}
:

[U]\coloneqq P_{\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(U)}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})\ .

Corollary B.1.10
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a subspace.

(a)

\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(U)=(U^{\perp})^{\perp}
.

(b)

The following assertion are equivalent:

(a)

U
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}
;

(b)

U^{\perp}=\{0\}
;

(c)

[U]=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof
.

Assertion
(a)
is a well-known consequence of the theorem of the orthogonal projection
[
171
, Cor. V.3.5]
. The first equivalence (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii) in
(b)
follows from
(a)
by noting that
\mathcal{H}=\{0\}^{\perp}
, and the equivalence (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) can be seen by using that
\eta=[U]\eta
iff
\eta\in\ran([U])
according to
Lemma

B.1.7
.

Lemma B.1.11
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a closed subspace and
P\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
be an orthogonal projection such that
P|_{U}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
. If
U^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(P)
, then
P=[U]
.

Proof
.

The identity
P|_{U}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}
implies that
U\subseteq\ran(P)
by
Lemma

B.1.7
. Since
\ker(P)=\ran(P)^{\perp}
(
Theorem

B.1.8
), it follows from the assumption that
U^{\perp}\subseteq\ran(P)^{\perp}
.

Let
\eta\in\ran(P)
be arbitrary. Then the previous observation implies that
\eta\perp\xi
for all
\xi\in U^{\perp}
, hence
\eta\in(U^{\perp})^{\perp}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}(U)=U
by
Corollary

B.1.10

(a)
and closedness of
U
. Thus,
\ran(P)=U
which is equivalent to
P=[U]
.

B.1.12
Order relation on
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
.

(
[
112
, p. 57]
)
For orthogonal projections
P,Q\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
, define the relation
P\leq Q
to hold true if and only if
\ran(P)\subseteq\ran(Q)
. This gives a partial order on
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
due to the respective properties of the set-theoretic inclusion,
cf.

Remark

B.1.9
.

B.1.13
Support projections.

(
[
156
, Sect. 2.13]
)
Let
T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
. The following projections related to
T
are of interest:

\mathbf{n}(T)\coloneqq\bigl[\ker(T)\bigr]{\ ,\quad}\mathbf{l}(T)\coloneqq\bigl[\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\ran(T)\bigr]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{r}(T)\coloneqq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}-\mathbf{n}(T)=\bigl[\ker(T)^{\perp}\bigr]\ .

Since
\ker(T)=\ran(T^{\ast})^{\perp}

[
171
, Thm. V.5.2 (g)]
, it follows that
\mathbf{r}(T)=\mathbf{l}(T^{\ast})
, and in case that
T
is self-adjoint, the
support
(
projection
) of
T
is defined to be

\mathbf{s}(T)\coloneqq\mathbf{l}(T)=\mathbf{r}(T)\ .

Definition B.1.14
(Partial isometry).

(
[
144
, p. 137]
,
[
156
, p. 27]
)
Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be two Hilbert spaces, and let
\mathcal{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{2}
be closed subspaces. A linear operator
V:\mathcal{H}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}
that maps the space
\mathcal{G}_{1}
isometrically onto the space
\mathcal{G}_{2}
and annihilates
\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\perp}
is called a
partial isometry
. The space
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is called the
initial space
of
V
, and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
is called the
final space
of
V
. It holds that
\mathbf{r}(V)=V^{\ast}V
is the projection onto
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathbf{l}(V)=VV^{\ast}
is the projection onto
\mathcal{G}_{2}
.

B.2
Closed and Closable Operators

Definition B.2.1
.

(
[
144
, Def. 1.3 & 1.5]
)
Let
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a linear operator.
T
is called
closed
iff its graph
\gr(T)\coloneqq\{{(\xi,T\xi)\,:\,\xi\in\dom(T)}\}
is closed in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}
, and it is called
closable
iff there exists a closed linear operator
S:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(S)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that
S\supseteq T
,
i.e.
,
\dom(S)\supseteq\dom(T)
and
S\xi=T\xi
for all
\xi\in\dom(T)
. A linear subspace
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\dom(T)
is called a
core
for the operator
T
iff for every
\xi\in\dom(T)
, there exists a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
such that
\xi_{n}\rightarrow\xi
and
T\xi_{n}\rightarrow T\xi
in
\mathcal{H}
.

B.2.2
Closure of an operator.

(
[
144
, pp. 6 f.]
)
Let
T
be a closable operator and
\overline{T}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(\overline{T})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be the closed operator defined by the relation
\gr(\overline{T})=\overline{\gr(T)}
. This operator, called the
closure
of
T
, is the smallest closed extension of
T
with respect to the operator inclusion
\subseteq
. By construction,
\dom(\overline{T})
consists of all those
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
for which there exists a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\dom(T)
such that
\xi=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\xi_{n}
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}T\xi_{n}
exists in
\mathcal{H}
; one defines
\overline{T}\xi\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}T\xi_{n}
.

B.2.3
Adjoint operators.

(
[
144
, p. 8]
)
Let
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a densely defined linear operator,
i.e.
,
\dom(T)\subseteq\mathcal{H}
lies dense. The
adjoint operator

T^{\ast}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T^{\ast})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
of
T
is defined by

\displaystyle\dom(T^{\ast})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\ :\ \exists\zeta\in\mathcal{H}\ \forall\eta\in\dom(T):\langle{\xi,T\eta}\rangle=\langle{\zeta,\eta}\rangle}\Big\}\ ,

\displaystyle T^{\ast}\xi

\displaystyle\coloneqq\zeta\quad\text{for}\quad\xi\in\dom(T^{\ast})\ .

B.2.4
Anti-linear operators.

(
[
156
, Sect. 9.35]
)
An
anti-linear operator
is a mapping
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
which satisfies for all
\xi,\eta\in\dom(T)
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}

T(\alpha\xi+\beta\eta)=\overline{\alpha}\,T\xi+\overline{\beta}\,T\eta\ .

The
adjoint operator
of
T
is the anti-linear operator
T^{\ast}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T^{\ast})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
defined by

\displaystyle\dom(T^{\ast})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\ :\ \exists\zeta\in\mathcal{H}\ \forall\eta\in\dom(T):\langle{x,T\eta}\rangle=\langle{\eta,\zeta}\rangle}\Big\}\ ,

\displaystyle T^{\ast}\xi

\displaystyle\coloneqq\zeta\quad\text{for}\quad\xi\in\dom(T^{\ast})\ .

Proposition B.2.5
(Properties of
T^{\ast}
and
\overline{T}

[
144
, Prop. 1.6 & Thm. 1.8]
).

Let
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a densely defined linear operator. The following properties obtain:

(a)

T^{\ast}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T^{\ast})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a closed linear operator.

(b)

\ran(T)^{\perp}=\ker(T^{\ast})
.

(c)

T
is closable if and only if
T^{\ast}
is densely defined.

(d)

If
T
is closable, then
(\,\overline{T}\,)^{\ast}=T^{\ast}
and
\overline{T}=T^{\ast\ast}

(e)

Suppose that
\ker(T)=\{{0}\}
and that
\ran(T)
lies dense in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Then
T^{\ast}
is invertible with
(T^{\ast})^{-1}=(T^{-1})^{\ast}
.

(f)

Assume that
T
is closable and injective. Then
T^{-1}
is closable if and only if
\ker(\overline{T})=\{0\}
. In this case, there holds
(\,\overline{T}\,)^{-1}=\overline{(T^{-1})}
.

Theorem B.2.6
(Polar decomposition of closed operators
[
144
, Thm. 7.2]
).

Let
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a densely defined, closed linear operator. Then there exists a partial isometry
U_{T}:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
with initial space
\ker(T)^{\perp}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\ran(T^{\ast})=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\ran(|T|)
, where
|T|\coloneqq(T^{\ast}T)^{1/2}
, and final space
\ker(T^{\ast})^{\perp}=\clos_{\|\cdot\|}\ran(T)
such that

T=U_{T}|T|\ .

This decomposition is unique. Moreover, if
T
is an anti-linear operator, then the partial isometry
U_{T}
is anti-linear as well
[
156
, p. 263]
.

B.3
Self-adjoint Operators

Theorem B.3.1
(Spectral thereom for self-adjoint operators
[
144
, Thm. 5.7]
).

Let
T:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a self-adjoint operator. Then there exists a unique spectral measure
E_{T}:\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
such that

T=\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}E_{T}(\lambda)\ .

Definition B.3.2
(Spectral integral).

(
[
144
, Sect. 4.3 & 5.3]
)
Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
with spectral measure
E_{T}
, and let
f:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}\cup\{{+\infty}\}
be an
E_{T}
-almost everywhere finite Borel-measurable function. The
spectral integral
of
f
with respect to
E_{T}
is denoted by

\mathbb{I}_{T}(f)\equiv f(T)\coloneqq\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\lambda)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}E_{T}(\lambda)\ .

For
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, let
\mu_{\xi,\eta}^{T}:\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty]
be the complex measure
\mu_{\xi,\eta}^{T}(A)\coloneqq\langle{\xi,E_{T}(A)\eta}\rangle
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
, and set
\mu_{\xi}^{T}(A)\coloneqq\mu_{\xi,\xi}^{T}(A)
. Then the domain of the spectral integral
\mathbb{I}_{T}(f)
is given by

\dom\bigl(\mathbb{I}_{T}(f)\bigr)\coloneqq\bigg\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\ :\ \int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}|f(\lambda)|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}^{T}(\lambda)<+\infty}\bigg\}\ .

Proposition B.3.3
(Properties of the functional calculus
[
144
, Thm. 5.9]
).

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
, let
f,g:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}\cup\{{+\infty}\}
be two
E_{T}
-almost everywhere finite Borel-measurable functions, and let
\xi,\eta\in\dom\bigl(\mathbb{I}_{T}(f)\bigr)
. Then the following properties hold true:

(a)

\displaystyle\langle{\xi,f(T)\eta}\rangle=\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\lambda)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi,\eta}^{T}(\lambda)
.

(b)

\displaystyle\|f(T)\xi\|^{2}=\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}|f(\lambda)|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}^{T}(\lambda)
.

(c)

(fg)(T)=\overline{f(T)g(T)}
. If at least one of the functions is bounded, then
(fg)(T)=f(T)g(T)

[
156
, p. 231]
, and in this case, the operators
f(T)
and
g(T)
commute with each other.

(d)

\mathbf{1}_{A}(T)=E_{T}(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proposition B.3.4
(
[
144
, Prop. 5.10]
).

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator with spectral measure
E_{T}
. A number
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of
T
if and only if
E_{T}(\{{\lambda}\})\neq 0
, and in this case
E_{T}(\{{\lambda}\})\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{H})
is the orthogonal projection onto the eigenspace
\Eig(T,\lambda)
.

Lemma B.3.5
.

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
and
U\in\mathscr{U}(\mathcal{H})
be a unitary operator. For a Borel measurable function
\varphi:\sigma(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, it holds that

\varphi(UTU^{\ast})=U\varphi(T)U^{\ast}\ .

Proof
.

By the multiplication operator form of the spectral theorem
[
92
, Thm. 16.5]
,
[
134
, Thm. VIII.4]
, there exists a measure space
(X,\varSigma,\nu)
, a measurable function
g:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and a unitary operator
V:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow L^{2}(X,\nu)
such that
T=V^{\ast}M_{g}V
. Define the unitary
W\coloneqq VU^{\ast}:\mathcal{H}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow L^{2}(X,\nu)
. One can then write
UTU^{\ast}=W^{\ast}M_{g}W
. From the functional calculus
[
92
, p. 426]
, it now follows that

\varphi(UTU^{\ast})=\varphi(W^{\ast}M_{g}W)=W^{\ast}M_{\varphi\circ g}W=UV^{\ast}M_{\varphi\circ g}VU^{\ast}=U\varphi(T)U^{\ast}\ .

Lemma B.3.6
.

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
and
\varOmega\in\Eig(T,\lambda)
be an eigenvector of
T
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
T\varOmega=\lambda\varOmega
. Then for every
E_{T}
-almost everywhere finite Borel-measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}\cup\{{+\infty}\}
, it holds that
f(T)\varOmega=f(\lambda)\varOmega
.

Proof
.

First, note that from
Lemma

B.1.7
and
Proposition

B.3.4
, it follows that
E_{T}(\{\lambda\})\varOmega=\varOmega
. Next, recall that if
A\cap B=\varnothing
for
A,B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
, then
E_{T}(A\cup B)=E_{T}(A)+E_{T}(B)
and
E_{T}(A)E_{T}(B)=E_{T}(B)E_{T}(A)=0

[
144
, Def. 4.2 & Lem. 4.3]
. Hence, if
\lambda\in A
for some
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
, then one obtains

\displaystyle E_{T}(A)\varOmega=E_{T}\bigl(A\setminus\{\lambda\}\bigr)\varOmega+E_{T}(\{\lambda\})\varOmega=E_{T}(A\setminus\{\lambda\})E_{T}(\{\lambda\})\varOmega+\varOmega=\varOmega\ .

Similarly, if
\lambda\notin A
, it holds that
E_{T}(A)\varOmega=E_{T}(A)E_{T}(\{\lambda\})\varOmega=0
.

Let
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
be arbitrary and consider the complex measure
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\langle{\xi,E_{T}(A)\varOmega}\rangle=\mu_{\xi,\varOmega}^{T}(A)
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
. By the previous two observations, it holds that
\mu_{\xi,\varOmega}^{T}(A)=\langle{\xi,\varOmega}\rangle\,\delta_{\lambda}(A)
, where
A\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\delta_{\lambda}(A)=\mathbf{1}_{A}(\lambda)
is the Dirac measure at
\lambda
. Using
Proposition

B.3.3

(a)
, one obtains

\langle{\xi,f(T)\varOmega}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\langle{\xi,E_{T}(t)\varOmega}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\,\langle{\xi,\varOmega}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\delta_{\lambda}(t)=\langle{\xi,f(\lambda)\varOmega}\rangle\ .

Since
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
was arbitrary, this shows that
f(T)\varOmega=f(\lambda)\varOmega
.

Theorem B.3.7
(Trotter product formula
[
134
, Thm. VIII.31]
,
[
50
, Thm. A.1]
).

Let
A
and
B
be two self-adjoint operators on
\mathcal{H}
, and assume that the operator sum
A+B
is essentially self-adjoint on
\dom(A)\cap\dom(B)
. Then the following limit exists in the strong operator topology on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
:

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(A+B)}=\operatorname*{\text{$so$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tA/n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB/n}\bigr)^{n}\ .

(B.2)

Definition B.3.8
(Strong resolvent convergence).

(
[
119
, Def. 10.1.1]
)
Let
T_{n}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) and
T
be self-adjoint operators on
\mathcal{H}
. One says that the sequence
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
T
in the
strong resolvent sense
iff
(T_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\rightarrow(T-\mathrm{i})^{-1}
in the strong operator topology, that is,

\forall\xi\in\mathcal{H}\ :\ \lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big\|{(T_{n}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\xi-(T-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\xi}\big\|=0\ .

Proposition B.3.9
(
[
119
, Prop. 10.1.8 & 10.1.9]
,
[
134
, Thm. VIII.20 & VIII.21]
).

The following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong resolvent sense;

(b)

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tT_{n}}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tT}
strongly for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
;

(c)

f(T_{n})\rightarrow f(T)
strongly for all bounded continuous
f:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

Proposition B.3.10
(
[
119
, Prop. 10.1.13]
).

Let
T_{n},T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})
be self-adjoint. If
T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong operator topology, then
T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong resolvent sense.

Proposition B.3.11
(
[
119
, Prop. 10.1.18]
,
[
134
, Thm. VIII.25]
,
[
50
, Prop. A.3]
).

Let
T
and
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be self-adjoint operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is a subspace contained in
\dom(T)
and
\dom(T_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and that
T
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathcal{D}
. Furthermore, assume that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}T_{n}\xi=T\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{D}
. Then it follows that
T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong resolvent sense.

Proposition B.3.12
(
[
50
, Prop. A.4]
).

Let
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of self-adjoint operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and assume that there exists another self-adjoint operator
T
such that
T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong resolvent sense. Furthermore, suppose that
(\varOmega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
and
\varOmega\in\mathcal{H}
are vectors such that
\varOmega_{n}\rightarrow\varOmega
weakly, and that there exists a constant
C\geq 0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|T_{n}\varOmega_{n}\|\leq C
. Then
\varOmega\in\dom(T)
,
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,T_{n}\varOmega_{n}
exists, and
T\varOmega=\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,T_{n}\varOmega_{n}
.

Remark B.3.13
.

(
[
50
, p. 482]
)
Suppose that
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,T_{n}\varOmega_{n}
exists. Then it follows from the uniform boundedness principle that
(T_{n}\varOmega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is bounded
[
171
, Cor. IV.2.3]
,
i.e.
, there is a constant
C\geq 0
such that
\|T_{n}\varOmega_{n}\|\leq C
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, one may replace the assumption
\|T_{n}\varOmega_{n}\|\leq C
in the above proposition by the existence of
\operatorname*{\text{$w$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,T_{n}\varOmega_{n}
.

Lemma B.3.14
.

Let
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of self-adjoint operators on
\mathcal{H}
converging in the strong resolvent sense to a self-adjoint operator
T
. Then for any continuous real-valued function
g:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, it holds that
g(T_{n})\rightarrow g(T)
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
in the strong resolvent sense.
1
1

1

The author wishes to thank Professor Dr. Jan Dereziński for clarifying this result and for discussing its proof.

Proof
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary, and observe that the function
h_{z}:\mathbb{R}\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
\lambda\varmapstochar\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\bigl(g(\lambda)-z\bigr)^{-1}
, is continuous and bounded. Indeed, since clearly
|g(\lambda)-z|\geq|\operatorname{Im}(z)|
, one obtains

|h_{z}(\lambda)|\leq\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Im}(z)|}\ .

Therefore, since
T_{n}\rightarrow T
in the strong resolvent sense, it follows from
Proposition

B.3.9
that
h_{z}(T_{n})\rightarrow h_{z}(T)
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
in the strong operator topology. But
h_{\mathrm{i}}(T_{n})=(g(T_{n})-\mathrm{i})^{-1}
and
h_{\mathrm{i}}(T)=(g(T)-\mathrm{i})^{-1}
, hence the previous statement is equivalent to convergence
g(T_{n})\rightarrow g(T)
in the strong resolvent sense.

Appendix C
Quadratic Forms and Self-adjoint Operators

In this final appendix, some definitions and results from the theory of positive quadratic forms will be outlined. Again,
\mathcal{H}\equiv(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle)
shall denote a complex Hilbert space, and
\|\cdot\|=\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle^{1/2}
the norm induced by the inner product.

C.1
Closed and Closable Quadratic Forms

Definition C.1.1
(Quadratic form).

(
[
83
, Def. A.9]
)
A
positive quadratic form
on
\mathcal{H}
is a mapping
\mathfrak{q}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty)
which satisfies for all
\xi,\eta\in\dom(\mathfrak{q})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
the following identities:

\mathfrak{q}(\lambda\xi)=|\lambda|^{2}\mathfrak{q}(\xi)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{q}(\xi+\eta)+\mathfrak{q}(\xi-\eta)=2\mathfrak{q}(\xi)+2\mathfrak{q}(\eta)\ .

The subspace
\dom(\mathfrak{q})\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is called the
domain
of
\mathfrak{q}
; if it lies dense in
\mathcal{H}
, then the quadratic form
\mathfrak{q}
is said to be
densely defined
.

Definition C.1.2
(Closed and closable form).

(
[
83
, Def. A.9]
,
[
144
, Def. 10.2]
)
A positive quadratic form
\mathfrak{q}
on
\mathcal{H}
is called
closed
iff
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})
,
\xi\in\dom(\mathfrak{q})
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\xi_{n}=\xi
and
\lim_{n,k\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n}-\xi_{k})=0
implies that
\xi\in\dom(\mathfrak{q})
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n}-\xi)=0
. The form
\mathfrak{q}
is said to be
closable
iff there exists a closed positive quadratic form
\mathfrak{t}
on
\mathcal{H}
which is an extension of
\mathfrak{q}
, that is,
\dom(\mathfrak{q})\subseteq\dom(\mathfrak{t})
and
\mathfrak{t}(\xi)=\mathfrak{q}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\dom(\mathfrak{q})
.

Definition C.1.3
(Lower semi-continuity).

(
[
144
, p. 223]
)
Let
(X,d)
be a metric space. An extended real-valued function
f:X\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is called
lower semi-continuous
iff for every sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
X
which converges to some element
x\in X
, there holds

f(x)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(x_{n})\ .

Proposition C.1.4
(Characterization of closable forms
[
83
, Thm. A.12]
,
[
144
, Prop. 10.3]
).

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a positive quadratic form on
\mathcal{H}
. The following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{q}
is closable.

(b)

\mathfrak{q}
is lower semi-continuous on
\dom(\mathfrak{q})
.

(c)

For every sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})
satisfying
\operatorname*{\text{$\mathcal{H}$}-lim}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\xi_{n}=0
and
\lim_{n,m\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n}-\xi_{m})=0
, there holds
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n})=0
.

C.1.5
Closure of a form.

(
[
83
, p. 224]
,
[
144
, p. 224]
)
Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a closable positive quadratic form. Define
\dom(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})\coloneqq\big\{{\xi\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,\exists(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})\ \text{s.t.}\ \|\xi_{n}-\xi\|\rightarrow 0\ \text{and}\ \mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n}-\xi_{m})\rightarrow 0}\big\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
and the mapping
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty)
by
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n})
for
\xi\in\dom(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})
. This is a well-defined positive quadratic form. Furthermore, one can show that
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}
is closed, and that it is the smallest closed extension of the form
\mathfrak{q}
. Therefore, one calls
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}
the
closure
of
\mathfrak{q}
.

C.2
The Form Representation Theorem

C.2.1
Order relation for self-adjoint operators.

(
[
83
, Lem. A.1]
,
[
144
, p. 230]
)
Let
T
and
S
be two positive self-adjoint linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
. Define the relation
S\leq T
to be satisfied iff

\dom\bigl(T^{1/2}\bigr)\subseteq\dom\bigl(S^{1/2}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\big\|{S^{1/2}\xi}\big\|\leq\big\|{T^{1/2}\xi}\big\|\quad\text{for all $\xi\in\dom\bigl(T^{1/2}\bigr)$}\ .

(C.1)

Theorem C.2.2
(Form representation theorem
[
83
, Thm. A.11]
,
[
144
, Thm. 10.7]
).

Let
\mathfrak{q}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty)
be a densely defined positive quadratic form. Then
\mathfrak{q}
is closed if and only if there exists a positive self-adjoint operator
Q_{\mathfrak{q}}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(Q_{\mathfrak{q}})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that

\dom\bigl(Q_{\mathfrak{q}}^{1/2}\bigr)=\dom(\mathfrak{q})\quad\text{and}\quad\forall\xi\in\dom\bigl(Q_{\mathfrak{q}}^{1/2}\bigr)\,:\,\mathfrak{q}(\xi)=\big\|{Q_{\mathfrak{q}}^{1/2}\xi}\big\|^{2}\ .

(C.2)

C.2.3
Operator associated with a positive form.

(
[
83
, Rem. A.13]
)
Let
\mathfrak{q}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow[0,+\infty)
be a densely defined positive quadratic form. If
\mathfrak{q}
is lower semi-continuous, then
Proposition

C.1.4
implies that the closure
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}
(
cf.

C.1.5
) is well-defined. Applying
Theorem

C.2.2
to this closed form, one obtains a positive self-adjoint operator
T\coloneqq Q_{\overline{\mathfrak{q}}}:\mathcal{H}\supseteq\dom(T)\relbar\joinrel\varrightarrow\mathcal{H}
satisfying (
C.2
). Since the closure
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}
is, in particular, an extension of
\mathfrak{q}
, it follows that

\dom\bigl(T^{1/2}\bigr)\supseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{q}(\xi)=\big\|{T^{1/2}\xi}\big\|{\ ,\quad}\xi\in\dom(\mathfrak{q})\ .

(C.3)

Moreover, it holds that
T
is the
largest
positive self-adjoint linear operator, with respect to the order relation (
C.1
), which satisfies the properties (
C.3
), and, furthermore,
\dom(\mathfrak{q})
is a core for the self-adjoint operator
T^{1/2}
(
Definition

B.2.1
). To see this, assume that
S
were another positive self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
satisfying the relations (
C.3
). For every
\xi\in\dom(T^{1/2})=\dom(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})
, there exists a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\dom(\mathfrak{q})
such that
\|\xi_{n}-\xi\|\rightarrow 0
and
\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n})\rightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
(
C.1.5
). Therefore, from the lower semi-continuity of
\mathfrak{q}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\big\|{S^{1/2}\xi}\big\|^{2}=\mathfrak{q}(\xi)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n})=\overline{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)=\big\|{T^{1/2}\xi}\big\|\ ,

hence
S\leq T
according to
C.2.1
. For the second statement, note that by definition of the closure
\overline{\mathfrak{q}}
(
C.1.5
) also
\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{n}-\xi_{m})\rightarrow 0
as
n,m\rightarrow+\infty
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\xi-\xi_{n}\|+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|T^{1/2}(\xi-\xi_{n})\|=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi-\xi_{n})=\lim_{n,m\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{q}(\xi_{m}-\xi_{n})=0\ .
